**Holding and operative rule:** Sections 1396a(a)(10) and (43)(A)–(C), read with §§1396d(a)(4)(B) and (r), give Medicaid-eligible children under twenty-one mandatory individual rights to the defined medical assistance, information, requested screening and arrangements for corrective treatment, enforceable through §1983. The right requires the applicable eligibility, service-category, request, screening-discovered need and coverage conditions stated below; it does not establish a personal right to optimal program administration, staffing, provider rates, overall capacity or a preferred provider.

**Authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; all nine directly adopt this holding and its statutory limits.

**Statutory content and conditions:**

- Section (43)(A) requires informing persons in the State under twenty-one who have been determined eligible for medical assistance including early and periodic screening, diagnostic and treatment services (EPSDT), about those services and the need for age-appropriate immunization against vaccine-preventable diseases.
- Section (43)(B) requires providing or arranging screening whenever requested. Section (43)(C) requires arranging, directly or by referral to appropriate agencies, organizations or individuals, corrective treatment whose need screening discloses. The request condition belongs to screening; the disclosed-need condition belongs to corrective arrangements. Screening of unwilling children is not compelled.
- Section 1396d(r)(1) requires screening at medically and dentally reasonable periodic intervals determined by the State after consultation with recognized child-health organizations, and at other medically necessary intervals. Its minimum comprises a comprehensive health and developmental history addressing physical and mental development, an unclothed physical examination, age-and-history-appropriate immunizations, laboratory tests including age-and-risk-appropriate lead assessment, and health education including anticipatory guidance. Pediatric immunization follows the separately referenced statutory vaccine schedule.
- Sections (r)(2)–(4) separately cover vision, dental and hearing services at professionally reasonable periodic and other medically necessary intervals. Their minima include diagnosis and treatment of vision defects, including eyeglasses; pain and infection relief, tooth restoration and maintenance of dental health; and diagnosis and treatment of hearing defects, including hearing aids.
- Section (r)(5) covers necessary health care, diagnosis, treatment and other measures of the kinds described in §1396d(a) to correct or ameliorate physical or mental defects, illnesses or conditions discovered through screening, whether or not the State plan otherwise covers the service. Actual statutory service-category, need and coverage conditions remain.
- A provider qualified to supply one or more EPSDT items may supply them without qualification for every item. Section (43)(D)'s reporting and participation measures are not converted into additional individual rights by this holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court applies Blessing's provision-specific inquiry. Congress identifies the eligible child and the mandatory benefit, and the service definitions supply standards courts can apply through medical evidence. Wright and Wilder show that clinical or administrative judgment does not make a defined entitlement judicially unmanageable. The officials' strongest objection treats the plan as an undertaking only to the federal administrator. But the particular child-directed benefits, together with §§1320a-2 and 1320a-10's rejection of state-plan placement as a categorical bar, defeat that objection. Suter retains its different reasonable-efforts and reporting holdings; it establishes no contrary rule for every plan provision. Gonzaga's FERPA-specific outcome does not erase Blessing or turn its four-Justice general formulation into controlling law.

Enforcement remains a separate inquiry. Blessing and Bruneau require actual congressional foreclosure, and §1396c's funding oversight supplies no sufficiently comprehensive incompatible individual remedy. Managed-care contracts do not eliminate the State's financing, informing and arrangements obligations. They establish neither an actual deprivation nor a guarantee that state employees personally render treatment. Aggregate deficiencies may prove an identified child's violation or support fitted relief; they cannot create a different personal entitlement.

### 2. Accepted federal duties may be enforced prospectively against responsible officials

**Holding and operative rule:** A valid Spending Clause enactment remains federal law when its accepted conditions govern a participating State. Officials actually connected to a continuing violation of these individual federal rights may be ordered to comply prospectively; relief must address the proved future violation and may not award retrospective State compensation or compel obedience to state law.

**Authority:** The same unanimous Opinion of the Court directly adopts this holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court applies Ex parte Young's connected-official route. Pennhurst's funding analogy explains clear notice and acceptance, while Bennett rejects reducing an enacted condition to an ordinary bilateral promise. Michigan's voluntary choice to participate does not make compliance voluntary while it participates. The State's real interest and implementation discretion therefore do not sustain categorical dismissal.

Verizon requires examination of the actual defendant, continuing federal violation and remedy. Coeur d'Alene rejects an additional generalized sovereign-interest barrier. Medicaid's general funding supervision supplies no exclusive remedial sequence displacing this action. Allegations of a continuing deprivation suffice against the categorical defense at this stage; coercive relief requires proof. Edelman permits the prospective costs of lawful compliance but bars retrospective treasury compensation, and Pennhurst separately bars state-law commands. The source-of-law conclusion, individual right, §1983 channel and immunity boundary remain distinct. Neither voluntary participation nor this officer action establishes consent to direct State damages, and the Court announces no separate private cause under the Supremacy Clause.

### 3. The live child-service claims return for adjudication under those rules

**Holding and operative rule:** The Sixth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it rejected the categorical defenses and allowed the reviewed child-entitlement action to proceed. On remand each live claimant must establish standing and eligibility, the applicable statutory version and obligation, concrete deprivation, responsible official, continuing breach and causation before a court awards relief fitted to that violation.

**Authority:** All nine join this application and remand in the same Court opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** Dismissal prevented findings on actual service failures and the proper scope of relief. Blessing requires matching the asserted right to the claimant, conduct, provision and remedy. An established statewide pattern may support relief connected to proved violations, but a statistical shortfall alone warrants neither a new personal right nor automatic redesign of the system. These are factual and remedial tasks under the rule just stated.

The December 1999 transportation-assistance preclusion ruling was not appealed and remains effective. The Michigan League for Human Services' unappealed dismissal, Michigan Welfare Rights Organization's affirmed dismissal and the professional associations' restored standing retain the posture fixed below. The distinct provider and professional-association claims lie outside the granted child-service questions; this decision adds no individual provider-rate entitlement under §1396a(a)(30)(A).

## Precedent Treatment

- Blessing v. Freestone: applies particular-right, foreclosure and fitted-remedy inquiries; the general program-adequacy exclusion remains.
- Bruneau v. South Kortright Central School District: applies the distinction between an existing right and actual remedial foreclosure; Medicaid is not treated as substantively identical to Title IX.
- Gonzaga University v. Doe: distinguishes the FERPA entitlement and preserves its outcome and the nonmajority status of its broader rights formulation.
- Suter v. Artist M.: preserves its actual reasonable-efforts, reporting and separate implied-action holdings and its independent case-plan question; no categorical state-plan bar is applied.
- Wright v. Roanoke Redevelopment and Housing Authority; Wilder v. Virginia Hospital Association: apply defined statutory entitlement and manageable qualitative application; regulations may elaborate an existing right but do not create the right held here.
- Pennhurst State School and Hospital v. Halderman; Bennett v. Kentucky Department of Education: preserve clear notice and acceptance while rejecting a contract-only account of enacted law; Pennhurst's separate state-law-relief bar remains.
- Ex parte Young; Edelman v. Jordan; Idaho v. Coeur d'Alene Tribe; Verizon Maryland Inc. v. Public Service Commission: apply the connected-official, ongoing-federal-violation and prospective-remedy rules, preserving retrospective and state-law limits.
- Popovich v. Cuyahoga County Court of Common Pleas: its distinct express-authorization and Fourteenth Amendment enforcement holdings do not alter this statutory right or prospective-officer route.

## Law After Decision

Eligible children may enforce the identified mandatory Medicaid child benefits under §1983, subject to their exact statutory conditions and proof of violation. Responsible officials remain amenable to prospective compliance relief. This provision-specific application adds the child-service holding while preserving the existing right, foreclosure and remedy framework; it creates no right to general administrative perfection.

## Separate Writings

**Scalia, joined by Kennedy, concurring.** They join the opinion under the governing statutory-right precedents. As in their Blessing concurrence, they retain the original-meaning reservation concerning whether beneficiaries of a funding undertaking possess rights of the kind §1983 was enacted to enforce. That reservation does not support treating a federal enactment as nonlaw or refusing the established inquiry into these defined benefits. They adopt no additional restriction on the Court's operative rule or relief.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for provision-specific adjudication of the live child-service claims and any properly supported prospective relief. The lower court must determine the actual unmet needs, causation, class scope and terms of any decree under the controlling rules.

## Source Notes

Westside Mothers v. Haveman, 289 F.3d 852; 42 U.S.C. §§1983, 1320a-2, 1320a-10, 1396a(a)(10), (43), 1396c, 1396d(a)(4)(B), (r). The action reaches review after dismissal; allegations of noncompliance are not findings of each child's unmet medical need.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Borden-Ranch-Partnership-v-Army-Corps-of-Engineers_merits_2002-12-16.md
Effective date: 2002-12-16


## Event

Borden Ranch Partnership v. Army Corps of Engineers, No. 01-1243. Merits decision, December 16, 2002. Argued December 10, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, which sustained unpermitted-discharge liability in drainages and swales, reversed the vernal-pool component and remanded for penalty recalculation. The granted questions concern deep-ripping discharges, normal farming and statutory recapture, and separate violations within one day. Jurisdiction over the remaining waters is conceded.

The approximately 8,400-acre grazing ranch was being converted toward vineyards and orchards. Ripping shanks reached roughly five to seven feet, and the trial findings describe active horizontal and vertical movement of earth, rock, sand and biological material, with filling or mounding of former channels. The trial attributed a discharge to each counted fully engaged pass: 348 passes in drainages, averaging twelve at each of twenty-nine locations, plus ten in the later-excluded pool. It imposed $1.5 million, alternatively $500,000 with restoration of four acres. The pool reversal requires a new calculation; no replacement amount has been set.

## Participation

Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. Kennedy took no part in the consideration or decision; no reason is stated.

## Public Action

The Ninth Circuit's surviving drainage and swale liability judgment and discharge-based counting method are affirmed. Its vernal-pool reversal and remand for penalty recalculation remain in force.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Affirmed as to the challenged surviving liability and application of the discharge-based counting rule, 6–2. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. **Opposing:** Scalia and Thomas, who would vacate that liability and its penalties and remand under a narrower addition requirement. All eight participating Justices agree with the separate legal proposition that distinct lawfully established discharges can be separate violations within one day.

The penalty must exclude the vernal-pool counts and reflect only remaining proved discharges under the applicable maximum and statutory factors. The Court fixes no replacement amount. Any restoration obligation rests on the operative decree and authorized remedial findings, not an automatic restoration direction from this statutory holding.

## Opinion Topology

O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. The complete Court-opinion coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Scalia and Thomas additionally join Part III's separate-discharge counting rule, but not its application sustaining these violations or the judgment. Scalia files an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Thomas.

| Writing or portion | Author | Direct support, including author | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Parts I–II: discharge and recapture | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Active conveyance and deposit; purpose-linked water-area conversion and statutory physical consequence |
| Part III: distinct actual discharges may be separate daily violations | O'Connor | All eight participating Justices | Statutory unit of violation, conditioned on lawful discharge findings |
| Part III: application and penalty remand | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Sustains drainage discharge findings and preserves pool exclusion and recalculation |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Scalia, Thomas | Joins the counting rule only; would vacate liability and penalties for a narrower addition inquiry |

## Holdings

### I. Actual mechanized conveyance and deposit satisfy the discharge elements

**Holding and operative rule:** Equipment that excavates, conveys and actively deposits dredged soil or other statutory pollutants into jurisdictional waters makes an addition from a point source even when the material was not imported from offsite. Mere machinery movement, in-place disturbance or hydrological alteration without a proved conveyance and deposit does not satisfy those elements.

**Authority:** Parts I–II and Part III's application in the opinion of O'Connor are joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, including the author; those six direct joins control the liability holdings and their application.

**Controlling explanation:** Sections 1311 and 1362 require an addition of a pollutant from a discernible, confined and discrete conveyance to covered waters. Dredged spoil, rock, sand and biological material are listed pollutants. Their lawful presence elsewhere on the property does not make them incapable of being deposited into a receiving portion of the waters through equipment. The point-source definition includes rolling stock, but a machine becomes relevant here because of its actual conveyance function, not merely its presence near water. Agricultural stormwater runoff remains excluded.

The findings establish more than broken clay or environmental injury: the engaged shanks actively relocated and deposited material, including filling or mounding channels. Those findings satisfy the statutory act. The contrary construction excludes active equipment-mediated deposition when excavation and deposition occur within the same wet area. The Court instead holds that the found excavation, conveyance and active deposit supply an addition despite that common origin; an offsite source is not required. The Court does not equate that operation with every instance of incidental fallback. National Mining Association's treatment of incidental fallback is distinguishable from the found active relocation and deposit.

PUD No. 1 requires the discharge trigger before broader activity controls attach. Sweet Home likewise requires the statutory act and its causal conditions rather than environmental purpose alone. Those principles prevent using hydrological damage to supply a missing addition. SWANCC preserves the need for enacted authority, but its isolated-pond migratory-bird holding does not resolve these conceded waters or the distinct addition question. The Government still must prove a discharge for each violation.

**Scope:** Jurisdiction over the surviving drainages and swales is conceded; the removed vernal pool stays outside the judgment. Neither the disputed later guidance nor the 2002 regulatory revision supplies a retroactive ground of liability.

### II. Recapture requires the purpose and effects specified for the water area

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1344(f)(2) requires a permit for a discharge incidental to an activity whose purpose is to bring a water area into a use to which it was not previously subject, where flow or circulation may be impaired or the waters' reach reduced, even if normal-farming protection otherwise applies. An actual discharge, the purpose of a new use of the water area, and the specified possible physical consequence are separate conditions; a crop change, ranch-wide agricultural label or accidental passage alone does not establish them.

**Authority:** Part II has the six direct supporters identified above.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 1344(f)(1)(A) protects normal farming activities, including plowing. Even assuming the operations would otherwise fall within that protection, the separate recapture text applies once a discharge and its stated conversion conditions have been established. The Court therefore need not determine an independent regulatory exclusion from the definition of plowing.

The pertinent unit is the water area being converted. The ranch's prior grazing history does not establish ongoing cultivation of every drainage and swale. The findings show active deposits connected to purposeful conversion of those areas toward drainage-dependent orchards and vineyards, with the relevant impairment of flow. They establish both the new-use purpose and the physical consequence. The Court therefore rejects the claim that farming followed by farming necessarily defeats recapture, without treating a change of crop as sufficient by itself.

An accidental pass alone would not prove the conversion purpose or its connection to the discharge. That qualification does not impose a separate intent-to-pollute requirement for each mechanical pass once a discharge incidental to the qualifying conversion activity is proved. The actual findings, rather than the dissent below's characterization of accidental passages, govern this application. The trial also rejected the asserted permission defense: directions to raise the shanks did not authorize fully engaged ripping.

PUD No. 1 and Sweet Home preserve the separation of statutory triggers from subsequent controls. The found deposit is necessary independently of recapture, and ecological harm alone would not supply it. Ordinary non-discharge cultivation, natural soil movement and farming that remains within an operative exemption fall outside this rule.

### III. Distinct proved discharges may be counted separately within a day

**Holding and operative rule:** Under §1319(d), each distinct lawfully established discharge may constitute a separate violation within the same day; a single project does not merge separate discharges into one daily violation. The applicable maximum here is $25,000 per day for each violation, and the assessment must consider seriousness, economic benefit, violation history, good-faith efforts, economic impact and justice; a pass lacking a proved discharge cannot be counted.

**Authority:** All eight participating Justices directly join Part III's statutory counting rule. Only Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer join its application sustaining the drainage count and the resulting judgment; Scalia and Thomas require a new lawful addition inquiry before any count can stand.

**Controlling explanation:** The statute uses both day and violation. Reading it to allow only one discharge violation per day would collapse separate unlawful discharges merely because the operator undertook them in one project. Conversely, counting movements without proving discharges would replace the statutory offense with a machine-use rule. Each unit therefore depends on a distinct proved discharge, subject to the lawful liability construction.

The trial attributed a discharge to each fully engaged pass it counted, rather than assuming that any tractor travel was unlawful. Under the Court's discharge construction, that supports the surviving drainage count. The ten pool counts were removed on appeal and cannot enter the recalculated amount. The statutory maximum is a ceiling, not a mandatory assessment; the listed factors govern the actual penalty. The lower court's existing recalculation remand remains the proper means of determining that amount, including any authorized restoration alternative. The Court does not apply the later increased maximum.

## Precedent Treatment

- PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology: applies the need for a discharge trigger before broader activity controls; its distinct certification authority is not an independent liability source here.
- Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon: applies the insistence on an actual statutory act and causal conditions; habitat or water damage alone cannot replace them.
- Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. Army Corps of Engineers: preserves its confined rejection of jurisdiction resting solely on migratory-bird use and its requirement of enacted authority; it does not decide the conceded surviving waters or the addition issue.
- United States v. Riverside Bayview Homes: its adjacent-wetland coverage remains intact; this case supplies no broader geographic definition.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T: preserves the requirement that agency action remain within the statutory grant; purpose or administration cannot manufacture an absent element.

## Law After Decision

Actual mechanized excavation, conveyance and deposit into covered waters can satisfy addition and point source without an offsite-source requirement. Hydrological injury alone cannot. Farming recapture requires a discharge tied to the purpose of a new use of the water area and the specified possible effect on flow, circulation or reach. Distinct proved discharges may be counted separately within a day, while amount and lawful restoration remain governed by the applicable ceiling, statutory factors and operative decree. The removed pool component remains excluded.

## Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in the separate-discharge counting rule and dissents from liability and its penalty application. He would require proof that material was introduced into the relevant waters, rather than merely moved within their existing substrate. Calling a tractor rolling stock and proving physical disruption do not answer that addition requirement. SWANCC and MCI require the statutory boundary to be established before agency purpose or deference can matter; recapture cannot supply an absent discharge. He would vacate the surviving liability and corresponding penalties and remand for the narrower inquiry. If lawful additions were established, the Government would still have to prove the water-area conversion purpose and the specified physical consequence, and each distinct proved discharge could be separately counted. Accidental passage or crop change alone supplies neither liability nor recapture. The pool reversal would remain untouched.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Ninth Circuit for the already ordered penalty recalculation, excluding the vernal-pool violations and applying the statutory factors to the remaining proved discharges. Surviving drainage and swale liability stands under the Court's construction. Any restoration alternative must conform to the operative decree and authorized findings.

## Source Notes

No. 01-1243; judgment below, 261 F.3d 810; 33 U.S.C. §§1311, 1319(d), 1344(f)(1)(A), 1344(f)(2), 1362(6), 1362(12) and 1362(14); the operative plowing regulation; National Mining Association v. Army Corps of Engineers, 145 F.3d 1399 (D.C. Cir. 1998). The trial findings concern actual relocation and deposits, not hydrological injury alone. Remaining geographic jurisdiction is conceded. The vernal-pool component was removed and the penalty remanded for recalculation; no replacement amount is established.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Cano-v-Davis_direct-appeal-screening_2003-01-10.md
Effective date: 2003-01-10


## Event

Cano v. Davis, No. 02-577. Direct-appeal screening, January 10, 2003. On appeal under 28 U.S.C. §1253 from the three-judge District Court for the Central District of California, which entered summary judgment for defendants on June 12, 2002, 211 F. Supp. 2d 1208. The appeal challenges California's 2001 congressional districting under the Fourteenth Amendment and the challenged congressional and state-senate districts under §2 of the Voting Rights Act. The preserved constitutional question concerns the measure of injury from asserted intentional electoral impairment; the statutory claims concern equal electoral opportunity.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated.

## Public Action

Probable jurisdiction is noted. The appeal is retained for plenary briefing and argument, including the preserved constitutional effects question and the independent statutory grounds. The screening tally and individual positions are not publicly disclosed.

## Judgment & Remedy

This order makes no merits disposition of the judgment below. The appeal remains pending.

## Opinion Topology

Unsigned procedural order; no separate statement.

## Holdings

The order announces no substantive holding. Plenary consideration does not expand the pleaded claims or decide whether an asserted injury is established.

## Precedent Treatment

No precedent is modified by this order.

## Law After Decision

The governing constitutional and statutory rules remain unchanged while the appeal proceeds.

## Separate Writings

No separate writing.

## Procedure After Action

The appeal proceeds to plenary briefing and argument. No argument or decision date is set by this order.

## Source Notes

Cano v. Davis, 211 F. Supp. 2d 1208 (C.D. Cal. 2002); 28 U.S.C. §1253; 42 U.S.C. §1973. An influence-district claim was expressly not asserted, and no standalone First Amendment count is presented.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Montiel-v-Davis_merits_2003-01-13.md
Effective date: 2003-01-13


## Event

Montiel v. Davis. Direct-appeal merits decision, January 13, 2003. On appeal under 28 U.S.C. §1253 from the three-judge District Court for the Southern District of Alabama, No. 01-0447-BH-S, which granted summary judgment for defendants on July 8, 2002, 215 F. Supp. 2d 1279. The questions concern population equality and racial electoral equality in Alabama's 2001 Senate and House plans. The congressional and education-board claims were severed and are outside this appeal.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the reviewed legislative judgment.

## Judgment & Remedy

Affirmed, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. The denial of relief on the population, constitutional racial, and statutory legislative claims stands within the plaintiffs' own electoral exposure. The requested invalidation and replacement of the legislative plans are denied. The severed congressional and education-board proceedings are unaffected.

## Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivered the opinion of the Court. The complete-opinion coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer join the complete opinion. O'Connor and Kennedy additionally join Parts I and III. Scalia and Thomas additionally join Part I. Thomas filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment, joined by Scalia.

| Writing or division | Author | Justices adopting the reasoning | Scope and relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Stone-Zsela | All nine Justices | Population equality; correct purpose rule; independently sufficient constitutional evidentiary disposition. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Stone-Zsela | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Additional constitutional territorial-design ground. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III | Stone-Zsela | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Independently sufficient statutory evidentiary ground. |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Thomas | Scalia | Joins Part I; affirms the statutory judgment on noncoverage, without joining Parts II or III. |

## Holdings

### Population equality and prohibited purpose

**Holding and operative rule:** A state legislative maximum population deviation below ten percent does not alone establish a prima facie violation, but remains challengeable through proof of arbitrary or discriminatory weighting. Where the governing claim turns on discriminatory purpose, that purpose need only be a substantial or motivating factor; once that predicate is proved, the government must prove by a preponderance that materially the same decision would have occurred without it.

**Authority:** Part I, adopted by all nine Justices, directly controls.

**Controlling explanation:** Voinovich applies substantial equality without making smaller deviations immune; Reynolds and Brown reject a demand for mathematical exactness. Maximum deviation adds the greatest departure above and below the ideal district population. The Senate's 9.78% and House's 9.93% figures describe that plan-wide range, not a separate allowance for each voter. Below the threshold, plaintiffs must connect their evidence to unlawful weighting. County splits, demographics, and more equal alternatives can be relevant, but do not prove that connection merely by existing. The reported submissions add no material evidence establishing the claimed arbitrary or discriminatory operation. The lower court's suggestion that an improper purpose must be exclusive is incorrect under Arlington Heights, Brooks, and Page. That error does not require remand because the independent evidentiary deficiency remains under the correct rule, and no excluded material proof is identified. Political explanations receive no categorical immunity; they are assessed against the evidence. The challenged judgment stands on the absence of the necessary showing.

### Territorial design and independent constitutional injury

**Holding and operative rule:** Racial consideration or predominance in drawing a shared territorial district does not alone establish an equal-protection deprivation. An independently asserted exclusion, intentional impairment of electoral opportunity, material restriction on participation, or inferior civic status must satisfy its own elements; voters of every race receive the same protection.

**Authority:** Part II, adopted by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies an independently sufficient majority ground for the constitutional racial judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** Shaw, Johnson v. Miller, Easley, and Robertson distinguish the composition of a common electorate from government action that impairs a protected civic interest. A design-only claim identifies motive, shape, racial composition, or district assignment without establishing that independent operation. Intentional dilution requires discriminatory purpose, actual material impairment of electoral opportunity, and causation. Unequal participation requires a real impediment to political activity, not merely placement in a less favorable district. Such an injury need not await a second governmental act or an electoral defeat. Here the population figures, boundaries, and suppositions establish neither the asserted unlawful weighting nor another concrete impairment. Race's correlation with party affiliation can be evidence; a partisan label neither proves a racial wrong nor excuses one proved. The asserted white-voter claim receives the same legal protection as any other racial claim, but does not acquire a different injury rule. This constitutional ground creates no additional prerequisite for an independent Voting Rights Act claim.

### Independent statutory proof

**Holding and operative rule:** A §2 district-dilution claim requires the relevant cohesion and opposing-bloc causal showing and unequal electoral opportunity under the statute; racial population figures and the availability of another map do not establish those requirements by themselves. The statutory claim here fails on its independent evidentiary deficiency, regardless of the disposition of the constitutional design theory.

**Authority:** Part III, adopted by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, controls by seven direct votes. Scalia and Thomas support the statutory judgment on another ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Gingles identifies the causal voting conditions, Voinovich places the initial burden on challengers, and De Grandy requires an independent examination of actual opportunity without maximizing districts. Section 2 protects citizens of every race and uses a results inquiry distinct from constitutional purpose. The population and boundary evidence does not establish the requisite cohesion, opposing-bloc mechanism, and unequal opportunity. Current law permits one protected group to establish capacity in a lawful, geographically compact district either through a numerical majority or rigorous proof of dependable crossover support sufficient to elect its preferred candidates. Those routes remain available, but no renewed capacity ruling answers this separate deficiency. Balderas preserves a genuinely independent sufficient lawful ground rather than ordering remand for an immaterial error in another part of the analysis. Preclearance does not adjudicate these independent statutory or constitutional claims. The Court rejects the requested statutory relief on the record presented.

## Precedent Treatment

- Voinovich v. Quilter: applied to population burdens and independent statutory proof; deviations below ten percent remain challengeable.
- Reynolds v. Sims: applied to substantial legislative population equality; mathematical exactness is not required.
- Brown v. Thomson: applied to the rebuttable significance of smaller deviations; those deviations receive no categorical immunity.
- Arlington Heights: applied to prohibited purpose as a substantial or motivating factor; purpose need not be exclusive.
- Mt. Healthy: preserves the government's burden to prove materially the same decision without the prohibited purpose once the necessary predicate is established.
- Brooks v. Barnes: applies that claim-specific motivating-factor and same-enactment sequence to electoral claims; the sequence remains intact.
- Page v. Bartels: applied to the correct purpose rule and independent failure of constitutional proof; electoral success is not a universal answer to participation injury.
- Shaw v. Reno: its territorial-design boundary remains controlling; independent constitutional and statutory claims retain their own elements.
- Johnson v. Miller: applies that boundary even to predominant racial design; government-imposed participation or status injury need not await another act or loss.
- Easley v. Cromartie: preserves the distinction between design alone and a properly supported independent injury.
- Robertson v. Bartels: applies the same protection to voters of every race; an incumbent-prospect label does not itself establish a distinct civic deprivation.
- Thornburg v. Gingles: applied to the independently necessary causal voting conditions; population arithmetic does not replace proof.
- Johnson v. De Grandy: preserves independent totality without maximization or a proportionality safe harbor.
- Valdespino: its numerical-majority and dependable-crossover capacity routes remain intact; no new capacity determination is needed here.
- Balderas v. Texas: preserves a genuinely independent sufficient lawful ground; no remand follows from an immaterial error elsewhere in the analysis.

## Law After Decision

Smaller legislative deviations remain open to supported constitutional challenge. A prohibited purpose need not be exclusive, and independent constitutional injuries retain their own elements. The statutory opportunity inquiry remains separate. This record-specific affirmance creates no numerical safe harbor, partisan immunity, racial allocation, or different protection for white voters.

## Separate Writings

Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurs in Part I and the judgment. Consistently with the statutory construction he maintained in Holder, De Grandy, Page, and Balderas, he concludes that §2 does not authorize the asserted judicial allocation of districts. That antecedent construction supplies his reason to affirm the statutory judgment; it is not a join in the Court's Gingles and totality analysis. He also withholds assent from Part II's territorial-design rule. His agreement with the independently sufficient constitutional evidentiary disposition does not adopt that broader rule.

## Procedure After Action

The legislative summary judgment stands and this appeal is concluded. The severed proceedings retain their separate posture.

## Source Notes

Montiel v. Davis, 215 F. Supp. 2d 1279 (S.D. Ala. 2002), and Black, J., concurring; 28 U.S.C. §1253; Fourteenth Amendment; 42 U.S.C. §1973; Reynolds v. Sims, 377 U.S. 533; Brown v. Thomson, 462 U.S. 835; Thornburg v. Gingles, 478 U.S. 30.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Consolidated-Edison-Co-of-New-York-Inc-v-Pataki_merits_2003-01-14.md
Effective date: 2003-01-14


## Event

Consolidated Edison Co. of New York, Inc. v. Pataki, 292 F.3d 338. Merits decision, January 14, 2003. On review of the Second Circuit's affirmance of a permanent injunction against New York Chapter 190 and its limited remand to correct a clerical error. The questions are whether a corporation is protected against state bills of attainder and whether Chapter 190's targeted replacement-power cost disallowance and refund command imposes punishment for past wrongdoing without judicial trial. The underlying outage began February 15, 2000; the statute became law August 8, 2000; the appellate judgment issued June 5, 2002.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

## Public Action

Affirmed, 9–0; the permanent injunction against Chapter 190 remains effective, with the limited remand for correction of the clerical error preserved.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

The permanent injunction bars enforcement of Chapter 190's cost-disallowance and refund commands in full. The limited remand to correct the clerical error in the judgment remains in place. Independent lawful PSC prudence review remains available; the judgment establishes neither that every replacement-power cost was prudent nor an entitlement to reimbursement. The approximately $250 million at stake is not a damages award.

## Opinion Topology

Souter delivered the opinion of the Court, in which Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer joined. The opinion and both holdings are unanimous.

## Holdings

### Corporate protection against legislative punishment

**Holding and operative rule:** A corporation may invoke Article I, §10 when a state enactment identifies it and imposes punishment for past wrongdoing without judicial trial. This protection concerns the corporation's legal and property interests under this guarantee; it does not confer every right belonging to a natural person.

**Authority:** The unanimous opinion of the Court, authored by Souter and joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls both holdings and the stated remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the constitutional restraint turns on the legislative act and the nature of its punishment. A State cannot avoid the prohibition merely because the property subjected to punishment is held corporately. United States v. Lovett supplies the prohibition against noncapital legislative penalties and the separation of punishment from legislative adjudication. Nixon v. Administrator of General Services requires punishment beyond mere identification. Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock applies that distinction to identifiable businesses but leaves this corporate-coverage issue for decision. Historical punishments often affected bodily liberty or the occupations of natural persons. That history informs what counts as punishment; it does not create an exception permitting the same prohibited method against an entity's property. The guarantee therefore reaches this asserted punitive financial disability without resolving unrelated corporate claims to personal rights.

### Chapter 190 imposes legislative punishment

**Holding and operative rule:** A challenger must establish identification, punishment and the absence of judicial trial; historical treatment, functional operation and legislative purpose inform one punishment inquiry rather than three cumulative requirements. Chapter 190's legislative determination of Con Ed's completed wrongdoing, combined with its conclusive denial and refund of all replacement-power costs for the identified outage, imposes punishment without trial and cannot be enforced.

**Authority:** The same unanimous Court opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that this law's combined operation supplies the constitutional defect. It names Con Ed, declares the company's completed operation unreasonable and makes that determination conclusively bar all replacement-power recovery for the outage, including repayment of amounts already collected. The Legislature itself fixes culpability and the complete financial consequence; the consequence does not depend on adjudicating which costs were imprudent.

Protecting ratepayers and allocating utility risk are legitimate regulatory objectives. Nixon and Ambassador Books establish that naming one operator, imposing a substantial burden, acting retrospectively or expressing hostility does not alone establish punishment. The Court accepts those limits. But describing who benefits from this command does not answer its operative character: a legislative judgment of past fault supplies the entire event-specific sanction without trial. That combination distinguishes this measure from genuine prospective rate regulation or an independently adjudicated cost disallowance. Neither an historically identical penalty nor a criminal conviction is necessary.

Selective Service System v. Minnesota Public Interest Research Group preserves the challenger's burden; the Court adds no motive presumption or least-restrictive-means test. The injunction fits the entire proved cost-disallowance and refund command. Ordinary lawful prudence review remains possible, and actual prudent costs and recoverable amounts remain matters for that process. The attainder holding supplies complete relief, so equal protection and takings do not furnish additional grounds for this judgment.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock:** Applies its separation of identification from punishment and its historical, functional and purpose inquiry; distinguishes genuine prospective business regulation from this completed-conduct penalty. Its protection for genuine regulation remains effective, while the present decision expressly establishes corporate coverage of this guarantee.
- **Nixon v. Administrator of General Services, 433 U.S. 425:** Applies the punishment inquiry and preserves the principle that identification alone is insufficient.
- **United States v. Lovett, 328 U.S. 303:** Applies the prohibition of legislative punishment without trial to a punitive financial disability rather than limiting the Clause to a criminal conviction or capital penalty.
- **Selective Service System v. Minnesota Public Interest Research Group, 468 U.S. 841:** Preserves the challenger's burden to establish punishment; no burden shift or required least burdensome alternative is added.

## Law After Decision

Corporations may invoke the state Bill of Attainder Clause against punitive disabilities affecting their legal and property interests. The decision expressly resolves that coverage question and applies the existing punishment inquiry to Chapter 190. Specific regulation, ordinary prospective rates, neutral cost-allocation rules and independent prudence proceedings remain lawful when nonpunitive. The whole adjudged command is unenforceable; the decision creates no general remedial presumption, corporate entitlement to recover costs, or constitutional requirement to preserve an existing regulatory procedure.

## Separate Writings



## Procedure After Action

The injunction remains effective. Upon the mandate, enforcing officials must omit Chapter 190's disallowance and refund command from implementation; regulatory orders and calculations may rest on independent lawful prudence findings. Reimposition of the same statutory command remains subject to enforcement of the injunction. A genuinely independent prudence determination proceeds through the ordinary regulatory review process. The lower court corrects the clerical error as already directed.

## Source Notes

U.S. Const., Art. I, §10; 2000 N.Y. Laws ch. 190; Consolidated Edison Co. of New York, Inc. v. Pataki, 292 F.3d 338. The approximately $250 million figure describes the costs at stake rather than an adjudicated award.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Craigmiles-v-Giles_merits_2003-01-14.md
Effective date: 2003-01-14


## Event

Craigmiles v. Giles, 312 F.3d 220. Merits decision, January 14, 2003. On review of the Sixth Circuit's December 6, 2002 affirmance of an injunction against applying Tennessee's funeral-director qualification to the plaintiffs' existing retail businesses. The questions are whether requiring that qualification for merchandise-only retailers violates due process or equal protection, and whether protecting incumbents from competition alone supplies a legitimate governmental interest. The separate Privileges or Immunities theory is outside the grant.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

## Public Action

Reversed and remanded, 7–2, for dissolution of the injunction against the funeral-director qualification for the plaintiffs' existing merchandise-only retail businesses.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

**Opposing:** Stone-Zsela and Stevens.

The lower court must dissolve the injunction barring application of the funeral-director qualification to the plaintiffs' existing merchandise-only retail businesses. The challenged qualification survives the granted due-process and equal-protection challenges. Review remains confined to that existing-business injunction; the separate Privileges or Immunities claim is outside the grant.

## Opinion Topology

Thomas delivered the opinion of the Court, in which O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer joined. Stevens and Stone-Zsela filed separate dissenting opinions. No Justice joined either dissent.

## Holdings

### The actual merchandising and accountability connection satisfies ordinary review

**Holding and operative rule:** An ordinary economic qualification that implicates neither a fundamental right nor a suspect class satisfies due process and equal protection when it has a reasonably conceivable rational connection to a legitimate public objective; the challenger must negate that connection without demanding empirical certainty, formal findings or the least burdensome system. Tennessee's funeral-director qualification may be applied to these merchandise-only retailers because its actual merchandise instruction and examination, together with professional discipline, rationally connect the qualification to competence and accountability in this specialized sales market.

**Authority:** The opinion of the Court, authored by Thomas and joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, is supported in full by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Seven Justices adopt this rule, its application to both granted constitutional grounds and dissolution of the injunction.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the trial findings do not negate every reasonably conceivable legitimate connection. Beach Communications permits supported economic generalizations without proof that the legislature chose the best system. Lee Optical allows imperfect regulatory lines, and Nordlinger permits substantial disparities when an actual legitimate purpose rationally explains them. Heller and Romer retain scrutiny of the relationship to the disadvantage imposed; they do not require a separate, least burdensome scheme whenever one is imaginable.

These stores do not handle bodies, arrange funerals or burials, embalm or make advance-need sales. Much of the required preparation concerns services they never perform. But the curriculum includes merchandise instruction, and 37 of 250 examination questions concern funeral merchandising. A common professional qualification and disciplinary system can rationally serve informed selection, ethical sales and accountability when goods are sold in the funeral market. This is a permissible legislative connection, not a new finding that the qualification measurably improves casket quality.

The dissent's strongest objection concerns the entire entry barrier, not the mere existence of useful instruction. The Court nevertheless rejects its application because ordinary review does not impose proportionality between the curriculum and each specialty's daily work. The findings about casket quality, unrestricted burial choices and the substantial unrelated training defeat some proposed safety explanations; they do not eliminate the actual merchandise and accountability connection. Existing fraud remedies and a possible retail-specific license do not make the common qualification irrational.

Moreno and Cleburne continue to prohibit illegitimate ends and irrational distinctions. The Court does not decide whether incumbent protection standing alone is a legitimate end: the second question's premise that no independent rational public connection exists is not established in this record. The distinct Privileges or Immunities theory lies outside the grant.

## Precedent Treatment

- **FCC v. Beach Communications, Inc., 508 U.S. 307:** Applies the reasonably conceivable legitimate-basis rule, challenger's burden and absence of a formal-findings requirement; it supplies no immunity for an irrational or impermissibly motivated line.
- **Williamson v. Lee Optical of Oklahoma, Inc., 348 U.S. 483:** Applies legislative latitude in economic regulation without requiring an optimally tailored occupation or curriculum.
- **Nordlinger v. Hahn, 505 U.S. 1:** Applies its tolerance of substantial disparities supported by legitimate policy differences; protection of an existing business's profits alone is not decided.
- **Heller v. Doe, 509 U.S. 312:** Preserves the required relationship to the actual disadvantage and its separate holding that a less extensive alternative does not alone invalidate a rational classification.
- **Romer v. Evans, 517 U.S. 620:** Preserves real rational-connection review without empirical certainty or least-restrictive-means requirements; distinguishes an unexplained disability from this actual merchandising and accountability connection.
- **Department of Agriculture v. Moreno, 413 U.S. 528, and City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, 473 U.S. 432:** Preserve the limits against bare harm and irrational prejudice; the Court adds no categorical rule that incumbent protection by itself is legitimate.

## Law After Decision

The existing rational-basis standard governs the reviewed licensing requirement. A unified occupational qualification may cover a merchandise-only specialty when actual merchandise instruction, examination and common professional accountability supply a rational connection to legitimate consumer interests, despite substantial training concerning other professional services. The decision imposes no curriculum-proportionality test and adopts no fundamental occupational right. It leaves unresolved the legitimacy of naked incumbent protection as the sole end because the actual record supplies a distinct rational public connection; it does not approve an arbitrary credential merely because incumbents benefit.

## Separate Writings

**Stevens, dissenting.** Stevens would affirm the existing-business injunction under ordinary rational-basis review. His approach in Beach and Nordlinger asks whether an impartial legislature could pursue the asserted purpose and whether that purpose rationally explains the actual inequality. He accepts that the curriculum teaches some merchandising; he rejects the conclusion that this limited content explains exclusion from the entire retail activity until the seller completes the substantially unrelated professional qualification. The trial findings concerning the stores' limited work, casket quality and the entry burden supply the basis for that conclusion. A private benefit cannot justify itself merely because the statute creates it. His objection does not demand the least restrictive fraud law or an optimally efficient course: it concerns the relationship of the whole prerequisite to the actual trade. Bare preservation of incumbent rents supplies no independent legitimate justification in his view.

**Stone-Zsela, dissenting.** The Chief Justice would affirm on both due-process and equal-protection grounds. Beach and Lee Optical permit imperfect economic lines, but Moreno and Cleburne preserve a legitimate-public-end requirement. He would extend that limit to bare protection of incumbent sellers from harmless competition. Incidental incumbent benefits, consumer protection, redistribution, aid to a distressed industry and Nordlinger's protection of established commitments remain distinguishable legitimate objectives.

He accepts the merchandising instruction, the 37 examination questions and the possible value of professional discipline. On this combined record, however, the stores perform none of the defining services that principally occupy the education and apprenticeship; the burial choices and quality findings also undermine the proposed casket-safety explanation. Discipline's usefulness does not itself connect those unrelated entry prerequisites to the admitted retail activity. Challengers retain the burden of negating reasonably conceivable legitimate explanations; no empirical proof, least restrictive system or specially optimized curriculum is constitutionally required.

He would retain relief against this separable qualification and presume equivalent protection for materially equivalent merchandise-only activity unless the State establishes that a narrower decree fully cures the proved violation. The trial findings and remedial jurisdiction must define the covered activity. Bodily care, funeral arrangements, advance-need transactions and lawful sales regulation remain outside that invalidation. The State could not obtain narrower relief by merely promising discretionary nonenforcement against the named stores. His rule would govern still-open direct-review cases under Harper, subject to Reynoldsville's independent procedural, immunity and remedial limits; final judgments remain closed and collateral relief requires its own authorization.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for dissolution of the existing-business injunction. Officials may enforce the reviewed qualification against the covered retail activity consistently with the judgment. The disposition concerns these granted due-process and equal-protection challenges and the existing injunction; the rejected Privileges or Immunities theory remains outside this review.

## Source Notes

U.S. Const., Amdt. 14; Tenn. Code §§62-5-101 and 62-5-201 and the related training and disciplinary provisions; Craigmiles v. Giles, 312 F.3d 220. The trial findings concern merchandise-only businesses with limited, rather than nonexistent, relevant training in the required curriculum.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Gibson-v-County-of-Washoe_merits_2003-01-14.md
Effective date: 2003-01-14


## Event

Gibson v. County of Washoe, 290 F.3d 1175. Merits decision, January 14, 2003. On review of the Ninth Circuit's May 22, 2002 judgment reversing summary judgment for the County on supported mental-health policy claims and affirming judgment for the named deputies and supervising sergeant. The question is whether a County policy may cause an actionable denial of a detainee's serious medical needs despite the named officers' lack of the subjective awareness required for personal liability.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

## Public Action

Affirmed, 9–0; the supported County screening/evaluation and medication-policy claims return for ordinary further proceedings, with the remaining dispositions preserved.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

The supported County claims concerning screening/evaluation and medication handling return for ordinary further proceedings, including trial if the remaining procedural requirements are satisfied. Summary judgment for the named deputies and sergeant, rejection of the unsupported communications-policy theory, and the unappealed consortium and state-law dispositions remain intact.

## Opinion Topology

Stevens delivered the opinion of the Court, in which Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer joined. The opinion and judgment are unanimous.

## Holdings

### The municipality's own policy may cause a constitutional deprivation

**Holding and operative rule:** A municipality may be liable under §1983 for its own deliberately indifferent custodial policy that actually causes a denial of constitutionally required medical care, even when the named employees lack the personal awareness required for their liability. The claimant must prove an actual constitutional deprivation, an attributable municipal policy or legally effective custom, a deliberate municipal choice concerning a known or obvious constitutional need, and an identified defect closely related to and actually causing the injury; negligence, employment alone and pooled employee knowledge do not suffice.

**Authority:** The unanimous opinion of the Court, authored by Stevens and joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls the rule, the summary-judgment application and the limited return for further proceedings.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a named employee's exoneration does not collapse the separate inquiries into the deprivation and its municipal source. Monell requires the municipality's own causal policy or custom. Collins separates the underlying constitutional wrong from attribution; the absence of a custody-based duty in its voluntary-employment setting does not govern a pretrial detainee's custody. Canton requires deliberate municipal choice and a close causal connection. Carter permits notice through a pattern or a sufficiently obvious need to perform a clear constitutional duty in known recurring circumstances; identical prior injuries are not invariably necessary.

The County correctly insists on an actual deprivation and more than negligent administration. But the named deputies' lack of relevant awareness establishes neither that all required care was furnished nor that a County policy could not disable that care. Farmer's individual-awareness rule does not permit combining employees' innocent information to manufacture personal culpability. The municipal claim instead must identify and prove the County's own deliberately indifferent policy and its injury-producing operation. This preserves the underlying constitutional-injury requirement rather than substituting institutional responsibility for it.

### The supported screening and medication evidence permits further proceedings

**Holding and operative rule:** At summary judgment, a claimant must identify evidence permitting a reasonable finding on every required municipal-liability element. The evidence concerning delayed mental-health evaluation and handling of psychotropic medication meets that standard here; the surviving claims return under the ordinary preponderance-of-the-evidence burden, while the unsupported communications theory and the preserved personal and unappealed dispositions remain excluded.

**Authority:** The same unanimous Court opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the evidence, viewed under Rule 56, supports a distinct policy inquiry. The jail regularly held mentally ill detainees, policymakers knew of that recurring population, and the evidence concerns practices postponing evaluation until an acutely disturbed person cooperated and treating relevant medication as property rather than a signal for assessment. General knowledge of mental illness alone would not suffice. These particular practices, combined with the recurring constitutional responsibility and medication evidence, permit a finding beyond hindsight that more training might have helped.

The County remains entitled to contest policy authority or effective custom, deliberate choice, deprivation and actual cause. Death during restraint does not itself establish a medical-care violation or that a different policy would have prevented the injury. The reasonable-force and personal-awareness rulings therefore survive, but they do not decide the independent medical-policy claim. The communications theory lacks the necessary policymaker-notice support and cannot survive merely by association with the supported policies. On remand, the family must prove the surviving elements and any compensable injury; ordinary factual and remedial adjudication under those rules does not make the municipal standard unresolved.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Monell v. Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658:** Applies the municipality's own policy-or-custom and causation requirements; employer-based liability remains forbidden.
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, 503 U.S. 115:** Applies the separate inquiries into constitutional deprivation and municipal attribution; distinguishes voluntary employment from actual pretrial custody.
- **City of Canton v. Harris, 489 U.S. 378:** Applies demanding municipal fault, notice, identified deficiency and causal connection; no negligence or hindsight standard replaces them.
- **Carter v. City of Philadelphia, March 13, 2000:** Applies its pattern-or-obvious-recurring-need notice rule and actual-cause requirement without adding an invariably identical-prior-incident condition.
- **Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825:** Preserves individualized actual awareness and the protection for a reasonable response; neither collective awareness nor a failed reasonable response supplies personal culpability. The distinct municipal inquiry does not alter that rule.

## Law After Decision

Existing municipal and custodial law governs. A municipality cannot obtain judgment automatically from the named employees' lack of personal awareness when supported evidence independently connects an actual medical-care deprivation to its own deliberately indifferent policy. The same demanding deprivation, attribution, fault and causation requirements control analogous custodial policy claims. The decision supplies neither institutional strict liability nor a new aggregate-awareness test. Section 3626's requirements for prospective prison-conditions relief remain unchanged.

## Separate Writings



## Procedure After Action

The case returns for ordinary proceedings on the supported County screening/evaluation and medication-policy claims. The trial issues must identify those surviving policies. The family bears the ordinary preponderance burden on the required liability elements and must establish compensable loss under the applicable damages rules; the County may contest each. The affirmed personal judgments and the excluded communications, consortium and state-law dispositions remain effective.

## Source Notes

42 U.S.C. §1983; U.S. Const., Amdt. 14; Fed. Rule Civ. Proc. 56; Gibson v. County of Washoe, 290 F.3d 1175. The policy and causal issues return on supported summary-judgment evidence, not findings of municipal liability.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Johnson-v-City-of-Cincinnati_merits_2003-01-14.md
Effective date: 2003-01-14


## Event

Johnson v. City of Cincinnati, 310 F.3d 484. Merits decision, January 14, 2003. On review of the Sixth Circuit's September 26, 2002 judgment concerning Cincinnati's drug-exclusion ordinance, prospective relief and an entered $38,500 attorney-fee award. The granted questions concern ordinary local passage and the claimed intimate relationships involved in Johnson's parent-authorized care of her grandchildren and Au France's access to his lawyer. The separate district-court double-jeopardy ground for Au France is outside the grant.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

## Public Action

The existing $38,500 fee judgment is affirmed, 7–2; the prospective components and the requested damages are remanded for their distinct specified treatment, 9–0.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Operative effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Existing $38,500 attorney-fee judgment | Affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The existing award survives the challenge to its constitutional basis. |
| Prospective declaration and injunction | Remanded for component-specific jurisdictional and equitable review, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Assess Burnett, cessation and actual exposure; apply equitable vacatur if moot and fitted relief if a live controversy remains. |
| Requested damages | Remanded to identify procedural status and adjudicate any surviving claim, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Apply ordinary preservation, liability, causation, compensation and defense rules to any surviving claim. |

The existing prospective orders remain subject to lawful lower-court treatment; this disposition itself neither automatically vacates them nor directs enforcement to resume. The district court's independent double-jeopardy ground for Au France lies outside the grant and remains unadjudicated in this review.

## Opinion Topology

Breyer delivered the opinion of the Court, in which Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg joined in full. Stone-Zsela joined Parts I, II, III and V; Scalia and Thomas joined Parts I and IV and the prospective-relief and damages-status portions of Part V. Stone-Zsela filed an opinion concurring in part and in the judgment. Scalia filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, in which Thomas joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg in full; Stone-Zsela, Scalia and Thomas as specified | Governs every component through its stated majority | I: collateral review, nine; II: movement, seven; III: caregiving, seven; IV: lawyer relationship, eight; V: fee affirmance, seven, and prospective/damages remand, nine |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Supports fee affirmance and both remand components | Joins I, II, III and V; declines the attorney-intimacy determination and adds a conditional remedial position |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Would also vacate the appellate fee affirmance and remand entitlement and allocation | Joins I, IV and V's prospective/damages instructions; rejects II, III and V's fee affirmance |

## Holdings

### I. The entered fee judgment presents a live collateral controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** An appeal from an existing attorney-fee judgment remains a live monetary controversy requiring decision of the granted federal-right predicates necessary to review that award, even when prospective relief requires separate jurisdictional examination. That collateral jurisdiction does not establish a continuing basis for an injunction or decide the status of requested damages.

**Authority:** Part I of the opinion of the Court, authored by Breyer, is joined by all eight other Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Cincinnati's appeal concerns an entered $38,500 award, not merely an interest in seeking fees after an underlying controversy has ended. The City contests the constitutional basis of that monetary judgment, and the Court must resolve the granted questions necessary to dispose of it. Laidlaw's statement that fees alone do not preserve an underlying controversy does not erase an existing collateral appeal. Arizonans permits correction of jurisdictional error and equitable vacatur when the relevant claim has become moot; its employment-departure and no-surviving-claim premises do not establish that this fee judgment or a damages claim has disappeared. Burnett and cessation must be addressed for prospective relief, but their examination supplies no substitute for adjudicating the monetary dispute. The remedies therefore require separate treatment.

### II. Substantial neighborhood exclusion burdens ordinary public passage

**Holding and operative rule:** Ordinary passage along public streets, sidewalks and public ways for lawful purposes is fundamental liberty. When a claimant establishes a personal exclusion substantially barring that passage throughout neighborhoods for months, the government must show a compelling justification and a close, individualized fit between the restriction's necessity and scope and the danger addressed; a past arrest or conviction alone does not discharge that burden.

**Authority:** Part II of the Court's opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices adopt the rule and its application to both exclusion mechanisms.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that both the arrest-based and conviction-based mechanisms fail this standard. Drug prevention is compelling, but excluding a person from every public route in all designated zones for every lawful purpose is a direct restraint on ordinary movement. Kent v. Dulles informs the importance of movement to liberty; Papachristou protects innocent public activity from arbitrary displacement. Neither already decided this precise rule. Saenz concerns equal state citizenship after interstate arrival, while Morales controls enforcement guidance. Objective predicates satisfy neither the distinct substantive question nor the required fit.

An arrest establishes no guilt or ongoing danger. Administrative review by a preponderance determines the predicate offense, not whether future lawful presence requires broad exclusion. Conviction establishes past guilt more securely, but does not itself justify the year-long all-zone ban. General crime findings and recidivism concerns fail to explain the necessity of excluding each person's lawful passage.

Written variances protect qualifying preexisting residents and workers; social-service agencies may issue limited variances. Those exceptions omit ordinary passage and the demonstrated caregiving and consultation purposes. The 1998 amendment ends arrest exclusions after acquittal, dismissal or nonprosecution, but does not cure the remaining lack of fit; Johnson's earlier exclusion continued after nonprosecution under the version applied to her.

The dissent treats these predicates, durations and variances as sufficient crime-control limits. They constrain administration but do not justify the full liberty burden. This holding concerns substantial personal neighborhood bans, not ordinary route, hours or place-use management. Lawful arrest, custody, bail, probation and protective restrictions retain their own predicates. The Court prescribes no universal hearing, proof standard or procedure for every geographic regulation; the government must substantiate the necessity and scope of the serious restraint challenged here.

### III. Johnson's established parent-authorized caregiving is protected

**Holding and operative rule:** Government may not substantially interrupt an established parent-authorized family caregiving relationship through a categorical neighborhood exclusion without a compelling justification closely fitted to that interference. The claimant must establish the actual caregiving relationship and substantial disruption; an asserted past offense does not itself justify that disruption.

**Authority:** Part III of the Court's opinion has the same seven-Justice coalition as Part II and independently sustains Johnson's constitutional success.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Johnson's regular, mother-authorized care of five grandchildren, including school trips, is an actual protected family relationship. Moore v. City of East Cleveland protects the reality of extended-family life; Roberts v. United States Jaycees distinguishes intimate relationships from ordinary professional associations. Troxel protects parental direction. The grandmother here seeks to continue the arrangement the parent chose, rather than impose contact against that choice.

The exclusion substantially disrupted that demonstrated caregiving. Drug suppression is compelling, but the City supplies no adequate connection between these lawful caregiving trips and a danger requiring the interference. Its variance structure supplies no accommodation for the established relationship. The dissent characterizes the restriction as conduct-based regulation rather than regulation of family formation. That description does not remove its substantial interruption of the actual parent-chosen relationship. The holding creates no automatic grandparent entitlement, right to override a fit parent's refusal, or immunity from criminal law. Ordinary indirect inconvenience from lawful regulation does not equal the substantial interference shown here.

### IV. Confidential professional representation alone is not intimate association

**Holding and operative rule:** An attorney-client relationship is not constitutionally protected intimate association merely because professional representation is important and confidential. Those features alone do not establish the close personal relationship required by the intimate-association doctrine, so the alternative attorney-association rationale cannot support Au France's judgment.

**Authority:** Part IV of the Court's opinion is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices adopt this proposition. Stone-Zsela does not join this part.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Roberts requires attention to a relationship's nature and function. Professional service and confidentiality alone do not make an adviser a family-like intimate associate. The difficulty reaching Au France's lawyer's office establishes an actual geographic burden; it does not establish the relational characteristics necessary for this distinct theory. No equally convenient alternative meeting site is presumed. Counsel, petitioning and access-to-courts protections have separate sources and elements. Independent claims under those protections lie outside this intimate-association question and are not adjudicated here. Au France's successful movement ground remains sufficient for the reviewed judgment.

### V. The supported fee award survives; continuing relief receives separate review

**Holding and operative rule:** The existing $38,500 fee award is affirmed against the challenge to its granted constitutional predicates because both plaintiffs retain successful movement claims and Johnson also retains the caregiving ground; rejection of the alternative lawyer theory does not itself remove that success. Prospective declaration and injunction components require separate assessment of Burnett, cessation and present exposure; the court must identify the damages request's actual disposition and adjudicate any surviving claim under ordinary rules.

**Authority:** Part V's fee-affirmance proposition has the seven-Justice coalition in Parts II and III. Its distinct prospective-relief and damages-status instructions are joined by all nine Justices.
