- Planned Parenthood of the Columbia/Willamette, Inc. v. American Coalition of Life Activists (December 10, 2002): applied within its civil-threat culpability and finding-specific remedial boundaries; no universal criminal-threat rule is added.

## Law After Decision

The existing facial commerce and speech rules remain unchanged. FACE's actual conduct, intent, service-connection, expression and own-minor limits govern covered enforcement. Civil threats within Planned Parenthood v. ACLA require purpose or knowledge of threatening meaning. Later enforcement must establish an attributable violation in its own proceeding; this affirmance resolves neither the ungranted warning-letter application nor the disputed encounters.

## Separate Writings

## Procedure After Action

The facial claims are concluded by the limited affirmance. The distinct lower-court dispositions outside the grant stand. Any later enforcement proceeds under the statute and controlling liability and remedy rules.

## Source Notes

18 U.S.C. §248(a)(1), (a)(3), (c), (d), (e). Norton v. Ashcroft, 298 F.3d 547 (6th Cir. 2002). The disputed driveway encounters have not been adjudicated as violations in this facial proceeding.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/United-States-v-Jimenez-Recio_merits_2003-01-21.md
Effective date: 2003-01-21


## Event

United States v. Jimenez Recio, No. 01-1184. Merits decision, January 21, 2003. Argued November 12, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit. Police secretly seized drugs from a truck and used cooperating drivers to arrange a controlled delivery. Jimenez Recio and Lopez-Meza later arrived to collect the vehicles. Following two trials, the court of appeals reversed their second-trial convictions because the Government had not sufficiently proved joinder before the seizure under its automatic-termination rule. The granted question is whether unknown governmental frustration of a conspiracy’s objective itself terminates the conspiracy; it does not independently submit all sufficiency, instructional, or double-jeopardy issues.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

## Public Action

Automatic-termination rule rejected unanimously; judgment reversed insofar as it rests on that rule and remanded, 8–1.

## Judgment & Remedy

**The judgment is reversed insofar as it rests on automatic termination, and the case is remanded, 8–1.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.
**Opposing:** Stevens.

All nine Justices agree that unknown government frustration does not automatically terminate a genuine conspiracy. Stevens would preserve the defendants’ appellate victory because of the Government’s accepted trial burden and untimely challenge. The remand requires consideration of remaining properly maintained appellate grounds from the trial record before reinstatement or further trial proceedings. Rejection of automatic termination does not itself authorize an additional trial or a new factual record to supply missing proof; any resulting trial remedy must rest on its own lawful predicate, including the applicable double-jeopardy limits.

## Opinion Topology

Thomas delivered the opinion of the Court, in which O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer joined in full. Stone-Zsela joined Parts I and III. Stevens joined Part I and filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer in full; Stone-Zsela joins Parts I and III; Stevens joins Part I | Part I has nine joins; Part II has seven; Part III has eight. |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Agrees with Part I’s rejection of automatic termination; would preserve the appellate reversal on the procedural ground and rejects Parts II and III. |

## Holdings

### I. Unknown government frustration does not itself terminate a conspiracy

**Holding and operative rule:** A drug conspiracy under 21 U.S.C. §846 does not automatically terminate when government action unknown to its members makes the intended transaction impossible; pre-seizure joinder is therefore not a categorical statutory element. The prosecution must still prove beyond a reasonable doubt an actual unlawful agreement and the defendant’s knowing participation: mere presence, association, awareness, or an arrangement solely with government agents is insufficient, and actual withdrawal, abandonment, termination, and entrapment retain their independent requirements.

**Authority:** Part I of the opinion of the Court, joined by all nine Justices: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Direct unanimous agreement on this proposition does not establish unanimous agreement on the judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** Shabani construes §846 as an agreement-based offense requiring no additional overt act. Salinas supplies the bounded distinction between agreeing to advance an unlawful undertaking and personally completing its acts, within its different RICO setting. Secret seizure changes the plan’s prospects without necessarily changing the private agreement or a participant’s knowing commitment. The Ninth Circuit’s Cruz rule incorrectly makes objective attainability a continuing legal condition and pre-seizure joinder an additional requirement. The defendants’ concern about manufactured liability identifies an actual evidentiary question: a police-only arrangement is not a genuine private conspiracy. The answer is proof of the unlawful agreement and knowing joinder, together with applicable defenses, rather than legal extinction of every agreement when police secretly remove its object. Actual abandonment or withdrawal must be distinguished from an unknown external obstacle. The Court rejects the categorical rule without supplying the factual agreement necessary for these convictions.

### II. Correction of the appellate legal rule preserves the consequences of the trial proceedings

**Holding and operative rule:** The Court may correct the court of appeals’ automatic-termination rule in deciding the granted statutory question without thereby granting relief for an unobjected-to trial instruction or extinguishing the legal consequences of the Government’s accepted trial burden and procedural choices. Those consequences remain for resolution on properly maintained appellate grounds, rather than being treated as irrelevant or conclusively resolved by the substantive correction.

**Authority:** Part II of the opinion of the Court, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Stone-Zsela does not join this additional procedural rationale, and Stevens dissents from it.

**Controlling explanation:** The Government did not object before jury retirement to the second-trial instruction, accepted pre-seizure joinder as its burden in closing, did not initially attack the circuit rule on appeal, and first challenged it on rehearing. The then-applicable Rule 30 objection requirement and the consequence of that accepted burden are real procedural questions. Stevens would treat them as sufficient to preserve the existing insufficiency reversal. The Court instead distinguishes the granted correction of a court of appeals’ statutory rule from a ruling authorizing relief for instructional error. Shabani’s limited appellate return and Quality King’s separation of a legal correction from unadjudicated grounds support leaving distinct questions to their proper disposition. This correction neither assumes government plain-error authority nor decides that nonobjection establishes intentional relinquishment. It also does not select a sufficiency benchmark that disregards the Government’s accepted instruction. The court of appeals must give the procedural history its proper effect on genuinely remaining claims.

### III. The limited reversal requires resolution of remaining proper appellate questions

**Holding and operative rule:** The appellate judgment is reversed only insofar as it rests on automatic termination, and the court of appeals must address remaining properly maintained sufficiency, instructional, and other trial objections from the existing trial record before reinstating the convictions or directing further proceedings. If legally required proof was insufficient, the remedy must respect the bar on another opportunity to supply missing proof; any other trial remedy requires its own lawful predicate.

**Authority:** Part III of the opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Stevens dissents from this remedial disposition while joining Part I.

**Controlling explanation:** Shabani’s remand preserves actual appellate objections after rejection of an added statutory element. Here the legal error concerns automatic termination; the convictions’ ultimate treatment also depends on claims with distinct preservation and remedial requirements. The remand preserves only arguments properly maintained, not a new right to revive forfeited issues. Both actual statutory elements and the legal consequences of the trial and appellate history remain relevant within those claims. Correcting the termination rule supplies no missing evidence and does not itself authorize a third trial. The court of appeals must resolve the remaining proper grounds and apply the corresponding sufficiency, instruction, and double-jeopardy consequences. That is ordinary appellate work under the governing rules, rather than an unresolved definition of the §846 agreement offense.

## Precedent Treatment

- United States v. Shabani, November 1, 1994: applied to preserve actual agreement, knowing participation, proof beyond a reasonable doubt, and the absence of an additional overt-act requirement. Its limited remand supplies the model for resolving remaining appellate grounds.
- Salinas v. United States, December 2, 1997: applied only by analogy for agreement rather than personal completion; its distinct RICO elements are not substituted for §846.
- Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L’anza Research International, Inc., March 9, 1998: the seven-Justice procedural portion applies the distinction between correcting a categorical legal premise and deciding remaining independent grounds; its copyright rule is not extended to conspiracy liability.
- United States v. Cruz, 127 F.3d 791: the lower court’s automatic-termination rule is rejected. Unknown police frustration no longer supplies its categorical pre-seizure-joinder requirement.

## Law After Decision

Effective January 21, 2003, unknown governmental frustration of the unlawful object does not automatically terminate a §846 agreement. Genuine agreement and knowing joinder remain indispensable, with their ordinary proof and defense requirements. The Court adds that legal application to Shabani without changing the offense to punish mere presence or an agreement only with agents. Correction of the appellate rule leaves the independent legal consequences of accepted instructions, preservation, sufficiency, and double jeopardy for proper appellate treatment. The statute’s rule is settled; the remaining work concerns these convictions under that rule and the applicable procedural protections.

## Separate Writings

**Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He agrees that §846 does not make a conspiracy end automatically upon secret government seizure. He would nevertheless preserve the defendants’ appellate victory. Rule 30 required a timely instructional objection before jury retirement, but the Government made none, affirmatively accepted pre-seizure joinder as its closing burden, and first attacked the circuit rule on rehearing after not challenging it in its initial appellate presentation. For Stevens, characterizing the requested relief as correction of appellate doctrine does not justify removing an insufficiency reversal reached under the very trial standard the Government accepted. His own procedural objection, also reflected in his Williams dissent, supplies the ground for leaving that judgment in place now; it neither endorses automatic termination nor finds that these defendants could never join a real conspiracy. He rejects the Court’s limited reversal because he regards the established procedural history as already sufficient to preserve the result.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns to the Ninth Circuit after reversal only of the automatic-termination basis. That court must resolve remaining properly maintained appellate grounds from the trial record, including the proper consequences of the instruction and the Government’s procedural conduct, and then determine the lawful treatment of the convictions. Neither reinstatement nor another trial follows automatically from this mandate; a remedy for insufficient legally required proof must preserve the applicable bar against a second opportunity to supply it.

## Source Notes

537 U.S. 270; No. 01-1184; 21 U.S.C. §846; Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 30 as applicable at trial; United States v. Cruz, 127 F.3d 791. The second-trial instruction appears at petition appendix 75a–76a; the Government’s closing acceptance of pre-seizure joinder appears at opposition appendix 34a. The procedural record supplies no new factual proof of the defendants’ knowing agreement.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Brown-v-Li_merits_2003-01-22.md
Effective date: 2003-01-22


## Event

Brown v. Li; Ninth Circuit No. 01-55930; judgment below, 308 F.3d 939. October Term 2002. Decided January 22, 2003, after full merits submission. The Court reviews the Ninth Circuit's affirmance of federal summary judgment concerning rejection and nonarchiving of an altered graduate thesis, with a separate state constitutional claim remanded. The question is whether the actual whole-thesis approval and professional-presentation requirements justify the challenged response under the First Amendment.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the federal judgment and preserves the separate state constitutional remand.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Federal judgment affirmed, 8–1. **Supporting:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. **Opposing:** Stone-Zsela, who would vacate the federal summary judgment and remand the surviving archive and injury claims. The awarded degree remains intact. The unresolved state constitutional claim continues on its existing remand.

## Opinion Topology

Souter delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Stone-Zsela dissents from the federal affirmance and joins no part of the opinion.

## Holdings

### Genuine approval requirements govern the complete academic submission

**Holding and operative rule:** A public university may require approval and professionally appropriate presentation of the complete graduate thesis it certifies, including optional acknowledgments, when those are genuine requirements reasonably related to its academic task. A supported actual academic ground may sustain summary judgment, but a merely conceivable reason cannot defeat evidence creating a material dispute about unconstitutional causation; under Mt. Healthy, the student must establish protected conduct's substantial or motivating role, after which officials bear the preponderance burden of proving the same decision would have followed for a lawful independent reason.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; eight complete joins establish the rule and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the university could insist on approval of the document actually submitted. Settle permits genuine academic requirements while retaining scrutiny of the reason actually used. Hazelwood supplies the connection between institutional responsibility and legitimate pedagogical control; here that connection is the committee's certification of a complete thesis. Horowitz and Ewing support respect for genuine academic judgment, rather than immunity for an academic label.

Brown obtained approval for a thesis without acknowledgments and then inserted unreviewed material under the existing signatures. The university's flexible-format instruction for optional acknowledgments did not displace the whole-document approval and professional-presentation requirements. The committee actually relied on those requirements in rejecting the altered versions. Rejection of the nonprofane revision therefore does not, on this record, make the stated academic ground merely hypothetical: removing profanity did not supply committee approval or satisfy the presentation judgment. The distinction from Settle is the supported causal record, not a weaker constitutional standard. The contrary view would require further inquiry into the scope and motive of the response; the Court concludes that the supplied record supports the federal judgment under the genuine requirements actually invoked. This holding concerns the thesis-certification function and preserves the protection applicable to independent criticism.

## Precedent Treatment

- Settle v. Dickson County School Board (June 25, 1996) is applied: genuine academic requirements and actual-causation review remain controlling; this record supports a different summary-judgment result.
- Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle, 429 U.S. 274, retains the student's substantial-or-motivating-factor burden and the officials' separate preponderance same-decision defense.
- Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier, 484 U.S. 260, supplies the legitimate-pedagogy rationale as fitted to the complete thesis's certification function.
- Board of Curators v. Horowitz, 435 U.S. 78, and Regents of the University of Michigan v. Ewing, 474 U.S. 214, support respect for genuine academic judgments while leaving pretext subject to constitutional review.

## Law After Decision

The genuine-academic-requirements framework applies to complete graduate theses, including optional acknowledgments submitted under committee approval. Flexible formatting does not itself exempt that material from actual approval and professional standards. Settle's causation and Mt. Healthy's separate same-decision rules retain their force.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela would vacate the federal summary judgment. He agrees that the altered document cannot automatically inherit approval given to another draft, but concludes that this ground does not conclusively explain every alleged sanction and continued archival refusal. The nonprofane revision and the scope of the response warrant inquiry under Settle and Mt. Healthy into actual motive and an independent same-decision defense. His proposed remand would identify the responsible defendants and live injuries; a proved violation would require a timely decision under genuine standards and an effective correction if no lawful refusal ground remained. Damages would remain subject to individual responsibility, conduct-date immunity and proof of loss.

## Procedure After Action

The mandate leaves the federal judgment in force and returns the state constitutional claim for its separate adjudication. The degree already awarded is preserved.

## Source Notes

Brown v. Li, 308 F.3d 939; Ninth Circuit No. 01-55930. The appellate judgment was issued August 12, 2002. The degree rests on the approved draft; the altered thesis remains the subject of the archive dispute.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Meyer-v-Holley_merits_2003-01-22.md
Effective date: 2003-01-22


## Event

Meyer v. Holley, No. 01-1120, 537 U.S. 280. October Term 2002. Argued December 3, 2002; decided January 22, 2003. The Court reviews the Ninth Circuit's reversal of a Rule 54(b) summary judgment for Meyer on the surviving FHA theory, following an earlier pleading disposition of the officer-capacity theory. Claims against Triad and Crank remained pending. The question is whether corporate ownership, office or designated-broker control alone creates personal vicarious liability for a corporate employee's alleged discriminatory refusal to sell. The Holleys allege a racially discriminatory interference with their attempted 1996 purchase. Meyer's presidency and broker designation are established, but ownership after his asserted 1995 transfer was disputed; direct participation was not alleged. The review concerns compensatory attribution.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer participated at submission and decision. All nine participate.

## Public Action

Ninth Circuit judgment vacated and remanded for ordinary attribution and genuinely preserved alternatives, 9–0.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Vacated and remanded, 9–0. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. **Opposing:** None. The Ninth Circuit's special personal vicarious-liability rule is removed. The lower courts must apply ordinary attribution law to genuinely preserved alternatives as directed in Part III. The pending corporate and salesman claims remain distinct.

## Opinion Topology

Souter delivered the opinion of the Court, in which Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer joined. The opinion is unanimous throughout Parts I–III.

## Holdings

### I. Ordinary agency and corporate principles govern compensatory attribution

**Holding and operative rule:** For the compensatory vicarious-liability question presented, the Fair Housing Act incorporates ordinary agency and corporate principles: an employee's qualifying conduct may be attributed to the corporate principal, but office, ownership or control of that corporation alone does not make the individual officer, shareholder or designated broker a second principal. Personal liability requires a legally sufficient individual agency relationship, the individual's own actionable wrongdoing, or an applicable basis for disregarding corporate separateness, with the relevant theory properly preserved and proved.

**Authority:** Part I of the opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this rule and explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** Nationwide Mutual Insurance Co. v. Darden supplies the interpretive principle that Congress ordinarily incorporates common-law agency where the statute supplies no different rule. The Court uses that method by analogy, not Darden's ERISA employee checklist as an FHA test. General Building Contractors Association v. Pennsylvania distinguishes agency responsibility from an inferred nondelegable duty. United States v. Bestfoods preserves corporate separateness while recognizing supported derivative liability and a genuinely independent direct statutory duty. The FHA's important antidiscrimination purpose cannot alone create an omitted individual principal or impose a nondelegable personal guarantee on every controlling officer. Corporate-employer liability and the actor's own liability remain effective remedies under the actual relationships. Faragher and Ellerth likewise require actual agency-based attribution in their employment settings; their material-aid rules do not make a corporate supervisor the individual employer of all corporate employees. The Holleys must establish the alleged discrimination and a relationship or conduct legally attributing it to each defendant by a preponderance. Here Meyer was president and designated corporate officer/broker, but ownership at the 1996 transaction was disputed after his asserted 1995 transfer. No direct participation was alleged, and the evidence did not establish his direction or approval. Those facts cannot be replaced by a status-based liability rule. Punitive vicarious damages lie outside this reviewed compensatory question.

**Precedent treatment:** Darden supplies the common-law interpretive analogy; General Building Contractors rejects an unsupported nondelegable duty; Bestfoods preserves corporate distinctions and genuine independent duties; Faragher and Ellerth remain confined to their actual agency-based employment holdings.

### II. The regulation and broker designation do not impose liability from control alone

**Holding and operative rule:** Former 24 C.F.R. §103.20(b) requires discrimination by the directing or controlling person's employee or agent acting within the scope of that authority; direction or control alone is insufficient. The later notification rule and California's designated-broker supervisory obligations do not themselves displace the ordinary federal attribution requirements.

**Authority:** Part II of the opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this rule and explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** The complete former regulation qualifies direction or control by an actual employee-or-agent relationship and conduct within its scope. The circuit's approach detaches the control language from those limits. The 1999 provision, §103.202(b), addresses notification of persons who direct or control, while §103.203(a) separately preserves defenses available in court. Notification is not a substantive expansion of responsibility. A state licensing obligation can be relevant to a properly presented agency theory, but the title and right to supervise do not alone establish that the corporate salesman was Meyer's individual agent. The actual relationship must satisfy the applicable ordinary elements. The statutory purpose cannot supply the missing relationship that neither statutory nor regulatory text establishes.

**Precedent treatment:** The ordinary-agency background described in Darden and General Building Contractors remains governing; Bestfoods's distinction between control of a corporation and the individual's legal responsibility is preserved.

### III. Vacatur removes the special rule while preserving only genuine alternatives

**Holding and operative rule:** The Ninth Circuit's judgment is vacated and the case remanded to determine whether any traditional veil-piercing or other ordinary attribution theory was actually raised and preserved and, if so, whether its elements are established. Rejection of the circuit's special rule neither proves a personal theory nor extinguishes a legally sufficient corporate or employee claim.

**Authority:** Part III of the opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this rule and explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** The challenged appellate rule governed reversal of a Rule 54(b) summary judgment for Meyer after an earlier pleading disposition of the officer-capacity theory; Triad and Crank claims remained pending. Leaving that broader rule in place would misdirect further proceedings. The opposing request to end all claims against Meyer rests on a contested preservation premise: the United States' argument identifies a claimed prior presentation of veil piercing, while Meyer disputes preservation. The Court resolves neither account by assumption. The return permits only genuinely preserved theories under ordinary procedure. Ownership must be resolved only as material to such a theory; sole ownership, inadequate assets or office alone is no veil-piercing determination. A general rule of personal responsibility for one's own wrongdoing cannot invent a direct-participation allegation or revive an unappealed dismissal. The choice between state and federal veil-piercing law is not reached because the question presented concerns the special FHA attribution rule and preservation of an ordinary theory remains undetermined; Bestfoods likewise left that choice open. The lower courts must apply the governing elements to the evidence and resolve defenses and corresponding relief. Ordinary proceedings may dispose of a preserved theory without trial or new discovery; this remand grants no independent entitlement to either.

**Precedent treatment:** Bestfoods is applied to the distinction between derivative responsibility and direct wrongdoing, with its choice-of-law reservation preserved. Darden and General Building Contractors supply the ordinary-law remit rather than the rejected special duty.

## Precedent Treatment

- Nationwide Mutual Insurance Co. v. Darden, 503 U.S. 318 (March 24, 1992): applied by interpretive analogy; its ERISA checklist is not an FHA holding.
- General Building Contractors Association, Inc. v. Pennsylvania, 458 U.S. 375: applied to reject an inferred nondelegable duty in place of an actual agency relationship.
- United States v. Bestfoods (June 8, 1998): applied; ordinary ownership does not collapse corporate separateness, and supported derivative liability remains distinct from an independently established direct duty. Its state-or-federal veil-piercing choice remains open in this posture.
- Faragher v. City of Boca Raton (September 8, 1997) and Burlington Industries, Inc. v. Ellerth (June 26, 1998): their actual-agency and material-aid principles support the attribution distinction without importing their employment-specific applications or reserved defenses as an FHA test.

## Law After Decision

Ordinary agency and corporate principles govern the presented FHA compensatory attribution. Status and supervisory control alone create no personal nondelegable guarantee. Corporate and direct-actor responsibility remain independent, and properly preserved individual-agency or corporate-disregard theories receive their ordinary elements and defenses. The regulation's relationship-and-scope limits and distinct notification provisions retain their force. This recurring attribution rule is settled; preservation, evidence and relief on the actual surviving theories remain ordinary adjudication.

## Separate Writings

## Procedure After Action

The vacated appellate judgment returns for further proceedings under ordinary agency and corporate law. The lower courts first identify the properly preserved personal theory, then apply its elements and defenses to the evidence. Existing claims against the corporation and salesman continue in their own posture.

## Source Notes

Meyer v. Holley, No. 01-1120, 537 U.S. 280; Holley v. Crank, 258 F.3d 1127 (9th Cir. 2001). 42 U.S.C. §§3604, 3613; former 24 C.F.R. §103.20(b); 24 C.F.R. §§103.202(b), 103.203(a). Brief for the United States as Amicus Curiae, No. 01-1120 (September 2002), pt. II & n.6. Ownership at the alleged transaction and preservation of ordinary alternatives remain disputed; no direct-participation allegation is established.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/FCC-v-NextWave-Personal-Communications-Inc_merits_2003-01-27.md
Effective date: 2003-01-27


## Event

FCC v. NextWave Personal Communications, Inc.; Nos. 01-653 and 01-657, consolidated. October Term 2002. Argued October 8, 2002; decided January 27, 2003. The Court reviews the D.C. Circuit's judgment setting aside final FCC license cancellations. The question is whether Bankruptcy Code §525(a) prohibits cancellation solely for nonpayment of dischargeable auction debt when payment also serves a regulatory purpose.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the judgment setting aside the FCC cancellation in both consolidated matters.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Affirmed, 8–1. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. **Opposing:** Breyer, who would vacate and remand for application of his bankruptcy-related purpose-or-effect construction. The FCC must treat the challenged cancellation as ineffective and conform licensing records and dependent reauction action to that result. The debt remains subject to lawful bankruptcy treatment.

## Opinion Topology

Scalia delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Stevens concurs separately while joining the complete opinion. Breyer dissents.

## Holdings

### Regulatory purpose does not remove a qualifying nonpayment cancellation from §525(a)

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 525(a) bars a governmental unit from canceling a qualifying debtor's license solely because the debtor failed to pay a debt dischargeable in the bankruptcy case, even when the payment requirement serves a regulatory purpose; a completed discharge is unnecessary. The debtor must establish statutory coverage, a qualifying debt and the operative nonpayment cause, subject to the express exceptions for the Perishable Agricultural Commodities Act, the Packers and Stockyards Act, and section 1 of the July 12, 1943 Agriculture appropriation Act; none applies here.

**Authority:** Scalia's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; eight complete joins establish this holding and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the FCC's regulatory purpose does not change what caused cancellation. The installment notes establish a right to payment and therefore a claim and debt under §§101(5) and 101(12). Davenport and Kovacs supply the payment-obligation distinction; these monetary instruments satisfy it directly. Section 525(a) expressly addresses governmental licenses and nonpayment of dischargeable debt. Its protection does not await an order discharging a particular sum.

The FCC's only operative cancellation trigger was failure to make those payments. A regulatory reason for requiring payment is not a second, independent cause. Congress's specified agricultural exceptions reinforce the conclusion that a broader regulatory exception cannot be supplied by administrative preference. Hartford Underwriters requires adherence to the enacted bankruptcy allocation. The dissent's concern about ordinary security enforcement and unequal treatment of government and private creditors cannot replace this express licensing protection with a requirement of discriminatory motive or proven fresh-start interference. The holding leaves a genuine independent service, construction or spectrum-use violation to its own requirements. Relabeling this same default does not make it independent.

### The auction statute and authorized review route permit this cancellation to be set aside

**Holding and operative rule:** Communications Act §309(j) contains no incompatible command requiring cancellation of these licenses notwithstanding §525(a). The D.C. Circuit may set aside the final FCC action through the authorized communications-law review channel, with the distinct debt, lien and independent-license issues left to their proper proceedings.

**Authority:** The same eight-Justice opinion controls.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the auction and bankruptcy provisions can operate together. Authority to conduct auctions and accept installment payments does not require cancellation in violation of the separate licensing prohibition. Auction efficiency and concern about strategic default do not identify an irreconcilable statutory command. Dow Corning rejects reliance on general bankruptcy implementation powers to extinguish independent nondebtor claims; the source here is Congress's express restriction on governmental licensing. Celotex preserves the distinction between jurisdiction, the authorized judicial act and its remedy. The D.C. Circuit is reviewing final FCC action under the communications statutes, not directing a bankruptcy court to exercise appellate power over the agency. Setting aside cancellation leaves the underlying debt in place. The treatment of liens and the specific availability of a secured-creditor remedy are outside the cancellation question reviewed here.

## Precedent Treatment

- Hartford Underwriters Insurance Co. v. Union Planters Bank, N.A., 530 U.S. 1, is applied for enforcing Congress's actual bankruptcy allocation rather than a policy substitute.
- Pennsylvania Department of Public Welfare v. Davenport, 495 U.S. 552, and Ohio v. Kovacs, 469 U.S. 274, supply the distinction between an enforceable monetary obligation and a regulatory duty; the FCC's notes establish the former.
- Celotex Corp. v. Edwards (April 19, 1995) preserves distinct questions of jurisdiction, adjudicative authority and relief; authorized agency review supplies the channel here.
- In re Dow Corning Corp. (November 5, 2002) is distinguished: its rejection of compulsory independent-claim releases through general plan powers leaves §525(a)'s express protection fully effective.

## Law After Decision

Qualifying debtors receive §525(a)'s licensing protection when dischargeable nonpayment is the sole operative cancellation cause. A regulatory purpose does not create an exception, and a completed discharge is not a prerequisite. Independent operational grounds and creditor remedies continue under their own legal requirements. Auction authority and the express bankruptcy prohibition operate together.

## Separate Writings

Stevens joins the Court's complete operative construction and concurs to explain why statutory context supports it. Although purpose can matter in interpretation, the express agricultural exceptions, preservation of an estate asset and the FCC's other regulatory protections support this result.

Breyer would vacate and remand. He requires a relationship between bankruptcy and revocation through bankruptcy-related discrimination or significant interference with the promised fresh start. An ordinary effort to enforce a secured sale may fall outside §525(a) when neither feature is present. He regards the different consequences for government and private creditors as a reason to consider statutory context, and would permit a determination of whether the FCC can establish the necessary conditions rather than conclusively approve the cancellation.

## Procedure After Action

The mandate leaves the challenged cancellation set aside. The FCC must conform the affected licensing and dependent reauction action to the judgment; any asserted independent licensing violation requires its own established ground and proper process. Bankruptcy treatment of the debt and any separate secured remedy proceed in their authorized forums.

## Source Notes

Nos. 01-653 and 01-657; 11 U.S.C. §§101(5), 101(12), 525(a); 47 U.S.C. §§309(j), 402. The installment notes establish monetary obligations, and nonpayment supplied the challenged cancellation trigger.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Konop-v-Hawaiian-Airlines-Inc_merits_2003-01-27.md
Effective date: 2003-01-27


## Event

Konop v. Hawaiian Airlines, Inc.; Ninth Circuit No. 99-55106; judgment below, 302 F.3d 868. October Term 2002. Decided January 27, 2003, after full merits submission. The Court reviews the August 23, 2002 appellate judgment on two questions: whether later retrieval of stored website pages is an interception under the Wiretap Act, and whether an eligible person who had not used the service may authorize access under the Stored Communications Act.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms both reviewed appellate privacy dispositions and remands the surviving Stored Communications Act claim.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Vote |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wiretap Act | Summary judgment for Hawaiian affirmed. | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | 9–0 |
| Stored Communications Act | Appellate reversal of summary judgment on the user-consent defense affirmed; surviving claim remanded. | The same nine Justices | 9–0 |

The separate labor and unappealed state-claim dispositions remain intact. On remand the court must identify each challenged access and purported authorizing user, determine the necessary use and consent facts, then adjudicate the remaining statutory elements, defenses and remedy.

## Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. The same unanimous opinion controls both statutory components.

## Holdings

### Later retrieval of stored pages is not contemporaneous interception

**Holding and operative rule:** For the later retrieval of stored website pages shown here, an interception requires acquisition contemporaneous with transmission, rather than subsequent access to stored content. Konop bears the burden to establish an interception, and the undisputed retrieval sequence does not satisfy that element.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, joined by all eight Associate Justices; nine complete joins support both holdings.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that obtaining these pages after storage is different from acquiring the electronic communication while it is being transmitted. The conduct-date Wiretap Act and Stored Communications Act protect different acquisition events through distinct conditions. Reading interception to encompass every later viewing of the same content would erase that structure. The general privacy objection cannot substitute for the acquisition element because stored access has its own enacted protection. Reno's protection of Internet expression does not define these statutory categories. This record concerns later retrieval; transient buffers and materially different transmission-storage settings are outside the facts and question decided.

### User consent requires actual authorized use and the specified communication relationship

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 2701(c)(2) requires authorization by a person who actually uses the service, is duly authorized by its provider to do so, and gives consent concerning a communication of or intended for that user. Hawaiian cannot obtain summary judgment on this exception without establishing those predicates free of a genuine material dispute; unused eligibility does not establish user status.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion controls.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that eligibility and use are cumulative rather than interchangeable. Section 2510(13) requires both use of the electronic communication service and provider authorization for that use. Section 2701(c)(2) separately requires a relationship between the consenting user and the communication accessed. A legislative reference to intended recipients cannot replace the express actual-use condition. Nor can one person's permission become unrestricted authority over other communications.

Wong had not used the website and therefore did not supply the required user status. Whether and when Gardner actually used it remain unresolved and must be determined in relation to the challenged accesses. The parties' electronic-storage, service and otherwise-unauthorized-access assumptions frame this exception question; they do not establish the remaining claim. The employer invoking the exception must substantiate its predicates, while Konop retains his burden to prove the remaining statutory violation by a preponderance. Relief then follows the operative statute and proved injury or statutory entitlement. The website's contractual restrictions do not replace this statutory inquiry.

## Precedent Treatment

- Hartford Underwriters Insurance Co. v. Union Planters Bank, N.A., 530 U.S. 1, supplies the source-specific approach that gives enacted actor and remedy conditions independent effect; its bankruptcy holding is unchanged.
- Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union concerns constitutional protection for Internet expression and is distinguished from the ECPA's acquisition and consent definitions.

## Law After Decision

The conduct-date ECPA distinguishes later retrieval of stored website pages from interception during transmission. Its user-consent exception requires actual provider-authorized use plus consent tied to a communication of or intended for that user. These rules govern the two claims separately; the remaining SCA facts and remedy require ordinary adjudication under them.

## Separate Writings

## Procedure After Action

The Wiretap Act judgment remains in force. The surviving SCA claim returns for the actual-use chronology, access-specific authorization, remaining elements and defenses, and any remedy established under the governing statute. The separate labor and state dispositions remain intact.

## Source Notes

Konop v. Hawaiian Airlines, Inc., 302 F.3d 868; Ninth Circuit No. 99-55106; 18 U.S.C. §§2510(4), 2510(12), 2510(13), 2701(a), 2701(c)(2), in the version governing the 1995–1996 conduct. Electronic storage, the relevant service and otherwise unauthorized access were assumed for the reviewed exception question. Gardner's actual use and timing remain factual questions.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Planned-Parenthood-of-the-Rocky-Mountains-v-Owens_merits_2003-02-24.md
Effective date: 2003-02-24


## Event

Planned Parenthood of the Rocky Mountains Services, Corp. v. Owens. Plenary merits decision, February 24, 2003. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, 287 F.3d 910, No. 00-1385, affirming 107 F. Supp. 2d 1271. The questions concern Colorado’s notice and forty-eight-hour delay without effective necessary-health protection, the asserted Children’s Code cure, and the prospective remedy.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The necessary-health constitutional determination is affirmed, 7–2; the decree is vacated only to the extent of unsupported foreclosure of independently lawful, severable operation and remanded, 6–3, with effective protection against the established health violation preserved.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Necessary-health defect and entitlement to effective prospective protection | Affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Notice, waiting and dependent criminal or civil sanctions cannot prevent timely care in the established serious-health circumstances. |
| Permanent foreclosure of independently lawful, severable operation without Colorado-law inquiry | Vacated to that extent and remanded, 6–3 | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Thomas | Conform the decree under Colorado severability law while preserving effective existing protection against the health violation. |

Stone-Zsela would retain the entire existing restraint until a lawful, fully effective remainder is demonstrated; Scalia and Thomas would reverse the health-based constitutional judgment and remove relief dependent on it. These are different alternatives. The majority requires protection of necessary-health applications throughout conformity, preserves broader relief where the command cannot lawfully be separated, and leaves independently pending federal claims for ordinary disposition.

## Opinion Topology

Souter delivers the opinion of the Court, with the joins specified below. Kennedy files an opinion concurring in part. Stone-Zsela files an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part. Scalia files a dissenting opinion, joined by Thomas.

| Writing or part | Author | Direct coalition, including author | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Court opinion, Parts I–II | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Necessary care and statutory insufficiency; seven full joins. |
| Court opinion, Part III | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Complete Casey framework; six full joins. |
| Court opinion, Part IV | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Partial vacatur and conformity of relief; six full joins. |
| Partial concurrence | Kennedy | Kennedy | Joins I, II and IV; withholds III’s residual-interference rationale. |
| Partial concurrence and partial dissent | Stone-Zsela | Stone-Zsela | Joins I–III; would preserve the existing restraint pending demonstrated lawful severance. |
| Dissent | Scalia | Scalia, Thomas | Would reverse the health-based constitutional determination and dependent relief. |

## Holdings

### I. Necessary health care cannot await imminent death

**Holding and operative rule:** A notice or waiting requirement may not prevent abortion necessary in competent, reasonable clinical judgment to avoid an established significant threat to the patient’s life or serious health injury before compliance is possible. Protection must permit timely treatment before imminent death or catastrophic deterioration; a death-only defense does not protect the necessary nonfatal care the record establishes.

**Authority:** Part I of the Court’s opinion; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join.

**Controlling explanation:** Casey retains timely necessary life-and-health care as a protected baseline. Voinovich distinguishes clinically established serious danger from mere distress or preference, and Stenberg preserves care when a statute’s life-only accommodation omits supported serious-health needs. Colorado’s forty-eight-hour interval can delay treatment in conceded covered complications until serious injury occurs even though death is not imminent. The availability of later punishment defenses cannot supply a health ground the statute excludes. Legitimate parental involvement does not justify that injury.

The clinician must identify the patient’s condition, the danger attributable to compliance and the timing necessary for treatment through reasonable professional judgment. Neither a bare assertion nor a preferred procedure automatically establishes necessity; ordinary professional standards and enforcement against knowing falsehood remain. Advance judicial permission cannot be required where obtaining it creates the protected danger. The Court’s conclusion follows from the established medical facts, so it does not require an injured minor to suffer the threatened harm first.

### II. The separate statutes do not supply the missing clinical protection

**Holding and operative rule:** Colorado’s listed notification exceptions, imminent-death defense and distinct judicial procedures do not fairly provide the necessary-health protection proved absent here. A saving construction must arise from the enacted terms or authoritative construction, and cannot replace those terms with a new clinical exception.

**Authority:** Part II, joined by the same seven Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** The Act excuses its duties for prior written acknowledgment of notice and for declared abuse or neglect by the person entitled to notice accompanied by the required report. Its treatment defense requires imminent death and inability to give notice in time. Neither covers the conceded nonfatal health danger. The general Children’s Code permits judicial authorization of emergency treatment when parents cannot reasonably be notified; it does not unconditionally release physicians from the specific later notice duty or guarantee permission before treatment must begin. The Act’s separate bypass becomes operative if notice is enjoined for lack of a bypass, a different trigger from clinical necessity.

Lambert sustained an independent notice-specific best-interests ground; it did not make emergency care await judicial permission. Reading provisions together cannot supply an exception their specific conditions omit. Because no fairly available construction cures this defect, certification is unwarranted on the presented statutes. The justification defense first raised in reply is unpreserved, and the disputed coverage of ectopic-pregnancy treatment is unnecessary because other covered complications establish the violation.

### III. The complete Casey standard remains controlling

**Holding and operative rule:** Casey’s complete framework governs abortion regulation: identify the enacted duty and protected baseline; determine actual function and purpose; establish attributable additional interference and a stable affected population; apply the ordered substantive protections; preserve clinical judgment and truthful communication; and tailor relief to the proved violation. Absence of a substantial obstacle does not end review of remaining severe or meaningful interference.

**Authority:** Part III; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this rationale. Kennedy’s necessary-care joins do not adopt this residual tier.

**Controlling explanation:** Challengers establish the duty, causation, affected population and triggering interference. Baseline violations, proved obstructive purpose and substantial obstacles independently fail. Other severe interference requires compelling justification and narrow tailoring. Remaining meaningful interference requires substantiated permissible reasons and fit, including an answer to a concrete, practicable, substantially less burdensome alternative adequately serving the objective. Experience and reasonable predictions may suffice; the standard requires neither scientific certainty, a new study in every case nor the cheapest imaginable policy. Below meaningful interference that heightened access inquiry ends while independent constitutional requirements remain. Casey, as applied in Fargo and Stenberg, preserves all of these distinct protections. This case is resolved at the necessary-care baseline; the Court does not replace the complete rule with an obstacle-only test or use Salerno’s no-valid-application rule to deny relief for proved health injury.

### IV. Relief must preserve necessary care and lawful severable operation

**Holding and operative rule:** The decree must bar notice, delay and dependent sanctions in the established necessary-health circumstances, while permitting independently lawful operation only as Colorado severability law allows without judicial rewriting. Its unsupported permanent foreclosure of such lawful operation is vacated for conformity, with existing effective protection against the established health violation preserved throughout that work.

**Authority:** Part IV; Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join.

**Controlling explanation:** Casey’s remedy principle and Leavitt require attention to the violation proved and the State’s severability law. The number of minors who can comply safely does not defeat necessary-care protection; the health violation likewise does not establish that every ordinary notice application must be permanently disabled. The lower court must determine whether an application-limited decree preserves an independently operable legislative arrangement, protect the covered medical circumstances and associated sanctions, and retain broader relief to the extent lawful separation is unavailable. Partial vacatur does not authorize harmful enforcement while the court performs that work. Existing health facts need not be proved again. Stone-Zsela’s preference to retain the entire decree until severability is demonstrated does not alter the majority’s obligation to protect care; the difference concerns whether the decree’s unsupported permanent reach stands pending conformity.

## Precedent Treatment

- Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey supplies the necessary-care baseline, complete six-step rule and violation-matched remedy; its full standard remains controlling.
- Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich supplies the distinction between serious, medically established health danger and unsupported preference, without importing Ohio’s particular defenses into Colorado law.
- Stenberg v. Carhart supplies the required protection of supported necessary-health applications despite a narrower life exception; its Nebraska-specific decree is not transplanted.
- Lambert v. Wicklund remains a limited decision about an independent notice-best-interests bypass, not approval of a judicial prerequisite to urgent clinical care.
- Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer preserves the complete substantive inquiry and the distinction between intelligible language and adequate care protection.
- Leavitt v. Jane L. requires application of the State’s severability law before extending invalidity to independently operable provisions or applications.
- United States v. Salerno supplies no no-valid-application barrier to this abortion challenge under Casey; the Court does not alter its operation throughout constitutional law.

## Law After Decision

A parental-notification system must permit timely clinically necessary treatment in established serious-health circumstances, even when death is not imminent. A distinct judicial procedure or punishment defense protects that care only to the extent its actual legal conditions do so. Colorado’s decree must preserve that substantive protection while lawful severability is determined. The full Casey framework remains unchanged.

## Separate Writings

**Kennedy, concurring in part.** He joins Parts I, II and IV because Casey’s protected-choice and necessary-care principles independently forbid the proved health-threatening delay. Voinovich’s necessary-health holding and his care-preserving Stenberg position support that conclusion. The conceded urgent need distinguishes this record from a dispute over the comparative necessity of abortion methods. He accepts the state-law severability inquiry and continuous care protection, but does not join Part III’s residual lesser-burden justification rule.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He joins the health, construction and full-framework holdings but would affirm the existing restraint and remand only to determine whether Colorado law permits fully effective severance. The State may obtain narrower relief upon demonstrating that lawful operation will protect necessary care without rewriting the statute. Because the present record does not establish that remainder, a possibility of later tailoring does not justify partial vacatur now. His position leaves unadjudicated federal claims available and does not condemn parental involvement as a category.

**Scalia, dissenting, joined by Thomas.** The dissenters retain their Casey, Voinovich and Stenberg objection to the asserted constitutional abortion-health entitlement. They would uphold reasonable parental-notification regulation under their constitutional standard, reverse the health-based determination and remove relief resting on it. Their disagreement does not deny the conceded medical complications or invent a health exception in the death-only text. Independent undecided grounds remain for ordinary disposition.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for conformity of the injunction under Colorado severability law. Effective protection against the established necessary-health violation remains while that work proceeds. The lower court must retain broader protection if no lawful independently operable remainder exists, and address independently pending federal claims in their preserved posture.

## Source Notes

287 F.3d 910, Tenth Circuit No. 00-1385; 107 F. Supp. 2d 1271. Colorado Revised Statutes §§12-37.5-101 through -108 and §19-1-104(3). The medical evidence establishes covered serious-health emergencies short of imminent death; disputed ectopic-pregnancy coverage is unnecessary to the judgment.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/California-First-Amendment-Coalition-v-Woodford_merits_2003-02-25.md
Effective date: 2003-02-25


## Event

California First Amendment Coalition v. Woodford; judgment below, 299 F.3d 868. October Term 2002. Decided February 25, 2003, after full merits submission. The Court reviews the Ninth Circuit's affirmance of a permanent injunction requiring observation of executions from chamber entry through declaration of death. The questions concern qualified public and press access to restraint and intravenous preparation and the security justification for concealing those stages.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the permanent observation injunction on the federal constitutional ground.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Affirmed, 6–3. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. **Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas. Admitted public and press witnesses must be able to observe continuously from the condemned prisoner's entry into the execution chamber through declaration of death, including restraint and intravenous insertion. Lawful admission, conduct and anonymity measures remain available to officials.

## Opinion Topology

Breyer delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg. Stone-Zsela concurs separately. O'Connor dissents, joined by Scalia and Thomas.

## Holdings

### Qualified access covers the continuous execution

**Holding and operative rule:** Public and press witnesses admitted to an execution have a qualified First Amendment right to observe the continuous proceeding from chamber entry through death, including integral restraint and intravenous preparation. The right concerns observation by admitted witnesses, subject to lawful admission, conduct and anonymity controls.

**Authority:** Breyer's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg; six complete joins control both holdings.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the initial procedures are part of the execution the witnesses attend. Richmond Newspapers and Press-Enterprise connect access with historical openness and informed public oversight. El Vocero directs attention to the type of proceeding and its function, rather than one jurisdiction's practice alone. The tradition of witnessed executions, including California's observation of corresponding preparations under earlier methods, supports continuity. Intravenous technology does not turn the restraint and invasive preparation into a different closed administrative event. Observation informs the public about pain, difficulties and the actual administration of the sentence; an official account is not an equivalent. Pell and Houchins reject general prison-access demands, but these witnesses seek to observe the particular governmental act to which they are already admitted. United Reporting preserves that distinction between general information and an independently protected activity. The continuing policy supplies prospective injury, and the earlier remand permitted the developed factual showing.

### The categorical curtain fails even contextual prison review

**Holding and operative rule:** Procedure 770's categorical concealment of those stages is an exaggerated response on the supported trial findings and fails Turner: it lacks an adequate connection to the asserted justification, supplies no equivalent alternative observation, and disregards workable anonymity measures whose supported operational costs do not justify the blackout. Turner requires contextual review of connection, alternatives, accommodation effects and ready alternatives; it does not require the least restrictive means.

**Authority:** The same six-Justice opinion controls.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that deference requires consideration of the record, not acceptance of an untested assertion. Shaw preserves meaningful application of Turner's factors. Here live testimony and demonstrations supported the finding that caps, masks and gloves could protect anonymity. Officials' concerns about struggles, communication and recruitment were considered; disagreement with the credited evidence establishes no clear error. The absence of demonstrated identification followed by retaliation is relevant but does not require officials to wait for an attack. Avoiding an appearance of brutality is not a legitimate reason to conceal the process. Witnesses have no equivalent means to observe the hidden acts, and the supported accommodation costs do not justify this blanket response. This contextual conclusion answers the dissent's security argument without making protective garb mandatory in every setting. Because the restriction fails the officials' preferred standard, choosing a stronger general closure standard is unnecessary. A materially changed threat would require a supported, fitted response on its own facts.

## Precedent Treatment

- Richmond Newspapers, Inc. v. Virginia, 448 U.S. 555, Press-Enterprise Co. v. Superior Court, and El Vocero de Puerto Rico v. Puerto Rico, 508 U.S. 147, are extended through their history-and-function rationale to observation of the continuous execution.
- Pell v. Procunier, 417 U.S. 817, and Houchins v. KQED, Inc., 438 U.S. 1, retain their limits on general prison access; admitted execution witnesses present a distinct protected activity.
- United Reporting, 528 U.S. 32, supplies the distinction between a general information demand and independently protected access or expression.
- Turner v. Safley, 482 U.S. 78, and Shaw v. Murphy, 532 U.S. 223, are applied as contextual penological review, preserving deference without making unsupported assertions conclusive.

## Law After Decision

Qualified access now reaches integral preparation within an execution already open to admitted witnesses. The challenged categorical curtain is invalid under Turner on the adjudicated record, while lawful attendance, conduct and anonymity controls continue. The Court need not select a more demanding general closure standard to resolve this restriction.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela joins the opinion and would additionally apply ordinary qualified-closure safeguards. Officials would have to establish an overriding interest likely to be prejudiced, narrow fit, consideration of reasonable alternatives and adequate findings. He would presume correction of the proved blanket practice for all admitted witnesses it governs unless officials established a narrower complete cure, with an effective viewing protocol and enforceable compliance.

O'Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would reverse. She regards the access precedents' adjudicative setting as insufficient to establish a right to observe prison preparations. Independently, she would sustain officials' anticipatory security judgment under Turner: workable protective garb does not establish a least-restrictive-means obligation or require acceptance of its operational costs in place of the curtain. The dissent distinguishes desirable public policy from a constitutional entitlement and would require any separate state-law claim to proceed through a permissible remedial channel.

## Procedure After Action

The injunction remains in force for covered executions. Officials must provide the required continuous observation while retaining lawful controls and anonymity measures. A materially changed security threat may be presented through the decree's modification process; the state-law alternative is unnecessary to the federal judgment.

## Source Notes

California First Amendment Coalition v. Woodford, 299 F.3d 868; Procedure 770; California Penal Code §3605. The decree followed a two-day bench trial and requires observation from chamber entry through death. The supported operational findings receive clear-error review.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Cartwright-v-Barnes_merits_2003-02-25.md
Effective date: 2003-02-25


## Event

Cartwright v. Barnes; judgment below, 304 F.3d 1138. October Term 2002. Decided February 25, 2003, after full merits submission. The Court reviews dismissal of the challenge to Georgia's congressional petition requirement of signatures equal to five percent of the registered voters eligible to vote in the preceding election in the relevant electorate. The questions separately concern an added Article I qualification and the combined practical burden on First and Fourteenth Amendment ballot access.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms dismissal of both constitutional theories.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Additional congressional qualification | Dismissal affirmed, 9–0. | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — |
| First and Fourteenth Amendment access burden | Dismissal affirmed, 5–4. | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, who would vacate this component and remand. |

## Opinion Topology

Thomas delivers the opinion of the Court.

| Writing or part | Joins | Relationship to judgment |
|---|---|---|
| Thomas, Part I: qualifications | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Unanimous controlling qualifications holding. |
| Thomas, Part II: practical access | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Breyer | Five-Justice controlling access holding. |
| Souter, concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stevens, Ginsburg | Joins Part I; would vacate and remand Part II. |
| Stone-Zsela, separately concurring in part and dissenting in part | No joins | Joins Part I; would vacate and remand Part II with his stated remedial conditions. |

## Holdings

### A genuine support petition is not an added personal qualification

**Holding and operative rule:** A genuine requirement to demonstrate electoral support regulates ballot access rather than adding a personal qualification for congressional membership, while remaining subject to independent First and Fourteenth Amendment review. Georgia's pleaded petition requirement measures support rather than age, status, prior service or another personal characteristic, so the Article I theory fails.

**Authority:** Thomas's Part I, joined by all eight other Justices; nine direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the rule's operation, not its ballot label, determines its classification. Thornton/Bryant prohibits States from adding personal congressional qualifications indirectly through ballot restrictions while distinguishing ordinary administration. Cook rejects ballot handicaps designed to penalize a candidate's refusal of a state-selected legislative position. This petition requires a showing of support and imposes neither kind of personal or policy penalty. Its procedural classification leaves the asserted associational burden to separate review.

### The accepted allegations do not materially distinguish the complete system sustained in Jenness

**Holding and operative rule:** Under Anderson–Burdick, courts weigh the character and magnitude of the actual burden against the State's precise interests and the need for its restrictions; severe burdens require narrow tailoring to compelling interests, while reasonable nondiscriminatory burdens ordinarily may be justified by important regulatory interests. Accepting the complaint's facts, this six-month, broad-signer, sheet-affidavit petition system and its alleged district-identification, surplus-signature and qualification-history burdens do not materially distinguish Jenness sufficiently to state the asserted constitutional access claim.

**Authority:** Thomas's Part II, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Breyer; five direct votes control this application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the complete system remains the relevant unit of analysis. Jenness sustained a five-percent demonstration of support within Georgia's open petition arrangements; Burdick and Timmons preserve contextual review rather than strict scrutiny of every election rule. Candidates have about six months to circulate, and voters may sign multiple petitions and participate in primaries. Verification requires an affidavit for the circulator's sheet, not notarization of every signature. Those features remain material when the pleaded district-identification difficulties and surplus needs are considered together.

The Court accepts the allegation that no Libertarian congressional candidate qualified by petition since 1943. That history is relevant, but the fact of nonqualification does not itself identify a changed legal or operational barrier in the support route. The added allegations concern identification and verification costs within the same basic open system; combined with the accepted history, they do not make this complaint materially different from the system Jenness sustained. Georgia's interests in a modicum of support and reliable verification justify this application. The dissent would require development before that conclusion; the Court finds the pleaded distinctions legally insufficient. The holding neither creates a universal five-percent safe harbor nor independently resolves the statewide one-percent rule.

## Precedent Treatment

- U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill (May 22, 1995) is applied to distinguish genuine support procedures from personal congressional qualifications.
- Cook v. Gralike, 531 U.S. 510, retains its prohibition on official policy-compliance penalties; this support petition imposes a different kind of condition.
- Jenness v. Fortson, 403 U.S. 431, is applied to the materially comparable complete Georgia petition system rather than treated as approval of a percentage in isolation.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780, Burdick v. Takushi, 504 U.S. 428, and Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party (April 28, 1997) retain the calibrated burden, precise-interest and fit framework; this decision applies it without replacing it with rational-basis review.

## Law After Decision

Congressional support petitions remain distinct from personal qualifications and remain subject to meaningful access review. The particular allegations here do not state a materially different constitutional barrier from the open system sustained in Jenness. Materially different or severe burdens continue to receive the scrutiny Anderson–Burdick requires.

## Separate Writings

Souter, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg, agrees on qualifications but would remand the access claim. Jenness's system-specific holding does not answer the cumulative allegations of district identification, affidavits, surplus needs and prolonged exclusion. The formal access opportunities favor Georgia, but factual examination should determine actual severity and justification.

Stone-Zsela also joins the qualifications holding and would remand access. He requires examination of the complete system before its burden is settled. If an unjustified common barrier is proved, he would require a workable lawful nomination route with relief tied to that barrier and the real election timetable, presuming correction for governed candidates and voters unless a narrower complete cure is demonstrated. His proposed remedy would begin with a state proposal and proceed to a fitted judicial order if necessary; it would not select a replacement percentage at the pleading stage.

## Procedure After Action

The mandate leaves the dismissal of both claims in force. The challenged congressional petition requirements remain operative.

## Source Notes

Cartwright v. Barnes, 304 F.3d 1138; O.C.G.A. §21-2-170. At this pleading stage, the alleged qualification history is accepted rather than adjudicated. The affidavit requirement applies to the circulator's sheet, not each signer's notarization.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Harvey-v-Horan_merits_2003-02-25.md
Effective date: 2003-02-25


## Event

Harvey v. Horan; Fourth Circuit No. 01-6703; 278 F.3d 370 and 285 F.3d 298. October Term 2002. Decided February 25, 2003, after full merits submission. The Court reviews the appellate dismissal of a prisoner's DNA-evidence-access action. The presented questions concern the §1983 channel and a constitutional testing-access right, but intervening state-ordered testing requires determination of the continuing federal controversy first.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court vacates the reviewed appellate merits judgment and remands for a precise continuing-controversy determination.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Vacated and remanded, 9–0. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. The remand does not automatically restore the district court's evidence-access order. The court must compare the actual federal request with the state testing order, samples, access conditions and completed testing, identify any preserved redressable request, and make claim-specific findings. If no effectual relief remains, it must dismiss for mootness and separately determine the equitable treatment of the remaining judgments. If a live request remains, adjudication resumes on that identified claim and its available remedies.

## Opinion Topology

O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

## Holdings

### Actual remaining relief must precede the access and channel merits

**Holding and operative rule:** When testing ordered and completed before review may have satisfied the pleaded evidence-access request, a court must determine what effectual relief remains before deciding either a constitutional access right or the proper remedial channel. Completed testing does not itself establish satisfaction of every requested sample or condition, and disagreement with a test result does not itself establish a continuing refusal; this record requires factual remand rather than either an assumed live controversy or immediate mootness dismissal.

**Authority:** O'Connor's unanimous opinion; all nine Justices join the complete rule and remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that judicial power depends on the actual remaining request. Spencer requires a concrete continuing injury, and Cavanaugh distinguishes an outstanding opportunity for meaningful relief from completed compliance.
