Arizonans permits correction of a jurisdictional problem without deciding abstract constitutional merits and keeps judgment clearing equitable.

The March 2002 state order and May testing occurred before this review. They must be compared with the federal complaint and access order. The certificate establishes that testing occurred, not that every requested sample, form of access or condition was supplied. The September state challenge shows disagreement with the results but does not itself preserve a federal refusal. The importance of innocence evidence supplies neither missing injury nor proof of complete satisfaction.

On remand, the court must examine the pleadings, state order and laboratory compliance record and permit the parties to identify the concrete unmet request. Harvey must identify relief capable of redressing his asserted injury; an assertion of complete satisfaction must be supported rather than inferred from an adverse result. The §1983-channel and constitutional-access questions remain undecided because continuing merits jurisdiction is not established. Any surviving claim must be identified before its channel, entitlement, preclusion or prior-default defenses are adjudicated.

## Precedent Treatment

- Spencer v. Kemna (March 3, 1998) is applied for a continuing personal injury redressable in the actual action.
- Cavanaugh v. Roller (November 30, 1993) is applied for examination of meaningful outstanding relief rather than presumed completion.
- Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona (March 3, 1997) supplies corrective jurisdiction and equitable judgment clearing without hypothetical constitutional-merits jurisdiction.
- Abdur'Rahman v. Bell (December 10, 2002) retains its distinction between procedural and renewed merits claims; its Rule 60 holding supplies no exception to Article III and no reopening of Harvey's 1995 judgment.

## Law After Decision

The continuing-controversy rules require a claim-specific comparison between the relief requested and the testing actually provided. The remand applies those existing rules. The merits of a constitutional DNA-access right and the channel for this access action remain open until a live preserved request is established.

## Separate Writings

## Procedure After Action

The lower courts must determine residual redressable injury and follow the conditional mandate. Complete satisfaction requires jurisdictional dismissal with separate equitable treatment of prior judgments; an established live request proceeds to its own channel and entitlement adjudication. Contesting a result alone does not require retesting.

## Source Notes

Harvey v. Horan, 278 F.3d 370; rehearing proceedings, 285 F.3d 298; Fourth Circuit No. 01-6703. The state testing order was entered March 1, 2002, the certificate was filed May 15, and a separate state challenge followed September 24. Those events do not establish the exact correspondence between the completed testing and every request in the federal action.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Miller-El-v-Cockrell_merits_2003-02-25.md
Effective date: 2003-02-25


## Event

Miller-El v. Cockrell; No. 01-7662. October Term 2002. Argued October 16, 2002; decided February 25, 2003. The Court reviews the Fifth Circuit's denial of a certificate of appealability after denial of federal habeas relief. The question is whether the proper certificate standard permits appellate debate about Miller-El's Batson jury-selection claim under the applicable habeas restrictions.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the certificate denial and directs issuance of a certificate on the Batson claim.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Reversed and remanded, 8–1. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. **Opposing:** Thomas. The Fifth Circuit must issue the issue-specific certificate and permit the authorized appeal. The conviction and sentence remain intact pending ordinary proceedings.

## Opinion Topology

Ginsburg delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer. Scalia concurs separately while joining the complete opinion. Thomas dissents.

## Holdings

### Certification tests reasonable debate under the actual habeas framework

**Holding and operative rule:** After a district court rejects constitutional merits, §2253(c) requires a substantial showing that reasonable jurists could debate its assessment under the applicable habeas restrictions, exceeding mere nonfrivolousness but not requiring proof of entitlement to a writ. Sections 2254(d)(2) and (e)(1) remain distinct: the certificate inquiry asks whether the district court's application is debatable, rather than requiring legal or factual unreasonableness to be conclusively proved by clear and convincing evidence before an appeal.

**Authority:** Ginsburg's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer; eight complete joins control both holdings.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that certification precedes final adjudication. Hohn permits review of the judicial certificate denial; Slack requires an issue-specific substantial constitutional showing rather than ultimate success. Williams keeps objective unreasonableness distinct from ordinary disagreement and preserves each factual-review provision's function. At this stage the court considers those statutory restrictions when asking whether the district court's treatment permits reasonable debate. A claim indisputably barred by a governing habeas provision does not qualify merely because its abstract constitutional premise is arguable.

Thomas would require completed clear-and-convincing rebuttal of the state finding before certification. That construction makes final resolution of the disputed factual question a prerequisite to the appeal that would review it. The Court instead asks whether the district court's assessment under the applicable factual provisions is reasonably debatable, preserving those provisions for the merits appeal. Early and Visciotti require attention to the actual state reasoning and whole supported record, not automatic relief for imperfect wording. They do not collapse Slack's threshold into a final merits judgment. Abdur'Rahman's bar on review of a different authorization act does not govern this judicial certificate denial.

### The cumulative jury-selection evidence warrants the Batson certificate

**Holding and operative rule:** Miller-El's combined strike pattern, comparative-juror evidence, disparate questioning, jury shuffles and historical office-practice evidence make the denial of his Batson claim reasonably debatable under the applicable habeas framework. He is entitled to a certificate identifying that claim and to appellate adjudication under the distinct governing statutory standards.

**Authority:** The same eight-Justice opinion controls.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the evidence must be considered together. Prosecutors struck ten of eleven eligible African-American venire members. The comparisons, questioning, shuffles and office history add context to that pattern. Batson and Franklyn/Gonzalez distinguish the production of neutral explanations from the ultimate inquiry into purposeful discrimination; the parties' acceptance of the first two stages leaves the latter disputed.

The State's death-penalty and criminal-history explanations, substantive juror differences and credibility findings receive their proper statutory consideration. They are substantial responses, but do not establish that no reasonable jurist could debate the district court's assessment of the combined evidence. Numbers create neither a quota nor a conclusive presumption; office history does not itself assign present intent, and a delayed hearing does not automatically eliminate deference. The relevant non-demeanor evidence nevertheless permits meaningful examination of the credited explanations. The claim therefore warrants the appeal rather than a remand merely to recite the standard. Clearly established substantive law remains measured at the state merits adjudication; the authorized appeal must apply that law and the independent habeas restrictions to determine ultimate entitlement.

## Precedent Treatment

- Hohn v. United States, 524 U.S. 236, and Slack v. McDaniel, 529 U.S. 473, are applied for reviewability, issue-specific certification and the reasonable-debate threshold.
- Williams v. Taylor, 529 U.S. 362, retains the separation of objective statutory unreasonableness and factual presumptions; this decision clarifies their role at certification.
- Batson v. Kentucky, 476 U.S. 79, and United States v. Franklyn / United States v. Gonzalez preserve the actual purposeful-discrimination inquiry after neutral explanations, with the ultimate persuasion burden unchanged.
- Early v. Packer and Woodford v. Visciotti (November 4, 2002) supply contextual statutory review within the certificate inquiry rather than a requirement to win the merits first.
- Abdur'Rahman v. Bell (December 10, 2002) retains its actual authorization-review bar; a judicial certificate denial is a distinct reviewable act.

## Law After Decision

Certificate review requires meaningful preliminary assessment of the whole claim under applicable habeas restrictions. The factual provisions retain their separate functions without becoming a merged clear-and-convincing requirement of final success before appeal. This cumulative record establishes the right to a Batson appeal; ultimate discrimination and habeas relief remain for that appeal.

## Separate Writings

Scalia joins the opinion and emphasizes that statutory eligibility must be debatable as well as the underlying constitutional claim. He regards the State's individualized reasons and substantial evidence as strong merits arguments even though the complete record warrants appellate consideration.

Thomas would affirm. He reads §2254(e)(1) to require actual clear-and-convincing rebuttal at the certificate stage, distinguishing Slack's context. He finds the historical and shuffle evidence insufficient to show pretext in these particular strikes and requires comparisons to account for the prosecutors' combined reasons. He rejects reducing the factual presumption merely because the Batson hearing followed voir dire by two years. His additional doubt under the majority's test does not replace that distinct statutory ground.

## Procedure After Action

The Fifth Circuit must issue the Batson certificate and permit briefing and decision of that appeal, applying the governing substantive-law cutoff and distinct habeas standards. Ultimate purposeful discrimination and entitlement to a writ remain for those proceedings.

## Source Notes

No. 01-7662; 28 U.S.C. §§2253(c), 2254(d)(2), 2254(e)(1). The parties accept the prima facie showing and production of neutral reasons; credibility and purposeful discrimination remain disputed. The state findings and the full comparative and historical evidence inform the certificate inquiry.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Washington-State-DSHS-v-Guardianship-Estate-of-Keffeler_merits_2003-02-25.md
Effective date: 2003-02-25


## Event

Washington State Department of Social and Health Services v. Guardianship Estate of Keffeler; No. 01-1420. October Term 2002. Argued December 3, 2002; decided February 25, 2003. The Court reviews the Washington Supreme Court's ruling that an appointed state representative payee's use of a child's benefits for current foster-care maintenance constitutes prohibited legal process under 42 U.S.C. §407(a).

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the categorical §407 ruling and remands the resulting relief for treatment consistent with the surviving properly presented claims.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Reversed and remanded, 9–0. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. The remand court must identify the basis of each injunction provision, restitution amount and fee component. Relief resting solely on the rejected §407 theory must be set aside; genuinely surviving, properly presented independent grounds and their authorized remedial consequences remain for adjudication.

## Opinion Topology

Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Stone-Zsela concurs separately while joining the complete opinion.

## Holdings

### Authorized current-maintenance expenditure is not the prohibited collection process

**Holding and operative rule:** A duly appointed representative payee's authorized use of a beneficiary's funds for current maintenance is not execution, levy, attachment, garnishment or comparable legal process merely because the State provides the care and records reimbursement. The residual process term concerns a judicial or quasi-judicial mechanism, not necessarily elaborate, transferring control of property to discharge or secure an asserted liability; the described practice asserts no enforceable foster-care debt against the child and compels no transfer from another holder.

**Authority:** Stevens's unanimous opinion; all nine Justices join the rule, its qualifications and bounded remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that lawful spending of funds already held for a child differs from compulsory collection against the child. The enumerated mechanisms give the residual process term its meaning. Congress separately authorized representative payees to use benefits for the beneficiary's current maintenance. Appointment and expenditure under that authority do not become collection process merely because reimbursement reduces the State's own costs or the State already owes a duty to supply care.

Philpott and Bennett protect benefits against governmental process reaching them; that protection remains intact. The children correctly identify the possibility that a custodian's reimbursement interest may conflict with a particular child's needs. That concern must be tested under the applicable appointment, notice, beneficiary-interest, misuse and conservation duties, rather than used to classify every authorized maintenance payment as §407 process. The construction defines §407(a)'s reach; it does not imply an exemption from that section contrary to §407(b)'s express-reference requirement. Anticipation and budgeting for expected payments likewise do not themselves assign the child's future rights. The conclusion is conditional on the authorized use described, not a finding of compliance in every account.

**Operative qualifications:** The holding concerns the ordinary current-maintenance benefits described. Current maintenance is distinct from collection of an earlier debt. Applicable payee rules permit payment of a debt predating the benefit period only when current and reasonably foreseeable needs are met and payment serves the beneficiary's interest; they do not require it. Remaining funds must be conserved or invested under the governing rules.

## Precedent Treatment

- Philpott v. Essex County Welfare Board, 409 U.S. 413, and Bennett v. Arkansas, 485 U.S. 395, are distinguished as State resort to collection process reaching protected benefits; their antiattachment protection remains in force.
- Hartford Underwriters Insurance Co. v. Union Planters Bank, N.A., 530 U.S. 1, supplies attention to the enacted statutory act and conditions rather than a policy substitute.
- Blessing v. Freestone (April 21, 1997) preserves separate questions of a particular right, enforcement route, defendant and fitted relief for any properly presented alternative; it supplies no blanket approval of payee conduct or new §1983 right here.

## Law After Decision

Section 407 does not categorically prohibit an appointed state payee's authorized current-maintenance accounting. Beneficiary ownership and separate appointment, notice, individual-needs, accounting and conservation duties continue to govern. A claimant must identify prohibited process or establish a distinct preserved violation under its own source and enforcement conditions.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela joins the opinion and would require source-specific treatment of every surviving ground and remedy. A statutory-right claim must establish its individual entitlement and available enforcement route; a constitutional notice claim requires its own entitlement and process analysis. If a common unlawful practice is proved, he would presume correction for those it governs unless the Department demonstrates a narrower complete cure, with an accounting demonstrating compliance. That proposal depends on an actual violation and proper claimant group, and does not justify repayment of lawful maintenance merely because the State was payee.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for treatment of the injunction, restitution and fees under the surviving properly presented claims, including the unresolved notice-related ground. Standalone §405(j), appointment or misuse claims outside the granted and preserved issues require their own appropriate lawful forum and preservation; the remand does not create new claims.

## Source Notes

No. 01-1420; 42 U.S.C. §§407(a)–(b), 405(j), 1383(a)(2), 1383(d)(1). The described practice involves current maintenance, no enforceable debt against the child and no seizure from another holder. The categorical judgment did not resolve every child's appointment, notice or expenditure compliance.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Scheidler-v-National-Organization-for-Women-Inc_merits_2003-02-26.md
Effective date: 2003-02-26


## Event

Scheidler v. National Organization for Women, Inc.; consolidated Nos. 01-1118 and 01-1119. October Term 2002. Argued December 4, 2002; decided February 26, 2003. The Court reviews the Seventh Circuit's affirmance of civil RICO liability and a nationwide injunction arising from clinic blockades, threats and other interference. The granted questions concern obtaining property under the extortion predicates and private RICO equitable authority. The United States supports the business-control extortion theory but opposes private RICO injunctions on a separate statutory ground.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the reviewed RICO judgment and vacates its associated treble damages and nationwide injunction in both consolidated dockets.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** RICO judgment reversed and dependent damages and injunction vacated, 8–1. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. **Opposing:** Stevens, who sustains business-control acquisition and dissents from the announced predicate-failure basis for eliminating the judgment and relief. The remand is for implementation: enter the corrected judgment, remove dependent monetary liability and dissolve the RICO restraint. Any implementation issue concerning relief already collected belongs in the proper remedial proceeding.

## Opinion Topology

Scalia delivers the opinion of the Court.

| Part or writing | Joins | Authority or judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|
| Parts I, II and IV: acquisition, related predicates and consequential relief | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Eight-Justice controlling opinion. |
| Part III: physical violence in furtherance of a qualifying plan | O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Seven-Justice controlling opinion. Stone-Zsela concurs in the disposition rejecting the four findings as an independent basis for this judgment, without joining this rationale or adding a ground. |
| Ginsburg concurrence | Breyer | Both join the complete Court opinion. |
| Stevens dissent | No joins | Opposes the acquisition holding and resulting RICO reversal. |

## Holdings

### I. Extortion requires acquisition as well as deprivation

**Holding and operative rule:** Hobbs Act extortion through wrongful force, violence or fear requires obtaining another's property, not merely coercing the owner to stop exercising it; attempted extortion must likewise seek the required acquisition. Intangible property, temporary acquisition and noncommercial use can qualify without profit or formal title, but the clinic-shutdown and service-interference theory here identifies compelled inactivity rather than property acquired by defendants or another person.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; eight direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that what the victim loses and what the defendant obtains are separate statutory questions. Enmons and the distinction between extortion and coercion preserve the acquisition requirement. The earlier NOW decision rejected an economic-motive requirement; it did not decide that these trial predicates satisfied every other element. Civil plaintiffs still must prove their invoked predicates by a preponderance.

Respondents and the Government characterize the coercive veto over clinic operations as acquisition of the owner's exclusive business-control right. Stevens accepts that theory. But its asserted acquisition consists entirely of the owner's forced cessation of business. Calling that deprivation transferred control would collapse extortion into general coercion. Carpenter involved actual appropriation of business information and does not erase the distinction. The Court excludes neither intangible interests generally nor ideologically motivated acquisition; the problem is the obtaining alleged here. The Government's qualified willingness to return the mixed liberty/property charge for examination does not cure this defect in the business-control theory itself, and respondents made no equivalent concession. The statutory failure resolves the extortion judgment without a new trial on deprivation alone.

### II. The related state, travel and conspiracy categories do not supply the missing acquisition

**Holding and operative rule:** State extortion invoked as a RICO predicate must satisfy the generic obtaining-property requirement, and attempts or agreements directed only to the same nonacquisitive coercion do not cure that failure. The actual Travel Act and attempted-travel predicates, characterized as furthering this extortion undertaking, fall with it; a broader instructional list is not an adjudicated independent unlawful-activity basis.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part II, joined by the same eight Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that different labels cannot substitute for the federal category's elements. Nardello supplies generic extortion analysis, and Taylor supports attention to the category rather than local nomenclature. State force, fear and value conditions do not supply acquisition absent from the undertaking. The travel charge mentioned extortion, arson and crimes of violence, but the reviewed predicates furthered extortion; the broader list alone establishes neither arson nor another qualifying unlawful activity. A crime of violence is not universally a substitute for the Travel Act's specified unlawful activity.

Salinas removes the requirement that every conspirator personally commit two predicates but retains knowing agreement toward a qualifying substantive undertaking. It cannot turn agreement to the rejected coercive-deprivation object into extortion. Nor does the direction to bypass separate conspiracy questions after Claim I liability supply an independent affirmative verdict. The existing judgment therefore lacks a surviving predicate merely by relabeling the same undertaking.

### III. The four physical-violence findings do not establish an independent Hobbs offense

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1951(a)'s physical-violence language requires violence or threats in furtherance of a plan or purpose to violate the section through robbery or extortion, including its inchoate forms; it does not independently federalize every act of violence affecting commerce. The four findings under interrogatory 4(e) cannot sustain this RICO judgment as freestanding Hobbs offenses after the extortion undertaking fails, and no distinct robbery undertaking is established here.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part III, joined by O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; seven direct joins. Stone-Zsela supports the disposition on this component without joining its rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statutory connection cannot be omitted. The four acts or threats were separately listed in the contemporaneous verdict and must be addressed. The instruction listed physical violence without connecting that category to a robbery or extortion plan. Its breadth cannot expand the enacted offense. The rejected acquisition theory therefore cannot be rescued merely by describing its acts as violence affecting commerce.

The verdict's combined pattern, commerce, causation and damage answers also do not segregate those four incidents into a separately sustainable recovery. The finding that extortion involved more than blockades or sit-ins concerns the extortion interrogatories, not independent validation of 4(e).

### IV. Failure of the liability basis removes the dependent relief

**Holding and operative rule:** Because the reviewed RICO judgment lacks the required predicates, its associated treble damages and nationwide injunction must be removed. General private equitable authority under §1964 remains undecided because failure of the underlying violation independently disposes of this decree.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; eight direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a remedy requires its own valid liability basis. The Government's structural objection to private injunctions and respondents' broader reading of §1964 need not be resolved once this RICO judgment fails. ACLA adjudicated different threatening conduct under its own authority and expressly left the RICO questions open. Its threat-meaning rule and independent intent findings cannot supply acquisition here. FACE likewise remains a separate source with its own conditions; this judgment cannot be converted into relief under that statute. Implementation removes the RICO restraint and monetary liability rather than authorizing a new search for hypothetical predicates. Other claims retain only the effect of their own judgments and lawful procedural existence.

## Precedent Treatment

- National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249, retains its no-economic-motive holding and independent element and remedy requirements; its pleading-stage decision did not establish acquisition at trial.
- United States v. Enmons, 410 U.S. 396, preserves the distinction between coercion and extortion requiring property acquisition.
- United States v. Nardello, 393 U.S. 286, and Taylor v. United States, 495 U.S. 575, supply generic-category analysis rather than reliance on state labels.
- Carpenter v. United States, 484 U.S. 19, recognizes genuinely appropriated intangible business property and does not dispense with acquisition.
- Salinas v. United States (December 2, 1997) preserves the qualifying substantive object despite dispensing with personal commission of two predicate acts.
- Planned Parenthood of the Columbia/Willamette v. ACLA (December 10, 2002) retains its source-specific threat rule and fitted remedy; neither establishes the statutory predicates or private RICO authority presented here.

## Law After Decision

Extortion requires acquisition, including acquisition of a genuinely obtainable intangible interest, rather than deprivation alone. Generic state extortion and dependent travel or conspiracy theories must satisfy that element. Section 1951(a)'s violence language remains tied to a qualifying robbery or extortion plan. The reviewed RICO damages and injunction fail; private RICO equitable authority remains open for a case requiring its decision.

## Separate Writings

Stevens dissents because exclusive control over business assets is itself valuable property. Coercion that forces surrender of that control can obtain it even when the actor seeks closure rather than profit or subsequent operation. He relies on Tropiano and Arena's treatment of business control, Carpenter's recognition of intangible property, and the established appellate construction he would leave to Congress to change. He accepts that the violence must further robbery or extortion, but finds a qualifying extortion undertaking here. He regards the acquisition as supported and opposes the Court's predicate-failure basis for eliminating the judgment and relief. His dissent supplies no separate affirmative holding on private equitable authority.

Ginsburg, joined by Breyer, concurs fully and emphasizes the consequences of a broader construction for protest activity subject to RICO's severe remedies. Congress's targeted FACE protection does not justify stretching extortion to cover every unlawful interference. Their distinction preserves liability for conduct that satisfies the actual statute and creates no immunity for violence or obstruction.

## Procedure After Action

The lower courts must conform the judgments to the reversal, remove the dependent treble-damages liability and dissolve the nationwide RICO injunction. The remand implements that result and does not order a new trial on the rejected deprivation-only theory.

## Source Notes

Nos. 01-1118 and 01-1119; 18 U.S.C. §§1951, 1952, 1961, 1962, 1964; petition appendix 146a–163a. Interrogatory 4(e) contains four acts or threats of physical violence; the combined findings do not separately allocate their pattern, causation or damages. The travel charge's broader list does not itself establish a nonextortion unlawful-activity finding. The Government's mixed-instruction remand position was qualified, and respondents defended their own property theories.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Steele-v-Industrial-Development-Board_merits_2003-03-03.md
Effective date: 2003-03-03


## Event

Steele v. Industrial Development Board of Metropolitan Government of Nashville and Davidson County. Plenary merits decision, March 3, 2003. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit, 301 F.3d 401, Nos. 00-6646 through 00-6649. The questions are whether these municipal taxpayers have standing, whether private capital and repayment exempt affirmative project-specific public bond financing from Establishment Clause review, and how actual financed use and safeguards determine relief.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 7–2.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Municipal fiscal standing | Standing sustained, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Proceed to the merits. |
| Institution-wide future-bond prohibition | Affirm removal, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The district decree cannot bar every future project because of religious institutional character. |
| Defendants’ categorical merits summary judgment | Vacated and remanded, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Apply actual financed function to supported uses and effective separation. |

Scalia and Thomas would vacate all merits adjudications and direct dismissal for lack of jurisdiction; their objection to the merits disposition is not support for either categorical merits endpoint. The remand must correct proved devotional financing, preserve lawful separated financing, and use established evidence without ordering its reproof. Interim protection remains available upon the ordinary equitable showing. Changes to existing obligations require consideration of applicable authority, existing contractual interests and nonparty bondholder rights.

## Opinion Topology

O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. The complete coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Scalia files a dissenting opinion, joined by Thomas.

## Holdings

### I. Supported municipal fiscal standing

**Holding and operative rule:** Municipal taxpayers may challenge this affirmative local financing decision where evidence establishes a probable effect on municipal revenue from foregone taxable alternative financing, the challenged transaction causes that effect, and prospective control of the defendants’ financing authority can redress it. Neither an exact loss calculation nor a municipal repayment guarantee is required; taxpayer disagreement with governmental policy or speculation about an alternative transaction is insufficient.

**Authority:** Part I of the Court’s opinion; the seven-Justice whole-opinion coalition stated in Opinion Topology directly joins this rule.

**Controlling explanation:** Frothingham preserves the direct municipal financial relationship that distinguishes a local taxpayer from a nationwide objector. Valley Forge requires a real injury rather than a desire to enforce the Constitution; Lujan and Laidlaw require supported causation and likely redress. The evidence here establishes that Lipscomb probably would have borrowed taxably for at least part of the expansion, with accreditation pressure explaining the library and student-center need. That concrete fiscal connection supports the lower court’s Hawley-based analysis. Private repayment answers whether the municipality guarantees the debt; it does not erase the separate probable revenue effect of the local financing choice. The Court does not infer that the entire project would proceed unchanged or that every tax exemption supplies standing. The supported fiscal ground resolves jurisdiction, so a broader rule based solely on public financing authority is unnecessary.

### II. Public bond assistance is governed by actual financed function

**Holding and operative rule:** Affirmative governmental issuance and tax qualification of bonds for identified institutional projects remain subject to the Establishment Clause even when private investors provide the capital and the borrower alone repays it. Financing demonstrated devotional work, including facilities functionally inseparable from that work, is impermissible; identifiable secular uses remain eligible on neutral terms when practical separation and enforceable controls protect them.

**Authority:** Part II, joined by the same seven Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini asks what governmental authority actually does. Jackson, Strout and Zelman reject financial routing as an automatic answer to demonstrated public support of integrated devotional instruction; Mitchell requires proof of the particular financed function and preserves lawful secular work. Here the government creates and approves the instrument that obtains the project’s borrowing advantage. The $15 million is private capital, not a municipal appropriation, but that distinction does not make affirmative project assistance disappear. Hunt illustrates restricted secular financing and leaves its distinct tax-exemption argument undecided. Walz and Mueller concern different general-tax and private-resource relationships.

The college’s religious character cannot establish every asset’s use. Conversely, evidence of devotional teaching must receive its actual force for the instructional facilities and infrastructure that support it. A contractual secular-use prohibition matters, but its words do not establish its observance. The lower court must identify actual use, workable allocation where functions are divisible, and effective controls proportionate to demonstrated risk. Mere fungibility, incidental savings and hypothetical diversion do not prove a violation. Separate incorporation, wholly distinct personnel and a fixed monitoring system are not invariably required. Genuinely independent private benefits retain their distinct protection; institutional borrowing does not by itself establish that relationship.

### III. Both categorical merits endpoints must give way to effective, confined relief

**Holding and operative rule:** Neither religious institutional classification nor the private-capital form resolves the constitutionality of every financed asset. Courts must use established evidence to prohibit proved unconstitutional financing, preserve independently lawful separated financing, and decide genuinely unresolved use or control questions before extending the decree to additional assets or altering existing obligations.

**Authority:** Part III, joined by the same seven Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Mitchell requires correction at the demonstrated scope rather than categorical condemnation or categorical immunity. The district court’s future-bond ban excluded lawful projects by institutional identity, while the appellate direction awarding defendants summary judgment foreclosed the necessary function inquiry. Removing both errors does not require taxpayers to prove established integrated uses again. Existing materials may supply the answer; additional proceedings serve only genuine remaining questions. The court must consider existing bondholders’ legal interests before changing contractual obligations and may preserve interim protection when ordinary equitable requirements are met. The decree must stop supported unlawful financing while permitting the legally separable remainder.

## Precedent Treatment

- Frothingham v. Mellon preserves the municipal financial relationship; the Court applies it to the supported probable local revenue effect of this financing decision.
- Valley Forge Christian College v. Americans United preserves the exclusion of abstract constitutional disagreement; this supported local fiscal injury is distinct.
- Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife supplies concrete injury, causation and redress requirements; none is displaced by taxpayer status.
- Friends of the Earth v. Laidlaw supports proof of likely redress without certainty; it does not itself create taxpayer standing.
- Agostini v. Felton supplies the unified governmental-authority-and-function inquiry, extended to the affirmative project-financing assistance here.
- Jackson v. Benson preserves actual instructional-function review despite routing through another decisionmaker.
- Strout v. Albanese preserves the distinction between financing integrated devotional work and genuinely separated secular service.
- Zelman v. Simmons-Harris preserves the requirement to connect the actual benefit to the instructional work and retain lawful separation.
- Mitchell v. Helms supplies actual-use proof, rejects institutional-character presumptions and requires correction at the demonstrated scope.
- Hunt v. McNair remains a restricted secular-project decision; it establishes no general conduit-financing immunity.
- Walz v. Tax Commission retains its broad property-tax relationship, distinct from affirmative approval of this identified project.
- Mueller v. Allen retains its distinct deduction and private-resource relationship.

## Law After Decision

Government-issued project bonds do not create a categorical escape from financed-function review. Lower courts must examine the supported uses and practical restrictions, preserving lawful secular financing and correcting proved devotional financing. Municipal standing here rests on a supported probable local fiscal effect; no general standing rule based on objection to public financing is adopted.

## Separate Writings

**Scalia, dissenting, joined by Thomas.** The dissent accepts the finding that some taxable borrowing probably would have occurred. Its objection is legal: Frothingham’s municipal exception concerns the taxpayer’s relation to municipal moneys, while this arrangement uses private capital, private repayment and no public guarantee. A probable tax on a different transaction does not create a proprietary interest in revenue never owed under the actual transaction. Under Lujan’s injury rule and the dissenters’ Laidlaw position, the action should be dismissed before reaching establishment. Their requested remedy is vacatur of the merits adjudications and jurisdictional dismissal.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns to the lower courts for use-specific application and a conforming decree. The categorical future-bond prohibition is removed, and the direction to enter categorical merits summary judgment for defendants is vacated. Existing proof controls the uses it establishes; remaining asset, control, contractual and equitable questions are decided under the Court’s rule.

## Source Notes

Judgment below: 301 F.3d 401; Sixth Circuit Nos. 00-6646 through 00-6649. Hawley v. City of Cleveland, 773 F.2d 736, supplies the lower court’s municipal lost-revenue analysis. The record establishes probable taxable alternative borrowing for part of the project, without quantifying a particular municipal tax loss.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Adland-v-Russ_merits_2003-03-04.md
Effective date: 2003-03-04


## Event

Adland v. Russ; review of 307 F.3d 471 (Sixth Circuit). Merits decision, March 4, 2003. The Court reviews the permanent injunction against Section 8 of Kentucky’s 2000 joint resolution directing relocation of a Ten Commandments monument to the Capitol grounds near the Floral Clock, with the resolution included in the display. The question is whether this proposed official presentation explains history or identifies civil authority with religious profession.

## Participation

Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated.

## Public Action

The judgment is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

**Opposing:** Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

The permanent injunction against implementation of the identified Section 8 proposal remains in force. Its reach is the placement and accompanying official presentation directed by that provision. A materially different historical presentation may be considered through the ordinary process governing compliance with or modification of the injunction.

## Opinion Topology

Breyer delivers the Opinion of the Court. Complete-opinion coalition, including the author: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Kennedy files a dissenting opinion, joined by Scalia and Thomas. The dissent agrees that the concrete proposal is fit for review but rejects the Court’s application of the governing Establishment Clause rule.

## Holdings

### Review of the authorized proposal

**Holding and operative rule.** People facing a supported, recurring personal encounter with a sufficiently definite, officially directed display may challenge that display before its installation. Where the monument, site, accompanying governmental text and proposed relationship to the surrounding material are identified, the constitutional controversy is fit for adjudication despite remaining implementation details.

**Authority.** Breyer’s Opinion of the Court, joined by the six-Justice complete-opinion coalition in Opinion Topology, directly controls this holding and the following holding.

**Controlling explanation.** Robinson requires personal contact rather than abstract disagreement, and Lujan requires a supported threatened injury. The individual plaintiffs regularly use the Capitol for political advocacy and would encounter the directed placement there. Texas and Ohio Forestry distinguish indefinite future action from an operative, sufficiently defined command. Section 8 identifies this monument, its location near the Floral Clock and inclusion of the resolution; Kentucky identifies seven surrounding memorials. The challenged presentation is therefore concrete. As Santa Fe illustrates, a court may evaluate an adopted governmental mechanism before its first completed religious performance. Possible later additions do not change the proposal presently directed and defended. The personal prospective encounter and defined official action permit resolution now.

### Historical explanation and official religious identification

**Holding and operative rule.** Government may preserve and explain religious artifacts, but it may not use its own official presentation to proclaim religious teachings as a foundation of present civil authority or commend their acceptance through governmental authority. The challenger must establish that prohibited function from the complete attributable presentation; Kentucky’s directed Section 8 arrangement does so and may be enjoined.

**Controlling explanation.** Agostini retains Lemon as one inquiry: specific prohibitions or distinct doctrines govern where applicable; established permissible relationships govern their own settings; otherwise attribution and function are assessed under ordinary procedure. Purpose, effects and institutional relationships supply evidence, not separate prongs. Robinson distinguishes official adoption from private origin and prohibits identifying present civil authority with faith. Santa Fe likewise examines the government’s actual undertaking.

Kentucky selects a prescriptive Decalogue monument with religious symbols and requires the accompanying resolution. The resolution praises Scripture and Christianity through attributed statements, describes the Commandments as the Commonwealth’s foundational legal code and frames the placement as recalling biblical foundations. The full combination uses present governmental authority to proclaim a religious foundation, bringing the proposal within Agostini’s prohibition on official religious adoption. Long storage of the 1971 donation also defeats the asserted premise of nearly three decades of continuous exhibition.

The State and dissent fairly invoke historical acknowledgment. The attributed quotations, seven civic memorials and much larger Floral Clock must all be considered. But the unrelated memorials do not turn the resolution’s legal-foundation proclamation and the prescriptive monument into an explanatory account of legal development. That conclusion rests on the complete directed arrangement, not one quotation, ownership, size, religious motive or a count of secular objects. A constitutional historical exhibit needs no prescribed curator’s format or exhaustive catalogue; its actual function must be explanation rather than adoption. Good News Club’s protection for independently chosen private expression concerns a different relationship. The injunction reaches this official proposal; the unchallenged school provision and a materially different historical exhibit lie outside the question presented.

## Precedent Treatment

- Robinson v. City of Edmond: applied for recurring-contact standing, official attribution and the distinction between historical references and identification of civil authority with faith; its specific civic-seal holding informs this contextual application without becoming a categorical monument rule.
- Lujan: applied for supported personal prospective injury.
- Texas v. United States and Ohio Forestry Association v. Sierra Club: applied to distinguish the concrete placement command from indefinite future implementation.
- Lemon, as modified by Agostini: applied as one governmental-authority-and-function inquiry; the former independent prongs remain superseded.
- Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe: applied to the adopted official operation and its availability for review before completed implementation.
- Good News Club v. Milford Central School: distinguished because private access differs from the State’s own selected presentation.

## Law After Decision

A government’s historical presentation of religious material is evaluated by its actual attribution and function under Agostini. An official display that proclaims religious teaching as the foundation of present civil authority crosses the constitutional line even when labeled historical and surrounded by secular memorials. The permanent restraint here governs Kentucky’s identified Section 8 arrangement; a different historical proposal is subject to the same functional inquiry.

## Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, agrees that the plaintiffs’ prospective encounter and the sufficiently defined proposal permit review. He also applies Agostini’s distinction between historical explanation and official religious adoption. The disagreement concerns the function of this arrangement. In the dissent’s view, the inherited artifact, attributed statements of historical figures and civic memorial setting commemorate the asserted contribution of religion to law rather than adopt the monument’s theology as present governmental truth. Public attribution settles whose presentation it is, but does not settle what it does. Robinson’s continuing civic-seal prohibition does not turn every official acknowledgment of religious influence into a profession of faith; neither that decision nor Agostini demands an academically complete legal-history exhibit. The religious language requires consideration, but the dissent reads the resolution as a historical account rather than an invitation to accept religious commands through current civil authority. Kennedy would dissolve the injunction against the described proposal. This reasoning does not depend on coercion as the exclusive Establishment Clause rule.

## Procedure After Action

The permanent injunction remains enforceable when the mandate issues. Officials must refrain from implementing the enjoined Section 8 placement and framing. Any compliance or modification proceeding must assess the actual governmental presentation, including its location and explanatory text, under the controlling functional rule.

## Source Notes

Adland v. Russ, 307 F.3d 471; Kentucky Senate Joint Resolution No. 57 (2000), particularly Sections 7 and 8. The proposal directs the monument’s relocation and inclusion of the resolution; the reviewed controversy concerns that proposed presentation.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Boeing-Co-v-United-States_merits_2003-03-04.md
Effective date: 2003-03-04


## Event

Boeing Co. v. United States, Nos. 01-1209 and 01-1382, 537 U.S. 437. Merits decision, March 4, 2003. Argued December 9, 2002. On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 258 F.3d 958.

The principal question is whether Treasury's research-expense allocation rule validly governs combined taxable income under the DISC provisions for 1979–1984 and the FSC provisions for 1985–1987, despite Boeing's use of airplane product-line accounting. The conditional cross-petition seeks corresponding reversal of Boeing Sales Corporation's favorable judgment only if Boeing prevails on that allocation question.

Boeing allocated approximately $3.6 billion in company-sponsored research by airplane program, including programs with no current sales. The IRS applied the broader transportation-equipment category. The Ninth Circuit reversed the district court's refund judgment for Boeing. Boeing seeks restoration of refunds under its product-line theory; the United States defends the regulation and the judgments resulting from its application.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the judgments in both consolidated dockets.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Docket and component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No. 01-1209: challenged research allocation | Affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Treasury's applicable allocation rule governs; Boeing's product-line refund theory is rejected. |
| No. 01-1382: conditional companion consequence | Affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Boeing Sales Corporation's favorable judgment stands because the condition for changing it does not occur. |

## Opinion Topology

Kennedy delivers the signed Opinion of the Court.

| Writing | Author | Coalition | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Complete-opinion coalition: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Full agreement with both holdings, both judgments, and their implementation. |
| Dissent | Scalia | Scalia; joined by Thomas | Would reverse both dockets and require consistent computations under the export-specific grouping and attribution rule. |

## Holdings

### Delegated research-expense allocation

**Holding and operative rule:** For the DISC and FSC computations governing Boeing's 1979–1987 tax years, Treasury may allocate currently deducted research expenses across the relevant two-digit transportation-equipment category under Treasury Regulation §1.861-8(e)(3). The court first identifies actual statutory authority and enforces its limits; within that authority, the regulation reasonably treats research as related to the broader technological category it can support, including when an individual program produces no current receipts.

**Authority:** The opinion of the Court, supported in full by the seven-member complete-opinion coalition stated in Opinion Topology. Each member adopts the authority inquiry, category rule, application, and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Code's allocation structure, including §§861 and 863, and Treasury's implementing authority under §7805(a) authorize rules for allocating the deductions used in combined taxable income. Sections 994(a)(2) and 925(a)(2) tie that income to export receipts, but do not require every research deduction to follow only the airplane program to which the company assigned it. Haggar requires an actual delegation and respect for statutory boundaries before a formal regulation receives controlling treatment. Mead confirms that agency expertise or official status alone does not supply that authority; MCI requires enforcement of an enacted limit before deference. Those requirements are satisfied here by the allocation scheme and the regulation issued to implement it.

Research is speculative. Work on a program that yields no current sales can produce knowledge, methods, and experience useful to other transportation products. Treasury therefore reasonably identifies the broader product category as the class of income to which these research deductions relate. Boeing's project accounting does not establish a statutory requirement of exclusive project tracing. The specific marginal-costing grants in §§994(b)(2) and 925(b)(2) concern establishing or maintaining export markets; the Court's authority analysis rests on the general allocation and implementing scheme, with those specific grants confined to their terms.

### Export grouping and the incorporated expense rule

**Holding and operative rule:** A qualifying choice to group export transactions by product or product line for the DISC or FSC pricing methods does not displace the research-expense allocation rule incorporated into the calculation of combined taxable income. Boeing's claimed refunds based on exclusive airplane-program allocation therefore fail, and the condition for changing the companion judgment is absent.

**Authority:** The same complete-opinion coalition adopts this holding in full as a necessary part of the Court's allocation ruling.

**Controlling explanation:** The DISC rule, §1.994-1(c)(6)(iii), requires allocation consistent with §1.861-8; the FSC counterpart, §1.925(a)-1T(c)(6)(iii)(D), likewise incorporates expense allocation. The grouping provisions do a separate job: they identify transactions to which an administrative pricing method will be applied. The DISC provision making an accepted grouping controlling and the distinct FSC grouping provisions retain that operation while the incorporated research rule determines the expenses entering the computation.

The dissent reads the specific grouping choice as controlling the allocation of costs as well as receipts. That reading would deprive the express research-allocation cross-reference of its assigned work. A taxpayer's accepted pricing group remains meaningful without becoming an election to treat potentially shared research as legally unrelated to the rest of its product category. Section 174 permits current deduction of research costs, but does not prescribe the income class that bears them for this calculation. United Dominion requires the computation supplied by the actual governing regulations. Here that computation includes a promulgated research rule expressly incorporated into the export system. The validity of that rule, rather than a new finding of abusive accounting, defeats the refund theory.

## Precedent Treatment

- United States v. Haggar Apparel Co.: applies its requirement of actual delegated authority and statutory boundaries before deference to a reasonable formal regulation.
- United States v. Mead Corp., 533 U.S. 218: applies its distinction between delegated interpretive lawmaking and administrative expertise or official status alone.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T, 512 U.S. 218: preserves statutory limits on administrative power; the allocation rule operates within the authority identified here.
- Christensen v. Harris County, 529 U.S. 576: distinguishes its nonbinding advisory interpretation from this operative, incorporated regulation.
- United Dominion Industries, Inc. v. United States, 532 U.S. 822: applies its insistence on the computation supplied by the governing scheme; the expense rule here forms part of that scheme.

## Law After Decision

The governing DISC and FSC provisions permit Treasury's research-specific allocation across the applicable transportation-equipment category for these tax years. The taxpayer's accepted transaction grouping determines how the pricing alternatives are applied, while the incorporated research rule supplies the expense allocation. The delegation-first method remains the governing review rule; this decision adds the answer to the particular grouping and allocation conflict.

## Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, dissents. The export-specific regulations make an accepted product grouping controlling, and the dissent reads that choice as governing cost attribution as well as the pricing method. Applying the broad research category first strips the more specific grouping rule of the effect it grants the taxpayer. Possible incidental benefits from research cannot replace the relationship required by that rule.

The dissent also rejects the suggestion that research costs allocated to a program without current receipts necessarily disclose an impermissible omission. Section 174 allows immediate deduction of speculative research, and expense-allocation systems can place costs in classes that presently produce no income. The legal question is which class the operative rules select. On that source-based ground, the dissent would reverse both judgments and require consistent tax and refund calculations. It confines its objection to the export regime's specific rules.

## Procedure After Action

The Ninth Circuit's judgments stand. Boeing's demand for restoration of its refund on the rejected product-line allocation theory is denied, and Boeing Sales Corporation's favorable companion judgment remains intact.

## Source Notes

Nos. 01-1209 and 01-1382; 537 U.S. 437; 258 F.3d 958. Internal Revenue Code §§174, 861, 863, 925, 994, and 7805(a); Treasury Regulations §§1.861-8(e)(3), 1.994-1(c)(6)(iii)–(iv), 1.994-1(c)(7), and 1.925(a)-1T(c)(6)(iii)(D), with the applicable DISC and FSC grouping provisions. The two-digit classification governs the years at issue.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Clay-v-United-States_merits_2003-03-04.md
Effective date: 2003-03-04


## Event

Clay v. United States, No. 01-1500, 537 U.S. 522. Merits decision, March 4, 2003. Argued January 13, 2003. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, 30 Fed. Appx. 607.

The question is whether, after affirmance of a federal conviction and no petition for certiorari, §2255 finality occurs when the appellate mandate issues or when the certiorari period expires. The Seventh Circuit affirmed the dismissal of Clay's motion as untimely using the mandate date. Clay seeks consideration of his collateral motion; the United States agrees with the certiorari-expiration rule, and appointed amicus defends the judgment.

Clay's arson and drug convictions were affirmed on November 23, 1998. The mandate issued December 15, 1998. His time to seek certiorari expired February 22, 1999, and he filed his initial §2255 motion on February 22, 2000. The limitations ruling prevented adjudication of his collateral claims.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the Seventh Circuit and remands for further proceedings on Clay’s motion.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

Clay's motion is timely under the ordinary final-judgment trigger. The mandate-based limitations dismissal is reversed.

## Opinion Topology

Ginsburg delivers the signed Opinion of the Court. Complete-opinion coalition: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. All join the complete finality rule, its application, and the judgment.

## Holdings

### Finality includes the available certiorari period

**Holding and operative rule:** When a federal conviction is affirmed on direct appeal and the defendant does not petition for certiorari, the judgment becomes final for §2255's ordinary one-year limitation when the time to seek certiorari expires. The appellate mandate does not start that clock while ordinary Supreme Court review remains available. Clay's February 22, 2000 motion was timely because his certiorari period expired on February 22, 1999.

**Authority:** The unanimous opinion of the Court; every member of the complete-opinion coalition in Opinion Topology adopts the rule, statutory explanation, application, and reversal with remand.

**Controlling explanation:** Finality in the ordinary collateral-review setting includes the end of direct review and the expiration of the time available to seek it. Griffith, Barefoot, and Johnson supply that established usage. The mandate performs a different function by returning authority to the lower court. Supreme Court Rule 13 measures the ordinary certiorari period from the judgment, not the mandate, so that administrative return cannot extinguish the remaining opportunity for direct review.

The appointed amicus relies on the contrast with §2244(d)(1)(A), which expressly mentions completion of direct review or expiration of the time to seek it. Section 2255 instead uses the unqualified term final. That difference does not select the mandate as an earlier event. Unlike a provision that specifies one event while omitting another, §2255 leaves the familiar legal concept intact. The fuller state-prisoner formulation also supplies a uniform federal measure across differing state usages. Russello's recognition that textual differences may matter does not make this difference decisive without its statutory context. Lindh likewise teaches attention to the particular comparison, not an automatic rule for every omission.

The Court therefore gives §2255 the ordinary direct-review meaning of finality. The rule follows from the statute and review framework independently of the Government's agreement. The Seventh Circuit used the wrong starting event; the district court must proceed with Clay's motion under the corrected calculation and the remaining applicable requirements.

## Precedent Treatment

- Griffith v. Kentucky, 479 U.S. 314: applies its ordinary account of finality after direct review ends or the certiorari opportunity expires.
- Barefoot v. Estelle, 463 U.S. 880, and United States v. Johnson, 457 U.S. 537: apply their established collateral-review usage including the expiration of available direct review.
- Russello v. United States, 464 U.S. 16: preserves attention to meaningful statutory differences while rejecting the inference that §2255's unqualified finality term selects the mandate.
- Lindh v. Murphy: applies contextual comparison and distinguishes its separate temporal question; its construction of AEDPA's application to identified pre-enactment cases remains intact.

## Law After Decision

For federal convictions affirmed on direct appeal without a certiorari petition, the ordinary §2255 one-year period runs from expiration of the time to seek certiorari. Courts must use that lawful expiration date in the statutory calculation. The rule resolves the finality trigger that produced the dismissal here and governs comparable pending disputes under that trigger.

## Separate Writings

None.

## Procedure After Action

The district court must proceed to Clay's unresolved collateral grounds under the now-settled timeliness of his motion.

## Source Notes

No. 01-1500; 537 U.S. 522; 30 Fed. Appx. 607. 28 U.S.C. §2255, paragraph 6(1), and §2244(d)(1)(A); Supreme Court Rule 13. The dates establish an ordinary certiorari-period calculation.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/McClendon-v-City-of-Columbia_merits_2003-03-04.md
Effective date: 2003-03-04


## Event

McClendon v. City of Columbia; judgment below, 305 F.3d 314. October Term 2002. Decided March 4, 2003, after full merits submission. The Court reviews summary judgment for Detective Carney and the City's separate judgment. The questions concern a July 1993 police-held gun transfer to an informant who later shot McClendon, affirmative-danger liability, and the sources capable of giving qualified-immunity notice.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms Carney's judgment on qualified immunity and preserves the City's independent judgment.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Carney's individual damages claim | Affirmed on qualified immunity, 8–1. | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, who would vacate and remand. |
| City's separate judgment | Preserved, 9–0. | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — |

## Opinion Topology

Breyer delivers the opinion of the Court.

| Part or writing | Joins | Authority or relationship |
|---|---|---|
| Part I: notice method | All eight other Justices | Unanimous controlling proposition. |
| Part II: July 1993 immunity application | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Eight-Justice controlling application. |
| Part III: City disposition | All eight other Justices | Unanimous independent disposition. |
| Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part | No joins | Joins Parts I and III; rejects Part II and would remand the individual claim. |

## Holdings

### I. Relevant persuasive law and obvious applications can provide fair warning

**Holding and operative rule:** Qualified immunity depends on whether relevant law existing when the official acted made the particular federal unlawfulness apparent to a reasonable official. Neither a Supreme-Court-or-controlling-circuit-only rule nor a factual-twin prerequisite is permissible; actual authority, consistency, context, contrary decisions and obvious applications govern, rather than a numerical circuit tally.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part I, joined by all eight other Justices; nine direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the source and application inquiries must both be performed. Harlow and Anderson require objective, particularized notice. Lanier permits relevant pre-existing lower-court authority and obviously covered applications; Hope preserves that principle while requiring the specific context to matter. Pinder and Uhlrig likewise require all relevant contemporaneous law rather than a search for identical facts. Later opinions may explain this method but cannot supply warning absent in July 1993. The reviewed en banc court allowed sufficiently strong persuasive authority; the Court does not attribute a contrary categorical rule to it.

### II. The particular gun transfer was not clearly established as unconstitutional in July 1993

**Holding and operative rule:** On the supported plaintiff-favorable account, pre-July-1993 law did not make sufficiently apparent that Carney's affirmative transfer into this escalating dispute constituted his own substantive-due-process deprivation. He therefore receives personal qualified immunity on this damages claim without a ruling adopting the lower court's alternative constitutional merits ground.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part II, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the transfer's affirmative character matters but does not by itself establish the required historical clarity. DeShaney rejects a general duty to protect against private violence while leaving distinct official creation of vulnerability for its proper analysis. Reed involved removal of a sober driver while leaving an intoxicated person with the keys; Freeman permitted an amended claim of official interference with otherwise available protection. Their particular control and causal settings do not establish a general rule resolving this gun transfer. Other contemporary cases differed in official conduct and culpability, including deliberate-indifference and gross-negligence formulations.

Stone-Zsela regards supplying the instrumentality of the known danger as an obvious application despite those differences. The Court considers the escalating conflict and affirmative transfer favorably to McClendon, but finds the causal and culpability application insufficiently settled at the time. This record does not establish the more definite premise of knowing instrumental aid to an intended assault. That distinction does not impose a universal purpose-to-harm standard or resolve disputed knowledge against McClendon. The asserted protective explanation is not adopted as a finding of reasonable conduct. Later Lewis, Saenz and custody cases retain their own boundaries and supply no retroactive warning. Because immunity independently resolves the individual claim and Saucier imposes no controlling merits-first sequence, the broader substantive theory need not be decided.

### III. Municipal responsibility remains separate

**Holding and operative rule:** Carney's personal immunity neither establishes nor defeats municipal liability; the City requires its own federal-deprivation, policy or custom, attribution and causation predicates. Its independent judgment remains because no preserved policy, final-policymaker or other demonstrated basis for disturbing that judgment is established in this review.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part III, joined by all eight other Justices; nine direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an employee's individual transfer is not itself municipal responsibility. Monell requires the City's own attributable policy or custom, and Collins keeps that inquiry distinct from the underlying deprivation. Gibson preserves the same source-specific separation. McClendon's challenge supplies no independent preserved basis for disturbing the City's judgment, so it stands on its own ground rather than borrowing Carney's immunity.

## Precedent Treatment

- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800, and Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635, retain objective conduct-date and particularized-notice requirements.
- Pinder v. Johnson (May 21, 1996), Uhlrig v. Harder (February 18, 1997), Lanier (March 31, 1997) and Hope v. Pelzer (June 27, 2002) supply the present notice method without retroactively establishing July 1993 law.
- DeShaney v. Winnebago County, 489 U.S. 189, retains its no-general-protection rule without converting every affirmative act into an omission.
- County of Sacramento v. Lewis (May 26, 1998) retains its context-sensitive culpability boundary; Saucier v. Katz (June 18, 2001) supplies no controlling compulsory merits-first sequence.
- Monell v. Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658, Collins v. City of Harker Heights (February 26, 1992), and Gibson v. County of Washoe (January 14, 2003) preserve independent municipal deprivation, attribution and causation requirements.

## Law After Decision

Qualified-immunity review considers relevant persuasive law and obvious applications without a factual-twin requirement. The particular July 1993 transfer receives personal immunity under that method. The Court adopts no general substantive rule foreclosing affirmative-danger claims, and the City's independent responsibility remains governed by its own elements.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela agrees on the notice method and City judgment but would remand the individual claim. He would recognize liability for affirmative creation or material increase of a specific grave danger when an official actually appreciates and consciously disregards it despite time and practical opportunity to avoid it, thereby causing injury. The favorable account of furnishing this weapon permits that finding and, in his view, makes the wrong apparent under preconduct authority despite the differing instruments and settings. He would require proof of knowledge, culpable causal contribution and compensable injury; Carney could contest those facts and the protective explanation, with immunity reconsidered if actual findings materially differed. His proposed rule creates neither negligence liability nor municipal responsibility from the individual act.

## Procedure After Action

The individual damages judgment is final on qualified immunity, and the City's separate judgment remains in force. No merits remand follows from leaving the broader constitutional theory undecided.

## Source Notes

McClendon v. City of Columbia, 305 F.3d 314; July 1993 conduct. The summary-judgment account is viewed favorably to McClendon without resolving credibility or converting the asserted protective purpose into a finding. Reed v. Gardner, 986 F.2d 1122, and Freeman v. Ferguson, 911 F.2d 52, concern distinct preconduct causal settings.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Moseley-v-V-Secret-Catalogue-Inc_merits_2003-03-04.md
Effective date: 2003-03-04


## Event

Moseley v. V Secret Catalogue, Inc.; No. 01-1015. October Term 2002. Argued November 12, 2002; decided March 4, 2003. The Court reviews the Sixth Circuit's affirmance of a dilution injunction against Victor's Little Secret. The question is whether the operative 1995 Federal Trademark Dilution Act requires actual impairment, what proof can establish it, and whether this summary-judgment record suffices.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the dilution summary judgment, vacates its dependent injunction and remands.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Reversed and remanded, 9–0. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. The restraint based on the deficient dilution judgment must cease upon implementation of the mandate. The rejected infringement claim remains undisturbed. Further proceedings must apply the actual-lessening requirement and ordinary proof rules.

## Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Kennedy concurs separately while joining the complete opinion.

## Holdings

### Actual dilution is required, with ordinary direct or circumstantial proof

**Holding and operative rule:** The operative Federal Trademark Dilution Act requires actual lessening of a famous mark's capacity to identify and distinguish goods or services, proved by a preponderance; a claimant seeking summary judgment must establish that injury without a genuine material dispute. Reliable direct or circumstantial evidence may suffice without lost sales, a mandatory survey or identical marks, but fame, mental association and offense directed at the junior seller do not themselves establish the required impairment.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion; all nine Justices join the rule, record application and remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the enacted cause and definition require actual statutory injury. The Act's causation language and definition of dilution describe lessening of identifying capacity, while its injunction provision supplies a remedy rather than a different likelihood-based threshold. Qualitex explains the source-identification function of trademark law but does not decide the separate injury Congress required for dilution. Ordinary infringement's confusion inquiry cannot replace that element.

Actual lessening is distinct from later financial consequences. A claimant need not wait for lost sales or prove destruction of the mark, and circumstantial evidence may demonstrate the impairment without a survey in every case. Nonidentical marks may dilute; identity alone does not create automatic liability. This preserves timely protection without rewriting actual causation as mere likelihood.

Here the officer associated the junior name with Victoria's Secret but continued to recognize the famous mark, and his offense concerned the junior store. The expert's general brand discussion supplied no demonstrated erosion of identifying capacity.
