Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at merits submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms vacatur of the preliminary injunction and remands.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Judgment:** Affirmed, 9–0. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. The preliminary injunction remains vacated; Vermont may enforce the challenged local-sale requirement while litigation proceeds. A supported final challenge or renewed request based on materially different evidence remains subject to the applicable procedural and proof requirements. This interlocutory judgment does not finally resolve every application of the law.

## Opinion Topology

Kennedy delivers the opinion of the Court.

| Part | Controlling subject | Justices joining the reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| I | Bounded factual-disclosure rule and application | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer (8) |
| II | Territorial reach of the local-sale command | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer (9) |
| III | Pike review and failure to establish likely success | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer (7) |

Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment and joins Parts II and III. Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in part and in the judgment; they join Parts I and II and agree with the Part III judgment on an assumed-Pike basis.

## Holdings

### I. Truthful product and disposal facts may serve a substantial health purpose

**Holding and operative rule:** A State may require truthful, noncontroversial information about a commercial product's mercury content and lawful disposal when the disclosure is reasonably related to a substantial health or environmental purpose and is neither unjustified nor unduly burdensome. Separate proof of consumer deception is unnecessary in this product-and-hazard setting; the conceded content, connection and burden shown here do not establish likely success on the speech challenge.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part I, joined by the eight Associates identified above, controls the disclosure rule and application. Stone-Zsela agrees with the judgment on separate grounds.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that useful facts about a purchased product may be required for its safe handling and disposal. Zauderer distinguishes justified factual disclosure from suppression of commercial speech while preserving objections to unjustified or unduly burdensome demands. Its reason for treating disclosure differently supports this defined extension beyond deception. Ibanez requires attention to the actual justification and burden rather than accepting an official characterization.

Mercury content is not apparent to purchasers, and the disposal information connects the covered product to lawful handling. Accuracy and commercial character are conceded. The State's substantial health and environmental purpose and the information's direct relation to it support the requirement; the present burdens do not show likely constitutional invalidity. Requiring deception as an indispensable additional condition would exclude useful product facts serving the same informational function.

United Foods concerns compelled financing of private promotion and expressly reserves factual disclosure and distinct health or safety purposes. It neither invalidates this label nor exempts all commercial mandates from scrutiny. The message's actual content, commercial connection, justification and burden govern. Disputed ideology does not become noncontroversial product information merely because government calls it factual. These conditions resolve the request for interim relief while preserving review of a materially different supported application.

### II. A local-sale requirement does not become an outside command through production choices

**Holding and operative rule:** A labeling rule governing goods sold or distributed for use in Vermont does not directly regulate independent transactions outside Vermont merely because manufacturers prefer nationwide compliance to segregating local goods. The rule's actual legal command, rather than that production preference alone, determines whether it directly controls outside commerce.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part II is unanimous.

**Controlling explanation:** Healy and Brown-Forman address direct outside-price control. Vermont's rule commands labels for its own market and does not condition access to that market on labels placed on independent outside sales. Manufacturers may experience costs elsewhere and may choose a uniform label, but those economic effects do not themselves extend Vermont's legal command to outside transactions. This distinction answers the direct-control claim without making the costs irrelevant. The asserted burdens are addressed separately in Part III.

### III. Evenhanded regulation remains subject to Pike review

**Holding and operative rule:** Pike review does not require a preliminary showing that a burden falls disparately on interstate commerce: a challenger to an evenhanded local measure must substantiate the interstate burden and show it clearly excessive in relation to the measure's putative local benefits. The identified production and distribution costs and speculative incompatible labels do not establish likely success under that standard on this preliminary record.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part III is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, forming a seven-Justice majority. Scalia and Thomas agree only with the judgment on this component.

**Controlling explanation:** Pike addresses burdens of evenhanded regulation. A categorical disparate-burden threshold would remove the inquiry precisely where that rule calls for one. The Court therefore rejects that threshold in the appellate reasoning while affirming its disposition.

The asserted costs matter, but they must be assessed against the disposal information's concrete local benefit. A firm's preference for uniform national production does not itself establish clear excess, and no actual conflicting sister-state requirement is shown. The present evidence thus fails to establish likely success warranting interim restraint. A supported record of conflict or disruption would require assessment of the actual burden rather than speculation or a categorical exemption. RCRA's preservation of state environmental authority supplies no automatic congressional authorization of an otherwise unconstitutional interstate burden; the judgment rests on the ordinary constitutional analysis.

## Precedent Treatment

- Zauderer v. Office of Disciplinary Counsel, 471 U.S. 626, is extended to the defined truthful product-content and lawful-disposal setting without requiring separate deception proof; its justification and undue-burden limits remain operative.
- Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation preserves actual justification and burden review; the State's description of a mandate does not settle its character or validity.
- United States v. United Foods, Inc., 533 U.S. 405, is distinguished as a private promotional-financing case whose express factual-disclosure and health-purpose reservation leaves this issue open.
- Healy v. Beer Institute, 491 U.S. 324, and Brown-Forman Distillers Corp. v. New York State Liquor Authority retain their direct outside-control rule; local compliance costs and a voluntary nationwide production choice do not alone satisfy it.
- Pike v. Bruce Church, Inc., 397 U.S. 137, governs evenhanded interstate burdens without a categorical disparate-burden prerequisite. Its clear-excess standard, applied to actual costs and putative benefits, defeats the present likelihood-of-success showing.

## Law After Decision

The bounded disclosure rule reaches truthful, noncontroversial mercury-content and lawful-disposal facts serving a substantial health or environmental purpose, with reasonable relationship, justification and burden limits. The legal reach of a local-sale command and its interstate practical burdens remain separate inquiries. Evenhandedness does not foreclose Pike review. The current record permits enforcement while the litigation continues.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela joins Parts II and III and concurs in the disclosure judgment. His disclosure rule requires a legitimate health or environmental objective rather than the Court's substantial-purpose threshold. The State must identify that objective and a reasonable connection; the challenger must substantiate burden or misleading operation and establish entitlement to interim relief. Accurate, nonmisleading purchased-product and disposal facts qualify without deception proof, subject to justification and undue-burden limits. Actual content and function distinguish those facts from ideological affirmation, misleading presentation of disputed propositions or unrelated advocacy. Zauderer supports the extension; United Foods, Barnette and Wooley address different compulsion. Vermont's requirement satisfies his rule on the present evidence.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, fully joins Parts I and II. Assuming Pike governs, they agree that the asserted costs and speculative incompatible labels fail to establish likely success. They withhold assent from Part III's affirmative balancing rationale while supporting the same interim disposition.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns with the preliminary restraint vacated. Vermont may enforce the challenged requirement. A renewed interim request must present materially different supporting evidence satisfying the ordinary requirements; repetition that uniform national production is cheaper is insufficient. Remaining final proceedings must apply the controlling disclosure conditions and commerce rules to the actual record, and any proved violation must receive relief fitted to the offending requirement.

## Source Notes

National Electrical Manufacturers Association v. Sorrell, 272 F.3d 104; 72 F. Supp. 2d 449; Vt. Stat. Ann. tit. 10, §6621d; 42 U.S.C. §6929. The required information's accuracy and commercial character are conceded. The legal command concerns goods sold or distributed for use in Vermont, and the preliminary record establishes no actual conflicting sister-state labeling mandate.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/A-Womans-Choice-v-Newman_merits_2003-03-24.md
Effective date: 2003-03-24


## Event

A Woman's Choice–East Side Women's Clinic v. Newman. Plenary merits decision, March 24, 2003. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, 305 F.3d 684, No. 01-2107, reversing the permanent injunction reported at 132 F. Supp. 2d 1150. The questions concern Indiana’s compulsory in-person communication of advance abortion information, review of trial and comparative evidence before enforcement, and preservation of separable informed-consent duties.

The statute’s medical-emergency exception excuses its informed-consent conditions. As construed by Indiana’s Supreme Court, it applies when the attending physician, exercising good-faith clinical judgment concerning the patient’s life and physical and mental health, finds pregnancy complications that make prompt therapeutic abortion medically necessary to address a significant threat. Severe but temporary conditions for which abortion is not medically necessary are excluded. The challenged added encounter burdens patients who remain subject to attendance after that exception operates.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

Reversed and remanded for restoration and implementation of the limited in-person-delivery injunction, 6–3.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

Restore and implement the permanent injunction against compulsory in-person delivery of the covered advance information. The independently remaining eighteen-hour interval is outside this reviewed injunction component; leaving it in place does not decide its constitutionality. Accurate material information, qualified communication, genuine clinical assessment and voluntary informed consent remain. A receipt certificate or dependent penalty cannot reinstate the enjoined physical-presence condition.

## Opinion Topology

Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. The complete coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Kennedy files a dissenting opinion. Scalia files a dissenting opinion, joined by Thomas.

## Holdings

### I. The complete standard and ordinary review rules govern prospective proof

**Holding and operative rule:** A compulsory abortion-information delivery condition is governed by the complete Casey framework, including meaningful-interference review even when no substantial obstacle is proved. Supported historical trial findings receive Rule 52(a) protection, while constitutional sufficiency and application are independently reviewed; reliable comparative experience may establish prospective injury when material similarities and differences are considered.

**Authority:** Part I of the Court’s opinion; the six-Justice whole-opinion coalition identified in Opinion Topology joins this rule in full.

**Controlling explanation:** Casey requires identification of the legal duty and baseline, actual function and purpose, attributable interference and affected population, the corresponding substantive protection, clinical judgment and truthful communication, and fitted relief. Challengers prove duty, causation, affected population and triggering interference. Baseline violations, objectively proved obstructive purpose and substantial obstacles independently fail; other severe interference requires compelling justification and narrow tailoring. Remaining meaningful interference requires substantiated permissible reasons and fit, including an answer to an identified concrete, practicable, substantially less burdensome alternative. Below meaningful interference that heightened access inquiry ends, while independent constitutional requirements remain.

Fargo and Mazurek preserve this complete inquiry. Neither Salerno’s universal-invalidity condition nor a fixed numerical fraction displaces it. A court need not await completed local injuries, demand a national study or prescribe one regression method. Comparative evidence is not automatically conclusive either: geography, provider availability, income, transportation and clinical practice can affect transferability. Respect for underlying trial findings does not convert a contested prediction into an established Indiana effect. The Court resolves this case on the separate concrete extra-visit burden, without resolving the disputed projected percentage.

### II. This additional physical encounter lacks sufficient justification

**Holding and operative rule:** When compulsory prior physical attendance adds meaningful travel, work, childcare, scheduling or similar burdens beyond communicating accurate information, the State must substantiate the added encounter’s permissible function and answer a concrete, practicable, substantially less burdensome alternative adequately serving that function. Indiana’s uniform advance-presence requirement fails that inquiry on this record because remote communication followed by clinical examination and consent verification at the procedure visit adequately addresses the stated functions, and the State has not identified a material deficiency.

**Authority:** Part II, joined by the same six Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Casey distinguishes useful information from burdensome timing and delivery; Akron’s protection against unsupported attendance restrictions survives at that scope. Indiana may provide accurate information favoring childbirth, and legislative opposition to abortion alone proves no obstructive purpose. But patients who otherwise need only the procedure visit must make a concrete additional encounter. That added burden exists without assuming every projected decline in abortions reflects prevention rather than persuasion, travel elsewhere or voluntary choice.

The State identifies personal communication, nonverbal responses, misunderstanding and coercion. Those are legitimate concerns, but generalized claims do not explain why reliable advance remote communication, opportunities for questions and later individualized clinical review cannot adequately address them. The performing clinician remains responsible for actual consent and may delay care when consent is unresolved. The alternative therefore preserves clinical responsibility while removing an unsupported uniform trip. Reasonable experience and predictions may substantiate regulation; no new study or numerical benefit calculation is invariably required. Courts may not select a preferred policy once justification and fit are shown. Here that necessary answer is absent. Different restrictions supported by actual clinical need remain governed by the same rule, not a universal entitlement to remote medical treatment.

### III. The delivery-only injunction supplies complete relief on this appeal

**Holding and operative rule:** The compulsory in-person delivery condition and enforcement dependent on it must remain enjoined, while legally separable accurate disclosure and genuine consent duties remain enforceable. The independent eighteen-hour interval is outside this appeal’s reviewed injunction component and is not sustained by the Court’s limited judgment.

**Authority:** Part III, joined by the same six Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Casey’s remedy principle removes the obligation that fails and preserves lawful requirements. The existing decree does precisely that: it prevents the unnecessary advance physical encounter without requiring a procedure or a clinically necessary consultation to occur remotely, or forbidding an in-person visit requested by the patient. Lawful receipt certification may document information actually supplied; it cannot enforce physical attendance after that obligation is enjoined. The established meaningful-interference record is sufficient, so restoration does not require a new trial directed to the disputed statewide percentage. Any preserved challenge to the interval must receive the governing analysis in its proper posture.

## Precedent Treatment

- Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey supplies the complete framework and separates lawful accurate content from unsupported timing and delivery burdens; its clinical and remedial qualifications remain.
- Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer confirms that absence of a substantial obstacle does not end review of meaningful interference.
- Mazurek v. Armstrong preserves concrete-burden proof and the full corresponding justification inquiry.
- Akron v. Akron Center for Reproductive Health retains its protection against unsupported waiting and delivery restrictions at the scope preserved by Casey, without barring accurate permissible information.
- United States v. Salerno does not impose universal invalidity or completed local injury as prerequisites to this Casey-governed relief; its treatment in other constitutional fields is unchanged.

## Law After Decision

Comparable compulsory delivery restrictions require justification of the added physical encounter against its established burden and concrete adequate alternatives. Accurate information and lawful persuasion do not themselves justify an unnecessary trip. Prospective proof remains available under ordinary evidentiary rules, without a universal causal percentage or demand that patients first lose access. Indiana’s separate interval receives no constitutional approval from this judgment.

## Separate Writings

**Kennedy, dissenting.** He retains protected choice but rejects the residual justification rule adopted in Casey and applied here. In his view, personal communication and an opportunity to observe misunderstanding are reasonably related to informed consent. Comparative abortion declines do not establish the number of women prevented from obtaining care, and the record does not justify overriding this reasonable delivery arrangement under his approach. He would affirm reversal of the injunction. He neither treats every decline as voluntary persuasion nor requires completed local injury in every prospective case.

**Scalia, dissenting, joined by Thomas.** They retain their Casey, Miller, Voinovich and Stenberg disagreement with the substantive constitutional abortion right. They would sustain this reasonable informed-consent regulation and affirm reversal of the injunction on that distinct foundation. Their position does not deny the extra burdens, replace supported trial findings or attribute the Court’s earlier contrary rule to a different Casey decision. It supplies no controlling law for lower courts.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for restoration and implementation of the delivery-only permanent injunction. The decree preserves the remaining duties at their existing scope and bars enforcement that would recreate compulsory advance physical attendance. No additional evidentiary trial is required to establish the already sufficient meaningful-interference ground.

## Source Notes

305 F.3d 684, Seventh Circuit No. 01-2107; 132 F. Supp. 2d 1150. Indiana Code §16-34-2-1.1; A Woman’s Choice–East Side Women’s Clinic v. Newman, 671 N.E.2d 104, 108–111 (Ind. 1996), construing the medical-emergency exception; Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 52(a). The projected ten-to-thirteen-percent reduction is not a finding that all such procedures were prevented; the judgment does not depend on that inference.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Commack-Self-Service-Kosher-Meats-v-Weiss_merits_2003-03-24.md
Effective date: 2003-03-24


## Event

Commack Self-Service Kosher Meats, Inc. v. Weiss. Plenary merits decision, March 24, 2003. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit, 294 F.3d 415, affirming 106 F. Supp. 2d 445. The questions concern New York’s enforcement of Orthodox religious requirements as the civil definition of kosher status, compulsory religious authorization, the effect of fraudulent-intent requirements, and the proper scope of relief.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

Affirmed, 9–0.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The declaration and injunction remain effective against the challenged Orthodox-standard mechanism in Agriculture and Markets Law §§201-a, 201-b(1), 201-c, 201-e(2-a) and (3-c), 201-f, 201-h and 26-a, at the existing decree’s scope. Removing only the rabbinic-washing language would not cure the remaining religious definition and enforcement structure. Unchallenged provisions and ordinary fraud, sanitation, factual labeling and certification-identification rules remain subject to their own lawful terms.

## Opinion Topology

Breyer delivers the unanimous opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.

## Holdings

### I. Civil enforcement cannot make religious orthodoxy government’s rule

**Holding and operative rule:** Government may not adopt a religious denomination’s account of orthodoxy as its coercively enforceable civil standard or determine religious compliance as governmental truth. It may enforce objectively verifiable representations about actual preparation or a named private certification, provided officials neither decide theological correctness nor give the certifier compulsory civil control.

**Authority:** Part I of the unanimous Court opinion; all nine Justices directly join the rule and its qualifications.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini forbids government from governing theology or adopting doctrine as public truth; Brown distinguishes private religious activity from official religious action. Kiryas Joel and Larkin preserve the limits on religious exercise of civil power, while Ballard separates factual representations from adjudication of religious truth. The challenged provisions make Orthodox religious requirements the State’s own standard for lawful commerce, and officials apply religious law to decide violations. This is more than expert evidence about a seller’s factual claim.

Consumer protection remains legitimate. A representation of certification by an identified organization can be tested against whether certification occurred; a representation of a specified preparation step can be tested against the act. But calling doctrine a trade standard does not remove its religious function. Requiring fraudulent intent limits culpability without changing the substantive religious command. Consumer preference cannot justify that command, and no conversion or additional coercion finding is needed once the governmental religious function is established.

### II. The washing condition makes religious authorization a civil requirement

**Holding and operative rule:** A civil regulatory condition that requires performance or authorization by a religious office gives religious authority impermissible civil force when that office supplies the governing religious judgment. Section 201-f’s challenged washing condition has that character; private expertise about facts remains permissible without transferring governing civil authority.

**Authority:** Part II of the unanimous Court opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 201-f(1) concerns meat or poultry represented as prepared under Orthodox Hebrew religious requirements that was not soaked and salted immediately after slaughter at the slaughter premises. Paragraph (1)(b) requires washing under those requirements within seventy-two hours of slaughter and in every subsequent seventy-two-hour period, by a duly ordained Orthodox rabbi or a person he authorizes. Section 201-f(3)(b) exempts liver from that washing requirement. These qualifications define the duty; they do not change its delegation of the relevant civil permission to religious authority. Larkin’s civil-power limitation applies to this regulatory condition, and Kiryas Joel’s denominational boundary does not depend on the subject being territorial government. The Court decides the civil allocation of authority, not the religious correctness of washing or any other practice.

### III. Effective relief reaches the religious-standard mechanism and preserves factual regulation

**Holding and operative rule:** The declaration and injunction cover the enumerated challenged provisions insofar as they compose the State’s Orthodox-standard enforcement mechanism. Severing only the rabbinic-washing condition cannot preserve the remaining official religious standard; unchallenged duties and independently lawful factual consumer protection retain their force.

**Authority:** Part III of the unanimous Court opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** The State’s proposed narrow severance leaves officials deciding Orthodox compliance through inspection and penalties. Because that governing function remains throughout the challenged mechanism, the existing decree provides the necessary effective relief. It does not immunize commercial falsehood or prohibit private certifiers from defining their own standards. Nor does it decide a new disclosure statute. The establishment ground fully resolves the challenged obligations and requested relief, so the independent free-exercise, vagueness, as-applied enforcement and prepublication theories require no additional constitutional holding.

## Precedent Treatment

- Agostini v. Felton supplies the unified governmental-authority inquiry and its prohibition on adopting doctrine as public truth; no former independent Lemon prong is revived.
- Brown v. Gilmore preserves the boundary between private religion and government religious activity, applied here to official enforcement.
- Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet supplies the civil-authority and denominational limits, applied to regulatory authority without combining its distinct earlier opinion coalitions.
- Larkin v. Grendel’s Den supplies the prohibition on a religious body’s civil veto, applied to mandatory religious authorization in commerce.
- United States v. Ballard preserves the difference between factual representation and governmental determination of religious truth.

## Law After Decision

Civil consumer protection may police truthful attribution, actual practices and material factual misrepresentation. It may not make religious orthodoxy the civil rule or delegate compulsory civil permission to religious office. This rule governs analogous religious-standard regulation; the injunction remains confined to the challenged New York mechanism and the parties bound by the decree.

## Separate Writings



## Procedure After Action

The Second Circuit’s judgment is affirmed. The existing declaration and injunction continue at their established scope; independently lawful and unchallenged statutory duties are not invalidated by association.

## Source Notes

294 F.3d 415; 106 F. Supp. 2d 445. New York Agriculture and Markets Law §§201-a, 201-b(1), 201-c, 201-e(2-a) and (3-c), 201-f, 201-h and 26-a. Section 201-f(3)(b)’s liver exception concerns the recurrent washing duty in §201-f(1)(b).


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Planned-Parenthood-of-Southern-Arizona-v-Lawall_merits_2003-04-28.md
Effective date: 2003-04-28


## Event

Planned Parenthood of Southern Arizona v. Lawall. Plenary merits decision, April 28, 2003. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 307 F.3d 783, affirming 189 F. Supp. 2d 975, district action No. CV-00-0386-RCC. Review concerns the confidentiality of necessary court handling of judicial-bypass information and the distinct statutory exception permitting outside-agency access under other law or rule.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

Rejection of the challenge to necessary confidential internal handling is affirmed, 9–0; rejection of the outside-agency-access claims is vacated and remanded, 6–3.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Necessary confidential internal handling | Rejection of the challenge affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Necessary confidential processing remains permissible. |
| Outside-agency-access confidentiality and informational-privacy claims | Rejection vacated and remanded, 6–3 | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Thomas | Determine the actual authorizing law, permissible audience and purpose, and safeguards before resolving these claims. |

Stone-Zsela would reverse approval of outside access lacking function-specific justification and remand for an injunction against that access. Scalia and Thomas would affirm rejection of both claims. The three therefore do not support a common alternative. The majority requires relief against established improper access, preservation of a usable bypass and necessary confidential processing, and state-law severability analysis for any invalid permission. The lower court must address surviving interim-protection requests under ordinary equitable standards and these governing rules.

## Opinion Topology

Ginsburg delivers the opinion of the Court, with the joins specified below. Kennedy files an opinion concurring in part. Stone-Zsela files an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part. Scalia files an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Thomas.

| Writing or part | Author | Direct coalition, including author | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Part I, necessary confidential administration | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer fully; Scalia and Thomas join only the bounded permission for necessary confidential handling | All nine support rejection of the internal-handling claim; Scalia and Thomas do not adopt Part I’s complete bypass rationale. |
| Part II, outside-access privacy and legal-scope rule | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Seven direct rationale joins. |
| Part III, complete reproductive-liberty inquiry | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Six direct rationale joins. |
| Part IV, partial vacatur and remand | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Six direct rationale joins. |
| Partial concurrence | Kennedy | Kennedy | Full I, II and IV joins; no III residual-tier join. |
| Partial concurrence and partial dissent | Stone-Zsela | Stone-Zsela | Full I–III joins; would order protection against access lacking sufficient legal justification. |
| Partial concurrence and partial dissent | Scalia | Scalia, Thomas | Bounded I permission and internal-claim result; would affirm rejection of outside-access claims. |

## Holdings

### I. Confidential adjudication permits necessary internal handling

**Holding and operative rule:** Judges, necessary court personnel and supervised professionals may handle bypass information confidentially for the proceeding and its lawful administration. Reasonable protection against identification is required, not an impossible guarantee against every unauthorized act; fictitious names, confidential records and enforceable nondisclosure duties matter, but a fictitious name alone does not protect every identifying fact.

**Authority:** Part I. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer join the complete rationale. Scalia and Thomas additionally join the bounded permission for necessary confidential internal handling and the rejection of that claim, without adopting the broader rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Bellotti and Ohio v. Akron require a confidential and usable bypass, not a proceeding unknown to the officials who must decide and administer it. Casey preserves confidential records and independent authorization, and Lambert requires attention to the actual operation of a claimed safeguard. Arizona’s confidential proceedings and appeal, restricted evidentiary record and permissible fictitious name can serve that function with enforceable disclosure duties. The possibility of an illegal leak, without a demonstrated inadequacy in the lawful protections, does not justify enjoining necessary court processing. Administration nevertheless remains reviewable for its actual operation and burdens; the label alone supplies no constitutional exemption.

### II. Outside-agency access requires a sufficient legal connection to its purpose

**Holding and operative rule:** When government authorizes outside officials to inspect intimate bypass information, the permitted information, recipient audience and governmental function must have a sufficient connection, with safeguards against unnecessary use and redistribution. General eligibility to inspect other closed files or punishment for unauthorized leaks does not itself establish that connection; actual authorizing law and its limits must be determined.

**Authority:** Part II; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join.

**Controlling explanation:** Sheets protects intimate family information against unjustified official dissemination; Paul P. requires a sufficient connection among protected information, recipients and protective purpose. The Court applies those principles to prospective authorized access in a compulsory bypass system. Whalen preserves legitimate, safeguarded administration. The governing distinction is between necessary handling and an additional legally permitted transfer, not between reliable and dishonest employees.

Arizona excludes from the public employees of agencies authorized by state or federal law or rule to inspect and copy closed records. That clause does not necessarily give every employee every file, but neither does its text establish that every independent authorization is confined to a necessary bypass function. The general confidentiality command and unauthorized-disclosure penalties cannot replace examination of those permissions. The State’s legal justification must concern this information and this audience. Appropriate limitation to a genuine official task, redaction where identity is unnecessary and safeguards against unnecessary redistribution may satisfy the rule; their adequacy depends on the actual function. No universal agency list, audit schedule, judicial-preclearance system, least-restrictive-means command or perfect-containment guarantee is imposed. An authorized exposure can implicate this independent privacy protection without proof that a measurable number of minors abandoned abortions.

### III. Any demonstrated access burden receives the complete reproductive-liberty inquiry

**Holding and operative rule:** If authorized access causes demonstrated meaningful or severe additional interference with the protected decision or practical use of the bypass, Casey’s corresponding protections apply; absence of a substantial obstacle or completed public leak does not end the inquiry. Challengers must establish the actual duty, affected population, causation and triggering interference before the State bears the corresponding justification burden.

**Authority:** Part III; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join. Kennedy does not adopt its residual-interference rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Casey requires identification of the duty and protected baseline, actual function and purpose, added interference, ordered protection, clinical and communication safeguards, and fitted relief. A previability prohibition, effective third-party veto, denial of necessary timely care, proved obstructive purpose or substantial obstacle independently fails. Other severe interference requires compelling justification and narrow tailoring. Remaining meaningful interference requires substantiated permissible reasons and fit, including an answer to a concrete, practicable, substantially less burdensome alternative adequately serving the objective. Experience and reasonable prediction may suffice; a new study, numerical benefit calculation or cheapest-policy rule is not required. Below meaningful interference the heightened access inquiry ends while independent constitutional protection remains.

An assertion of fear alone does not establish the triggering burden, and the subject of abortion alone does not shift proof to the State. Conversely, confidentiality is not established simply by showing that no leak has yet occurred. The actual authorization and its effects must be identified. The independent information-transfer rule in Part II does not depend on first finding a substantial obstacle.

### IV. The legal authorizations must be resolved before the outside-access claims are rejected

**Holding and operative rule:** Rejection of the outside-agency claims is vacated because the governing authorizations and their recipient, purpose, court-control, use and redistribution limits were not determined. On remand the court must apply Parts II and III to those actual legal permissions, prevent established improper access, and preserve necessary confidential administration, lawful access and a usable bypass under applicable severability law.

**Authority:** Part IV; Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join its disposition and independent privacy/bypass ground. The five in that coalition other than Kennedy also join Part III.

**Controlling explanation:** The appellate assumption of need-based judicial control cannot resolve the distinct outside-agency exception without examining the laws and rules that confer access. Equally, the exception’s reference to independent legal authorization prevents treating it as an established unlimited license. The remand determines that legal scope and then matches protection to the operation proved. If existing material supplies the answer, no new hearing is required. The court must prevent access lacking a sufficient connection and safeguards, preserve necessary processing and a practical alternative to parental consent, and decide any surviving interim request under ordinary equity. Stone-Zsela would order an injunction now against access without function-specific justification; the majority first requires resolution of the actual legal permissions, rather than treating the still-unidentified permissions as conclusively unbounded.

## Precedent Treatment

- Bellotti v. Baird supplies the independent, confidential and expeditious bypass protection; it does not forbid necessary officials from knowing the proceeding.
- Ohio v. Akron Center for Reproductive Health permits reasonable anonymity safeguards without a guarantee against every unauthorized act.
- Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey supplies confidential bypass and reporting permissions and the complete access framework; confidential collection does not itself justify additional dissemination.
- Lambert v. Wicklund preserves attention to the actual protection furnished by a bypass, without approving every distinct disclosure condition.
- Sheets v. Salt Lake County supplies protection for intimate marital and family information against unjustified official dissemination, applied here to authorized prospective exposure of bypass records.
- Paul P. v. Verniero supplies the required connection among information, recipients and purpose and fitted safeguards, extended from its confined residential-information setting without adopting universal secrecy or a fixed balancing formula.
- Whalen v. Roe preserves legitimate safeguarded collection and administration, which the Court’s distinction between necessary handling and additional transfer retains.

## Law After Decision

Necessary confidential bypass administration remains permissible. Additional authorized government access to intimate bypass records must have a sufficient legal connection among the information, audience and function, with fitted protection against unnecessary dissemination. This independent informational-privacy inquiry and any proved Casey access burden remain distinct. The actual outside permissions and their safeguards must be applied on remand; their legality is not settled by either a general confidentiality label or a hypothetical leak.

## Separate Writings

**Kennedy, concurring in part.** He joins Parts I, II and IV under the confidentiality principles of Ohio v. Akron and Casey and the information-recipient-purpose protection he adopted in Sheets and Paul P. An express legal outside-access exception presents a different question from speculative unauthorized misconduct. Actual permissions and safeguards require examination before the provision is sustained. He withholds Part III’s residual lesser-burden rule, while fully joining the independent privacy ground and partial vacatur.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He joins Parts I–III but would reverse approval of outside access insofar as the State has supplied no function-specific legal justification, and remand for an injunction against that access. Eligibility to inspect closed files is not a reason to obtain this minor’s identity and intimate account; penalties for unlawful leaks do not cure an unnecessarily broad lawful transfer. A genuine preexisting legal restriction may narrow the decree, but an expectation of responsible administration is insufficient. The remedy should preserve necessary internal handling and practical bypass access while preventing exposure lacking bounded authority. His position does not require a finding that the exception substantially obstructs abortions.

**Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Thomas.** They accept the permission for necessary confidential internal handling but would affirm rejection of both claims. Their Sheets and Paul P. positions reject the asserted broader substantive informational-privacy right. The statutory confidentiality command, fictitious-name option, separate authorization requirement and penalties make speculative misuse insufficient under their rule. They do not conclude that every future agency request is lawful or that a proved improper disclosure defeats every independently grounded claim. Their disagreement with the partial vacatur is therefore different from Stone-Zsela’s demand for more immediate protection.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for determination of the operative outside-access laws or rules and their limits, followed by application of the constitutional standards and any necessary fitted relief. The internal-handling judgment remains affirmed. Necessary court processing and usable bypass access must be preserved. The lower court must decide surviving interim-protection requests under ordinary equitable standards.

## Source Notes

307 F.3d 783; 189 F. Supp. 2d 975; district action No. CV-00-0386-RCC. Arizona Revised Statutes §§36-2152(D), 36-2152(F) and 38-504(B); Arizona Rule 123(b)(11). The excluded alleged breach is not a finding of completed disclosure. The operative outside-access permissions and their legal limits remain to be determined on remand.


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Benson-v-Terhune_merits_2003-05-27.md
Effective date: 2003-05-27


## Event

Benson v. Terhune; judgment below, 304 F.3d 874, Ninth Circuit No. 01-16833. Merits decision, May 27, 2003, on full merits submission reviewing the September 11, 2002 affirmance of denial of federal habeas relief. The question concerns medication, informed consent and fair-trial rights, subject to §2254, where the State found voluntary acceptance, capacity to refuse and no established impairment of trial participation. Benson seeks a new trial on her 1988 second-degree murder conviction and seventeen-years-to-life sentence. A ten-day state hearing occurred in 1997; later state denials culminated on January 25, 2000. Her federal application was filed April 19, 2000 and denied July 26, 2001.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated.

## Public Action

The judgment denying the requested habeas relief is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The requested new-trial writ is denied on the statutory and supported-record grounds stated below. The conviction and sentence remain undisturbed by this review.

## Opinion Topology

Breyer delivers the opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. All nine join the complete opinion. Stone-Zsela files a concurrence, joined by no other Justice, proposing a distinct affirmative-information rule while joining the habeas disposition.

## Holdings

### 1. Habeas review requires the eligible constitutional rule and the actual state adjudication

**Holding and operative rule:** For this application governed by AEDPA, §2254(d)(1) requires separate examination of whether the state merits decision contradicts an eligible Supreme Court holding or applies that holding objectively unreasonably. General governing holdings count, factual differences alone do not defeat the contrary-to inquiry, and later holdings or circuit decisions cannot supply the constitutional rule clearly established at the state merits date.

**Authority:** Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine join this rule and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** Williams gives the two statutory clauses independent work; Early and Woodford require examination of the actual legal rule and whole supported record, rather than case citations, factual identity or an isolated formula. The application filed April 19, 2000 is governed by AEDPA although direct review ended in 1989. The relevant constitutional holdings are those eligible when the State decided the claim, following the 1997 hearing and the later unexplained denials ending in January 2000. Harper and Riggins were then available. Weston's 2001 advance-review addition cannot supply an earlier constitutional command. White v. Napoleon may offer persuasive treatment-information reasoning but is not a Supreme Court holding.

State factual determinations receive the separate treatment prescribed by §2254(d)(2) and (e)(1); the clear-and-convincing factual rebuttal burden is not imported into legal unreasonableness. The developed record supplies no established basis for displacing the material findings. Direct-review finality has a different role under Teague. Horn requires a preserved applicable nonretroactivity defense to be resolved before granting relief, not before this independent sufficient denial; the Court makes no preservation or waiver finding.

### 2. This medication record does not establish entitlement to a new-trial writ

**Holding and operative rule:** The supported findings of repeated coherent treatment requests, effective refusals, retained ability to seek information and no established impairment of trial participation permit denial of this writ under the eligible holdings. Incomplete information, medical decision-making capacity, voluntary ingestion and trial injury remain distinct; neither silence nor competence alone establishes informed consent, and no incompetency finding is a prerequisite to the trial protection Riggins supplies.

**Authority:** All nine directly join this statutory and actual-record application in the same Court opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** Harper protects the bodily-liberty interest, and Riggins requires sufficient justification for medication continued over objection. Riggins also requires a new trial when unjustified compelled treatment creates a supported, substantial defense risk that cannot reliably be reconstructed; the prisoner need not prove exactly how the unmedicated trial would differ. Those holdings do not clearly require invalidating this conviction because a requesting detainee lacked full information about the regimen.

The state judge found that Benson was not fully informed about the drugs, dosages and effects. Her nursing experience does not erase that finding. The judge nevertheless credited numerous coherent requests, honored refusals, retained ability to ask and object, and testimony consistent with participation. Conflicting impairment evidence was heard and resolved against the claim. The safety-cell remark concerned behavior during a demand for more medication, not a threat compelling ingestion; permitted alternatives were not pursued under the offered arrangements.

Benson's strongest argument is that an undisclosed psychotropic intervention can undermine both meaningful refusal and defense participation without causing incompetence. The Court takes that distinction seriously. But its plausibility does not establish an eligible constitutional command requiring this new trial, nor does the presence of a psychotropic drug alone prove Riggins's trial-risk predicate. The denial rests on the actual statutory and factual showing, not a universal no-duty-to-inform rule, automatic harmlessness, or a competence-as-consent rule. The unmaintained sentencing-medication claim and damages or ongoing-treatment relief are outside the granted custody question.

## Precedent Treatment

- Williams v. Taylor: applies the independent statutory clauses, Supreme Court-source and state-merits-date requirements and distinct factual treatment; no extra burden is imposed on legal unreasonableness.
- Early v. Packer; Woodford v. Visciotti: apply whole-opinion and whole-supported-record examination; citations and correct formulas alone decide nothing.
- Washington v. Harper: preserves the protected refusal interest and justified treatment in its distinct confinement setting.
- Riggins v. Nevada: distinguishes the actual requested-regimen findings while preserving refusal protection and the supported, substantial, unreconstructable trial-risk remedy without an incompetency prerequisite.
- United States v. Weston: preserves its current safety and advance-effects rules; its 2001 addition cannot supply the constitutional rule governing the earlier state adjudication.
- Lindh v. Murphy; Carriger v. Stewart: apply provision-specific AEDPA temporal rules to the April 2000 application; an older conviction does not make this application pre-enactment.
- Teague v. Lane; Horn v. Banks: preserve the distinct direct-review-finality and preserved-defense inquiries; an independent sufficient denial requires no new retroactivity holding.
- White v. Napoleon: supplies persuasive circuit reasoning only and cannot replace §2254(d)(1)'s Supreme Court source.

## Law After Decision

The existing habeas framework and medication protections are applied without change. This supported record establishes no entitlement to a new-trial writ under the eligible law. Capacity, treatment choice and trial effects remain distinct; the decision creates no general immunity for inadequate medical information and no new affirmative-information rule. Stone-Zsela's proposed development remains noncontrolling.

## Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the denial under the eligible law and supported findings but would develop Harper and Riggins to protect meaningful treatment information. A request for pain relief does not necessarily authorize every undisclosed mind-altering drug selected in custody. A refusal right can be ineffective if its holder must discover a material risk before the custodian must explain it; capacity to understand is not proof that understanding was supplied.

His proposed rule would require a reasonable explanation of a psychotropic regimen's identity, therapeutic purpose, material risks to thought and participation, and feasible alternatives sufficient for the actual patient to accept or refuse. A material change of regimen or dosage requires an explanation of that change. The State could establish that the patient already understood the relevant matters, but nursing training or drug experience alone would not categorically substitute. The rule would require neither exhaustive warnings about remote effects, a criminal-waiver colloquy nor the patient's preferred treatment. Genuine emergencies and independently justified treatment over refusal remain distinct.

He identifies that information rule as a proposed development, not earlier clearly established law. Benson's substantial hearing and the supported findings do not establish forced ingestion or the asserted trial disruption. Riggins protects more than minimal competence, but its supported unreconstructable risk cannot be presumed from medication alone. The safety-cell remark and unused alternatives must retain their found character. White's reasoning does not meet the statutory source requirement, and this independent denial needs no unbriefed Teague ruling or automatic structural-error theory. The concurrence supplies no holding on a properly brought claim of deliberate concealment, ongoing unwanted treatment or materially different defense impairment. No other Justice joins the proposed development.

## Procedure After Action

The denial of this habeas claim stands. Supreme Court review of the granted medication and fair-trial custody question is complete; the judgment supplies no new-trial or further-hearing direction.

## Source Notes

Benson v. Terhune, 304 F.3d 874; 157 F. Supp. 2d 1093; 28 U.S.C. §2254. The state judge found incomplete treatment information while separately finding voluntary ingestion, retained capacity and no established trial impairment. The January 2000 denial was unexplained. Direct review ended in 1989, a date distinct from the state merits adjudication relevant to §2254(d)(1).


## Source: terms/OT2002/records/Roe-v-Texas-Department-of-Protective-and-Regulatory-Services_merits_2003-06-02.md
Effective date: 2003-06-02


## Event

Roe v. Texas Department of Protective and Regulatory Services. October Term 2002. Merits decision June 2, 2003, reviewing the Fifth Circuit's July 17, 2002 interlocutory immunity judgment, 299 F.3d 395, No. 01-50711. The questions concern a child-protection worker's nonurgent intimate examination and photography, conduct-date fair warning, the mother's scheduled-interview entry claim and overlapping substantive-due-process theories. The search occurred July 10, 1999. The Court accepts the supported plaintiff-favorable account for the legal appeal. The worker, Beverly Strickland, claimed neither probable cause nor exigency; consent to the intimate search remains disputed. The child's representatives seek federal damages relief; Strickland seeks immunity. The state-law claims had been remanded, and other defendants' dismissals were not appealed.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer participated at submission and decision. All nine participate.

## Public Action

Child-search immunity judgment reversed, 7–2; entry and duplicative-due-process dispositions affirmed, 9–0; remanded with state claims treated independently.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and count | Supporting | Opposing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Child's Fourth Amendment damages claim: personal immunity | Reverse, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas |
| Mother's scheduled-interview home-entry claim | Affirm rejection, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None |
| Duplicative substantive-due-process claims | Affirm rejection, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None |
| Independent Texas immunity inquiry | Preserve separate state-law consideration, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None |
| Consequential remand with a surviving child-search federal claim | Remand in restored-claim posture, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas favor remand after federal dismissal |

The child's Fourth Amendment claim returns for ordinary further proceedings under Parts II, III and V. The unappealed dismissals remain intact. The differing child-search merits reach is stated in the opinion topology and is not a separate damages judgment.

## Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivered the opinion of the Court, in which Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer joined, and in which Scalia and Thomas joined as to Parts I, IV and V-A. Scalia filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, in which Thomas joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; Scalia and Thomas as to I, IV, V-A | Affirms entry and due-process dispositions; reverses child immunity; directs remand | Seven join all parts; all nine join I, IV and V-A |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm all reviewed federal dismissals and preserve independent state-law return | Joins I, IV and V-A; does not reach II; dissents from III and consequential V-B |

## Holdings

### I. The agreed interview authorized entry within its own scope

**Holding and operative rule:** An objectively understood, voluntarily arranged home interview permits the identified worker to enter for that interview when she arrives as agreed, absent withdrawal or an expressed limitation. The absence of a renewed express doorstep invitation does not revoke that antecedent permission, but the interview agreement does not authorize intimate bodily examination or photography.

**Authority:** Part I of the opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this rule and explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court accepts the mother's account that she did not expressly invite Strickland inside again at the doorway. Her acknowledged earlier agreement to an appointment at home, after learning the worker's identity and welfare purpose, nevertheless supplies affirmative authorization for the interview. The result rests on that agreement and its objective scope, not on silence as consent. Schneckloth v. Bustamonte supplies voluntary consent, and Ohio v. Robinette requires attention to the actual circumstances rather than an indispensable verbal formula. Johnson v. Jones and Behrens v. Pelletier permit assessment of the legal effect of the accepted account, not a credibility choice in the worker's favor. The mother's separate entry claim therefore fails, while the child's distinct search claim requires its own justification.

**Precedent treatment:** Schneckloth and Robinette are applied to voluntary, scope-limited authorization; Johnson and Behrens preserve the assumed-fact boundary of interlocutory legal review.

### II. This nonurgent intimate examination required consent or judicial authorization

**Holding and operative rule:** A nonemergency investigative visual examination and photography of a child's genital and anal areas in these circumstances requires voluntary consent or appropriate judicial authorization supported by probable cause; genuine exigency is an independent exception. The claimant must establish the governmental search, and the official must substantiate consent or another invoked exception; cooperation with official directions does not conclusively establish voluntary consent.

**Authority:** Part II of the opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Scalia and Thomas do not reach the constitutional merits; they neither join this rule nor vote that the search was constitutional. Seven direct joins control.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court examines the actual operation, as Ferguson v. City of Charleston requires, and the demonstrated need, as Chandler v. Miller requires. A protective purpose is substantial, but the challenged operation was intimate exposure, manipulation and photography by a worker without training in that photography, rather than an ordinary interview or superficial observation. The ten-day response classification, absence of claimed urgency or probable cause, and acknowledged medical-examination route supply no necessity for immediate intrusion without ordinary authorization. Griffin and Vernonia allow justified protective arrangements; they do not make a general protective mission sufficient for every investigative method. Statutory reporting duties or possible police contact do not categorically defeat special needs, and Ferguson's coordinated prosecution program is not a fact of this case. Good v. Dauphin County Social Services applies the Fourth Amendment in child-protection investigation; Darryl H. v. Coler likewise retains limits on scope and necessity under its different approach. Consent remains a distinct question. Under Schneckloth and Robinette, the mother's assistance in removing clothing and positioning the child, in response to official direction, must be considered with her questions about necessity and counsel, distress and lack of advance explanation of the eventual scope. These facts permit a finding of submission rather than permission; the Court requires no particular warning or verbal formula and assumes no threat to remove the child. On the supported plaintiff-favorable account of no voluntary consent, this search lacked the required justification. Actual consent remains for ordinary proceedings.

**Precedent treatment:** Ferguson and Chandler are applied to current merits, with their actual-operation and demonstrated-need limits. Griffin and Vernonia retain their justified protective settings; Good supplies child-investigation protection; Darryl H. is distinguished in scope and safeguards; Schneckloth and Robinette require actual voluntary consent.

### III. Pre-July 10, 1999 law supplied fair warning on the accepted account

**Holding and operative rule:** Qualified immunity does not protect the assumed unconsented, highly intimate and nonurgent search because preconduct law made its lack of justification apparent under either plausible search framework. The legal inquiry uses supported plaintiff-favorable facts and law effective before July 10, 1999; materially different established facts remain subject to their own defenses, and later decisions supply no earlier notice.

**Authority:** Part III of the opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Scalia and Thomas dissent from reversal of immunity. Seven direct joins control; no fractured-rationale synthesis is used.

**Controlling explanation:** Harlow v. Fitzgerald and Anderson v. Creighton require objective, particularized conduct-date notice. Wilson v. Layne distinguishes violation from personal immunity without demanding a factual twin; Elder v. Holloway requires all relevant preconduct law. Hope v. Pelzer explains how obvious application can give warning, but is not itself July 1999 notice. Before the search, Camara and New Jersey v. T.L.O. subjected civil officials and children's searches to the Fourth Amendment. Bell v. Wolfish required examination of scope, manner, justification and place. Vernonia and Chandler required real contextual justification, while Schneckloth and Robinette required voluntary consent rather than submission. Good specifically rejected a general child-protection exemption. The contrary authorities do not disappear: Darryl H. rejected a categorical warrant requirement and granted immunity despite substantial necessity and consent doubts. Yet it retained reasonableness limits, declined to treat maternal assistance as conclusive consent and identified medical examination for suspected sexual abuse. Landstrom involved a nurse's examination of complained-of soreness and a March 1987 notice date; Wildauer concerned an investigative home visit. Doe v. Louisiana granted Fourteenth Amendment immunity and expressly reserved the Fourth Amendment question. That reservation warrants care but does not affirmatively authorize this distinct nonmedical photography beyond the shared scope, necessity and consent limits. The Court's conclusion therefore does not depend on a settled warrant rule for every protective examination. It rests on the particular intrusion's obvious excess under either approach. Scalia and Thomas regard the immunity outcomes and unresolved local question as dispositive uncertainty; the Court instead finds the shared limits sufficiently concrete for the accepted lack of consent, probable cause and urgency. Ferguson, Wallis, Calabretta, Tenenbaum, Saucier and Hope create no July 1999 warning. Neither actual reading of precedent nor proof of actual reliance is required. No compulsory merits-first sequence is imposed.

**Precedent treatment:** Harlow, Anderson, Wilson and Elder retain objective, context-specific notice. Hope supplies the present method only. The listed preconduct scope and consent rules provide the warning; contrary child-protection cases are considered in their actual settings, not treated as a universal exemption.

### IV. The same entry and search do not create duplicate substantive-due-process claims

**Holding and operative rule:** The mother's home-entry claim and the child's bodily-search claim are governed by the specific Fourth Amendment protection applicable to those acts, not a duplicate substantive-due-process damages theory. Failure to recover on a specific claim, including because of personal immunity, does not supply a separate substantive-due-process remedy for the same intrusion.

**Authority:** Part IV of the opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this rule and explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** Graham v. Connor and the specific-provision rule preserved in County of Sacramento v. Lewis direct the identified intrusions to their express constitutional source. The record contains neither removal of the child nor a separate coercive displacement of a parental decision beyond the challenged examination. The mother's emotional injury does not itself establish that additional governmental act. This conclusion does not erase a parent's distinct family-liberty interest when a different supported act infringes it. It rejects these duplicative theories concerning the entry and examination, whose actual scope and justification are addressed by the Fourth Amendment.

**Precedent treatment:** Graham and Lewis are applied to the particular acts and claimants; distinct family-liberty claims based on different governmental conduct are not extinguished.

### V. The restored federal claim and state-law claims return in their actual posture

**Holding and operative rule:** Texas official immunity must be assessed under its own law, independently of federal qualified immunity. With the child's federal search claim restored, any decision about supplemental jurisdiction must take account of that surviving claim; the unappealed dismissals of other defendants remain intact.

**Authority:** Part V-A, the independence of Texas immunity, is joined by all nine. Part V-B, the restored-federal-claim remit and its supplemental-jurisdiction consequence, is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer; Scalia and Thomas would instead leave the federal dismissal in place and preserve the independent state-law return.

**Controlling explanation:** The federal notice determination does not decide a Texas defense. The Fifth Circuit's state-law return must be conformed to the federal claim's restored posture, rather than implemented on an assumption that all federal claims have ended. Ordinary proceedings determine actual consent, personal responsibility, causation, injury, liability and remedy. Strickland must substantiate an invoked consent justification on the actual circumstances; the child must prove the actionable search and resulting compensable injury by a preponderance. If the proved facts materially differ from those assumed for the legal appeal, the court applies the governing defenses to those facts. A lawful discretionary refusal of supplemental jurisdiction remains governed by its actual predicates, and a jurisdictional dismissal is without prejudice. The department and other officials whose dismissals were not appealed are not restored by this mandate.

**Precedent treatment:** Johnson and Behrens preserve the difference between legal assumed-fact review and actual liability; the existing objective federal defense does not decide Texas official immunity.

## Precedent Treatment

- Schneckloth v. Bustamonte, 412 U.S. 218, and Ohio v. Robinette (November 18, 1996): applied to voluntary, circumstance-sensitive consent without a mandatory verbal formula.
- Johnson v. Jones (June 12, 1995), and Behrens v. Pelletier (February 21, 1996): applied; interlocutory legal review uses supported assumed facts and cannot decide credibility or evidentiary sufficiency.
- Ferguson v. City of Charleston (March 21, 2001): applied to current merits; actual operation requires its own justification, without a categorical mixed-purpose bar or retrospective notice.
- Chandler v. Miller (April 15, 1997): applied; a demonstrated need must justify the intrusion.
- Griffin v. Wisconsin, 483 U.S. 868, and Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton (June 26, 1995): their actual protective arrangements are distinguished from this unsubstantiated intimate method; the search and no-waiver principles remain.
- Camara v. Municipal Court, 387 U.S. 523; New Jersey v. T.L.O., 469 U.S. 325; Bell v. Wolfish, 441 U.S. 520: their preconduct civil-search, child-search and scope/manner/justification rules inform the particular warning.
- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800, and Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635: applied; objective, particularized legal notice remains the defense.
- Elder v. Holloway (February 23, 1994), and Wilson v. Layne (May 24, 1999): applied; all relevant preconduct law and the violation/immunity distinction remain material without a factual-twin requirement.
- Hope v. Pelzer (June 27, 2002): applies the obvious-application method with attention to contrary authority and hierarchy; it does not create 1999 notice.
- Saucier v. Katz (June 18, 2001): preserves separate objective immunity analysis; it creates neither compulsory merits-first sequencing nor earlier notice.
- Graham v. Connor, 490 U.S. 386, and County of Sacramento v. Lewis (May 26, 1998): applied to these acts under the specific Fourth Amendment source, without eliminating independent family-liberty claims.
- Wallis v. Spencer (April 2, 2001): its independent-examination reservation supplies no resolution or conduct-date warning here.

## Law After Decision

The bodily-search holding requires voluntary consent, probable cause with appropriate judicial authorization, or genuine exigency for the nonurgent intimate investigative operation addressed here. It does not impose one procedure on every protective inquiry or make police reporting alone decisive. The scheduled interview authorizes only its objective scope. On the accepted no-consent, no-probable-cause and no-urgency account, pre-July 10, 1999 law gave sufficient warning for the child-search claim to proceed. Actual liability remains subject to the proved facts and ordinary defenses. The same intrusions do not support duplicate substantive-due-process damages theories. Texas official immunity remains independent, and jurisdictional treatment must reflect the restored federal claim.

## Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in Parts I, IV and V-A and dissents from the reversal of child-search immunity and the consequential federal-claim remit. They affirm the interview-entry and duplicative-due-process rulings and preserve the independence of Texas immunity. They would affirm dismissal of all reviewed federal claims and return the state-law claims for evaluation of Texas immunity or a lawful decision declining supplemental jurisdiction, with a jurisdictional dismissal without prejudice. They do not decide the child's Fourth Amendment merits. In their view Anderson's particularized-notice requirement is not satisfied by the general gravity of this intrusion. Darryl H. rejected a categorical warrant-and-probable-cause requirement and granted immunity despite serious questions about necessity and consent; Landstrom likewise found insufficient clarity for an intimate examination. Doe v. Louisiana's local Fourth Amendment reservation left the application unsettled. Nonmedical photography is a material distinction, but the preconduct authorities had not made its personal-damages consequence sufficiently definite by July 10, 1999. A factual twin is unnecessary, all relevant other-circuit authority remains relevant, and child-protection workers have no blanket immunity. Nevertheless, the present Court's stronger merits interpretation cannot cure the earlier uncertainty. Ferguson, Wallis, Calabretta, Tenenbaum, Saucier and Hope cannot supply retrospective notice. Their position finds neither voluntary consent nor constitutional approval of the search.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns with the child-search federal claim restored and the entry and duplicative-due-process rejections intact. The courts proceed under the consent, responsibility, proof and remedy directions in Part V and assess the preserved state claims under their own law in that posture. The mandate does not revive unappealed defendant dismissals.

## Source Notes

Roe v. Texas Department of Protective and Regulatory Services, 299 F.3d 395 (5th Cir. 2002), No. 01-50711. Good v. Dauphin County Social Services, 891 F.2d 1087 (3d Cir. 1989); Darryl H. v. Coler, 801 F.2d 893 (7th Cir. 1986); Landstrom v. Illinois Department of Children & Family Services, 892 F.2d 670 (7th Cir. 1990); Wildauer v. Frederick County, 993 F.2d 369 (4th Cir. 1993); Doe v. Louisiana, 2 F.3d 1412 (5th Cir. 1993). Actual consent, responsibility and injury remain factual issues; the interlocutory review does not resolve them by credibility choice.
